- **The hand-built `Visualize.Axis` keeps d3's defaults.** Ten ticks and the default label stay, so an axis built by hand is still d3-axis and its golden is unchanged; it gets the derived ones by calling the two functions (spec/05 §1.5).
- **Derived on every realization.** A fixed scale derives the same count and format each time, so its axis stays a static region; a moving scale's axis re-derives per tick, and a resize re-derives the count at the new size.

**Consequences.** Every axis in a design without a format or a count changes: time axes read `Mar 05` or `12:00` instead of ISO timestamps, axes over thousands are grouped and over millions carry one SI prefix, and an axis shorter than 800 px across (350 px down) carries fewer ticks than ten — the gallery's goldens move accordingly. A numeric axis over years (`1990..2020`) is grouped (`1,990`), as d3's default is; such a design says `format: "d"`. metresis can drop `:width` from binding and its own time and number formats, which a follow-up proposal there does once this ships. A design that relied on ten ticks on a small axis states `ticks: 10`.

## 135. D-135 — A theme carries meaning as four ramps and five roles, whose hue order and lightness the library fixes and checks when the theme is built

**Context.** #533, from #531 §3 and its review item E. Color carries meaning a reader brings with them — the stop-light, cold blue and hot red, below and above a target — but a theme offered only categorical `:series_n` slots and chrome, and the sequential and diverging colors of a scale were the background-to-first-series run or a fixed d3 scheme. A palette position promises *distinct*, never *red-ish*: a status chart written with series slots — the gallery's Gauge colored its ok, warm and hot zones `:series_4`, `:series_2`, `:series_3` — draws success, warning and failure as orange, gray and light blue under some host's theme, and nothing notices. The direction for boards and feeds (#531) needs a vocabulary a feed's schema can name without naming a color — `status: %{"ok" => :good}` — so that color is chosen only by the theme or by a deliberate choice in the design. Building it also showed `Visualize.Color.to_lab/1` was wrong: it converted sRGB through the D65 matrix but divided by the D50 white point that `lab/4` (d3-color's constants) uses, so the two were not inverses — `#808080` read back as `#758194`, and every gray had a chroma of about 12, which no contract on "neutral" could have been written against.

**Decision.**
- **Four ramps, each with a meaning the library fixes:** `status` (good → caution → bad), `thermal` (cold → neutral → hot), `diverging` (below → at → above a midpoint) and `sequential` (less → more). A ramp is a list of anchor colors evenly spaced along `0..1`, interpolated in CIE Lab; a position is `{:ramp, name, t}`, continuous, so any number of ordered levels fits. A theme holds them in a `ramps` map of anchor lists — plain data, so the theme-as-a-stored-value work (#534) has nothing to translate.
- **Five roles are the meaning vocabulary:** `:good`, `:warning`, `:bad` are the `status` ramp at 0, 0.5 and 1 — fixed points, not colors of their own, so a role and its position cannot disagree — and `:info` and `:neutral` are the theme's `roles`. *(Amended by #663, below.)* They are theme slots: `var(--vis-good, …)` in CSS, declared by `stylesheet/1`. A ramp position resolves to a literal in both modes, as the contrast ink does, since CSS has no property per position — except the status roles' own positions, which resolve as the roles, so the two spellings of one meaning are one color even under a stylesheet.
- **Contracts are checked when a theme is constructed** — `new/1`, `merge/2`, an inline design theme — and a breach raises `ArgumentError` naming the theme, the ramp or role and the term broken, with the measured values; the validator reports the same message as `{:contract, message}`. The terms, in CIE LCh with honest tolerances: hue regions as arcs (red < 45° or ≥ 345°, amber 45–100°, green 100–200°, blue 200–315°, each placed by measuring pure sRGB primaries); chromatic at ≥ 20 C*, neutral at ≤ 10 C*; lightness monotonic, either way, at ≥ 3 L* a step between consecutive anchors (about three just-noticeable differences — visible on a screen, not only on a meter; and linear Lab interpolation makes the anchors sufficient); one hue family within 45° for anchors of ≥ 10 C*. `status` is green-or-blue → amber → red with its hue falling across every chromatic anchor and its lightness monotonic; `thermal` is blue → neutral → red; `diverging` has chromatic ends at least 60° apart about a neutral middle; `sequential` is one hue family with monotonic lightness. `:info` is a chromatic blue and `:neutral` is neutral.
- **Lightness is monotonic either way; the built-ins run it away from their background.** #533 wrote the sequential contract as "light → dark", which on a dark page would make the smallest value the most salient: the dark theme's `sequential` runs dark → light, and its `status` rises from good to bad, so on every built-in theme the low end recedes and the role that must be noticed, `:bad`, stands furthest from the page. That direction is held by the built-ins' tests and recommended to hosts, not made a contract: tying it to `background` would make `new(background: "#000")`, which keeps the default's light-page ramps, refuse to build, and a contract that fails on a one-field change is a trap, not a guarantee. What the contract fixes is that lightness carries the order, which is what a reader who sees no hue relies on.
- **The color-vision-safe variant is a whole theme,** `color_safe/0` (`theme: :color_safe`): the default with `status` as blue → orange → red, still monotonic in lightness by 6 L* or more a step so that order survives with no hue at all. Its series stay the default's: no ten-color palette is both color-vision-safe and 3:1 on white, and the series promise only distinctness.
- **Fallback is the default theme's ramp or role, never a series color.** `ramps` and `roles` merge key by key in `new/1` and `merge/2`, and a struct that still lacks a key takes the default's at resolution.
- **Scales range over ramps.** `range: {:ramp, name}` on an ordinal color scale spreads its domain end to end (category i of n at `i / (n − 1)`), on a threshold scale its `k + 1` buckets the same way, and on a sequential or diverging scale interpolates the ramp; a quantize or quantile scale, whose range length is its bucket count, refuses one. The `:auto` range of a `:sequential` scale is the theme's `sequential` ramp and of a `:diverging` one its `diverging` ramp, where they ran from the background to the first series color.
- **Precedence:** a design's literal or its color scale, then the theme's rendering of a role or ramp position, then the theme's default. A feed's schema (#531) will name meanings and sit between the last two.
- **`to_lab/1` uses the D50-adapted matrix** of d3-color, the inverse of `lab/4`'s, and treats an exact gray as neutral, so Lab and LCh read true and `lab(to_lab(c))` is `c`.

**Consequences.** A design says what a color means and every theme draws it in its own tones with the meaning held: the Gauge's zones are `[:good, :warning, :bad]` and are green, amber and red on the light theme, brighter on the dark, blue, orange and red on the color-safe one. A host theme that reorders a ramp, or flattens its lightness, fails when it is built rather than drawing a misleading chart. The four ramps and the contracts are the library's vocabulary: a host cannot add a ramp or a role yet, and a request for one is a proposal. Physical anchors (a temperature's freezing point placed on `thermal`) are #531's next step and not in this one; a ramp here is positioned by `t` alone. Every `:sequential` and `:diverging` color scale left at `:auto` changes color: the gallery's heatmap, contour, hexbin and radial bar charts now draw along the theme's blue sequential ramp, Lab-interpolated, instead of white → `#3b6fa8` in RGB. Lab and LCh values from `Visualize.Color` change for every chromatic color, as do `brighter/2`, `darker/2`, `interpolate_lab/3` and `interpolate_hcl/3`; nothing in the library drew through them before this decision.

**Amended by #663** (#650, D-173). **Role colors are names by use in the theme's color table** (spec/08 §7.1). The theme's `roles` field is gone: `:info` and `:neutral` are entries of `colors`, and `:good`, `:warning` and `:bad` are the entries `{:ramp, :status, 0}`, `0.5` and `1`, so by default a role and its position still cannot disagree, and setting `status` still sets them. A theme may now set a status role's entry itself, where #533 refused it, because what a meaning *looks like* is its role style's to say (D-171, D-173). The refusal pointed a host to the one place a meaning could be changed, and that place is no longer the color. `roles:` given to `new/1` or `merge/2`, and a `roles` key in an inline or stored theme, is read as `colors`. The `:info` and `:neutral` contracts hold on those entries, under the subject `{:color, name}`.

## 136. D-136 — A feed describes its fields by meaning in a schema identified by a content hash; a delivery names the version and never carries the schema; a design states a contract the schema must meet

**Context.** #535, from the research of #531 (review A–C and the revision that sources carry meaning, never color). A source gave rows only, and a design declared its slot's `fields` and `types` (spec/14 §2.3). Everything else a chart says about the numbers — their unit, a series' name, the order of a set of states and what each means, the gap beyond which a line breaks — was computed by each host before binding (metresis's `MetresisWeb.Charts.Design` builds them into variables from its catalog), so every host would repeat that step and two hosts would drift. Sending a description with every data point is out of the question at a feed's rates. Five things had to be decided: what a description holds and what it must not; how a description is identified so that a delivery can name it cheaply and every node agrees on the name; where the descriptions are held, before any board process exists to own them; what a delivery is; and how a design says it needs a description and reads one.

**Decision.**
- **A feed has a schema; a delivery names its version.** `Visualize.Data.Schema` maps a field to a description of what it means: `type`, `unit`, `quantity`, `label`, `order`, `status`, `polarity`, `target` and `continuity` (spec/08 §10.1). A delivery carries the schema's version and never the schema.
- **Meaning, never color.** A description has no color key: `color`, `colour`, `colors`, `colours`, `palette`, `fill` and `stroke` are faults that say so. A state's meaning is a role atom under `status` (#533's names, carried and not checked here), which a theme turns into color. A literal color is the design's deliberate choice, never the data's. <!-- spelling: allow -->
- **Seams, not guesses, for what other proposals own.** `unit` is any non-empty string behind one private check that #536 replaces with UCUM validation; `quantity` is any atom until #536 fixes the list; `status`'s roles are any atom until #533 defines them.
- **The version is a content hash of our own canonical encoding.** It is SHA-256 over a tagged, length-prefixed encoding whose first line names the encoding (`visualize.schema/1`). Field names are encoded as strings, since the two spellings are one column (spec/08 §6.2). A map's entries are sorted by their keys' encoding, and floats are written in their shortest round-trip form. `:erlang.term_to_binary/2` is not used: its format is not promised across OTP releases, and `:deterministic` holds only within one. A test pins one version as a literal string (spec/08 §10.2). `:crypto` joins `extra_applications` for the hash.
- **The cache is a value, not a process.** `Visualize.Data.Schema.Cache` maps `{feed, version}` to a schema. `resolve/4` answers a cached version without calling anything. For an unknown version it calls a given `describe` function once and returns the grown cache. Its owner holds it: the board process that #531 plans, and until then the caller of `apply/2`, which takes it as `schemas:` with `describe:` and returns it on the applied chart. A global ETS table would need an owner process, a name and a lifetime this library does not have. It would also make `apply/2` impure, which D-65 and D-132 kept it from being.
- **A delivery is a struct and one more source shape.** `%Visualize.Data.Delivery{rows, window, resolution, capped, reduced_by, schema}` is read by `Visualize.Data.Table.rows/1` as the source it carries. `apply/2` binds it as that source, and a compiled chart takes one per slot.
- **A design states a contract; the schema supplies the facts; `apply/2` checks one against the other** (spec/14 §7.4). A slot writes `requires: %{field => [attribute, …]}`, which names attributes rather than giving values. Its keys are the slot's fields and its values `Visualize.Data.Schema.attributes/0`, a map of lists because a design's JSON form has no tuples (§11). A design reads a fact through `Visualize.Chart.fact(source, field, key)`, a `%Visualize.Chart.Fact{}` allowed wherever a value can appear and as a text hole `#{fact(:source, :field, :key)}`. It is resolved at application from the bound schema, as a variable is from `vars:`. An axis title is then the plotted field's `label` or `unit`, and an ordinal domain its `order`. **Reading a fact is a requirement too**, so a design cannot read an attribute it did not, in effect, require. A slot whose schema lacks one is `{:missing_attribute, slot, field, key}` at the path of the requirement or of the use, which names all three. A declared type the schema contradicts is `{:schema_type, field, declared, described}`. `Visualize.Chart.contract/1` lists a design's requirements so that a picker can test a feed before offering it.
- **A schema change recompiles,** as a resize does (spec/14 §12.1, §12.5). An applied and a compiled chart record the version each slot's delivery named. `Visualize.Chart.Compiled.stale?/2` tells the caller that a delivery names another version. `tick/3`, `step/3`, `render/2` and `svg/2` refuse such a delivery with an `ArgumentError` rather than draw facts that no longer hold, such as a label, a domain or an ordered state. The compiled chart cannot recompile itself because the design and the cache are the caller's. The caller therefore recompiles and `carry/2`s the easing across, as for a resize.

**Consequences.** A host stops computing facts into variables: a metresis feed describes its columns from the catalog once per catalog change, and every host draws the same labels and orders from the same design. A design that needs a unit says so, and a feed without one fails at `apply/2` with the slot, the field and the attribute named, never by drawing a blank label. Delivering a cached version costs a map lookup per slot. Variables return to being genuine choices. Two hosts computing a schema's version independently agree, so a version can cross a wire. The version also changes whenever the content does, labels included. A relabeled field therefore costs one recompilation, which is the price of never drawing a stale label. A caller that ignores `stale?/2` is told at its next tick. The schema's checks of units, quantities and roles are deliberately loose until #536 and #533 land; each tightens behind its own seam with no change here. No mark reads `continuity` yet, and color from `status` waits for the theme's roles (#533).

## 137. D-137 — A reader refuses a newer schema by name before it reads a key, and every reader applies sanity limits the host may tighten

**Context.** #541, from #531's review (H, item 2). visualize is on Hex, and the boards milestone makes its documents travel: a design or a board is written in one host (metresis) and read in another (Exio), across releases and across trust boundaries. A design already carried `version` and `Visualize.Chart.Migration.migrate/1` already refused one above the current, as `{:unsupported_version, v}` worded "version 3 is not supported" — which neither said what the document needed nor what this library reads, and was the same word as a version of `0`. `from_json/1` ran the codec before the migration, so a newer document was decoded by the older schema first, its unknown keys made atoms, and only then refused. A library fragment's JSON (`Fragment.to_json/1`) carried no version at all, and a bundle's provenance none. And no reader bounded what a document could ask for: a design is authored, so the codec has always trusted its names (D-57), but a host reading designs from elsewhere had no way to say how deep, how long or how many ticks it would accept.

**Decision.**
- **One version policy for every document** (spec/14 §9.1): a writer stamps the schema version it wrote — a design its `version`, a fragment document `"$version"`, a bundle's provenance `schema`; a reader refuses a newer one as `{:newer_schema, needs, this}`, worded "needs schema v3, this is v2", **before anything else is done with it** — on the parsed JSON before the codec, before the limits, before migration and validation, before a bundle's first put; an older one migrates; an unstamped fragment document or bundle reads as the current version, which is what it was written at. `{:unsupported_version, v}` stays for a version below `1`. The board (#539) follows the same rule, and its reader reads every cell's design through `from_map/2` with its limits.
- **Sanity limits on every reader** (spec/14 §9.2): `depth`, `items`, `ticks`, `iterations` and `counts`, with generous defaults in `Visualize.Chart.Limits.defaults/0`, applied by `from_map`, `from_map!`, `from_json` and `Fragment.from_json` whether or not `limits:` is given. A fault is `{path, {:limit, name, max, given}}` in the validator's own `{path, reason}` shape, so `Validator.format/1` words it and one reader serves every error. The structural limits run on the document as given — on the parsed JSON before decoding — and the schema's on the migrated design.
- **`items` is the rows limit.** A design holds no rows: a source is a slot the pool fills at application (spec/14 §7.2). So the proposal's "rows" can only mean a list held inline — a variable's default, a tick's `values`, a domain — and the limit is on the length of any list or map, which also bounds the keys a decoded map can make atoms of.
- **The host holds the number.** The defaults are this library's sanity values and every gallery design passes them; a host loading untrusted boards knows its inputs and tightens them at its layer, through `limits:`. They are sanity limits, not a render budget: runaway protection while a chart draws belongs to the board runtime's supervised cells (#540).

**Consequences.** A host on an older release gets one sentence naming both versions instead of a list of unknown keys or a raise in a migration, and no atom is made from a document it cannot read. `{:newer_schema, …}` is a new reason where `{:unsupported_version, v}` was returned for a newer version, so a consumer matching that reason for a newer version must match the new one. `Fragment.to_json/1` now writes a `"$version"` key; documents written before read as they did, and `from_json(to_json(f))` is still `f`. `Fragment.from_json` gains two error reasons, `{:newer_schema, …}` and `{:limits, errors}`, in its existing single-reason shape. A design over a default limit no longer reads through `from_map/1`; the defaults sit orders of magnitude above any gallery design, and a host with a genuine need sets its own. A variable's value is the host's at application and is not checked, so a limit bounds the document, not every value the document can be bound to. `Composite.flatten/2` is not limited: it reads the host's own store, whose cycles it already refuses, and a fragment that arrived from elsewhere passed `Fragment.from_json/2`'s limits on the way in.

## 138. D-138 — Units are UCUM codes and a fixed list of quantities chooses display scaling; an axis takes them as a `measure`

**Context.** #536, from #531 item 6. A unit needs one name: if metresis says `"s"` and Exio `"sec"`, labels and formatting drift, and a dashboard that embeds both shows two words for one thing. A field also needs to say what it measures so it is displayed sensibly — 1,500 ms as 1.5 s, 2,500,000 bytes as 2.5 MB — and that choice depends on the domain, so it belongs beside #532's derived formats (D-134) and not with each host. Measured before the change: an axis's only unit was `unit`, a literal suffix (#135: `"M"`, `" c/s"`, `"/yr"` in the gallery), the one scaled format was `:duration` over seconds (#191), and metresis labels its y axis with the unit — else the quantity — by building a frame label from its query result in every panel (`MetresisWeb.Charts.Design.y_labels/1`), a stopgap its own comment names as waiting for visualize.

**Decision.**
- **Units are UCUM codes** (ucum.org), as OpenTelemetry and FHIR use them. `Visualize.Unit` reads a strict subset of UCUM's grammar — products `.`, quotients `/`, signed integer exponents, integer factors, `{annotation}` blocks — over a fixed table: prefixes `k M G m u n Ki Mi Gi`, atoms `s min h d By bit % Hz Cel [degF] K m g W J Pa`. A symbol is matched whole before it is split into prefix and atom, as UCUM requires, so `min` is the minute; a prefix is refused on a non-metric atom (`kmin`). Strict enough that `sec`, `B` and `m//s` fail; permissive enough for every code OpenTelemetry's conventions and `Telemetry.Metrics` produce. Parentheses and UCUM's other atoms are left out until a source sends one.
- **A code has a dimension and an exact factor** (rationals, so 1,500 `us` is exactly 1.5 `ms`), and `Cel` and `[degF]`, UCUM's special units, have no factor. Scaling between two codes of one dimension is multiplication by the ratio; **conversion between unit systems (°C/°F) is out of scope** and raises.
- **`Telemetry.Metrics` units map by a table of atoms**, with no dependency on `telemetry_metrics`. `:native` is refused (it has no size until converted) and so is an unknown atom, rather than becoming an annotation that would hide a typo.
- **A fixed list of twelve quantities**, each with a base unit and a display family: `duration`, `information`, `data_rate`, `frequency`, `ratio`, `count`, `temperature`, `length`, `speed`, `power`, `energy`, `pressure`. The list grows by proposal. A unit implies its quantity by its dimension; a quantity alone means its base unit; a pair whose dimensions disagree is refused.
- **Decimal information by default; the data picks the family.** A unit in `kBy` (UCUM's 1,000 bytes, and `Telemetry.Metrics`' `:kilobyte`) or `By` scales `B → kB → MB → GB`; one written with a binary prefix (`MiBy`) scales `KiB → MiB → GiB`; one in `bit` scales in bits. Likewise a frequency in `Hz` scales in hertz and an event rate (`{request}/s`) in `/h`, `/min`, `/s`, and an energy in `kW.h` in watt-hours. There is no setting: which family is a fact the unit already states.
- **The display unit is the largest family member in which the domain's magnitude is at least one**, and the ticks are formatted by #532's `notation/2` and `number/2` in that unit, then suffixed with its symbol (`1.5 s`, `45%`, `12/s`). One display unit per axis, as one SI prefix per axis (D-134); a log axis gives each tick its own. A `count` is SI from a thousand.
- **The axis key is `measure`, a node of `unit` and `quantity`.** `unit` on an axis is already #135's literal suffix, used by the gallery with non-UCUM strings; giving it a second meaning would make `unit: "M"` ambiguous, and renaming it would be a version-3 migration of every stored design for a word. A node also matches the field description's shape (#531), so #535 supplies an axis's measure from a feed's schema in one place. `measure` and `quantity` are `:content`, and **they are facts, not knobs**: what is shown (`format`, `ticks`) stays the design's to override, and an explicit `format` wins over the scaling.
- **The label rule is "the unit, else the quantity, else nothing", and the axis says it once.** When the ticks carry the unit, nothing more is drawn; when they cannot — a count (`1`, `{request}`), an explicit `format`, a non-numeric scale — the axis draws a title by the rule, placed as an `{:axis, s}` label and inside the axis's region, unless the design anchors its own label there. Drawing both a suffix and a title of the same unit (`s` above `0.5 s, 1 s, 1.5 s`) was considered and not taken: it says one thing twice, and a title in the data's unit (`ms`) beside ticks scaled to `s` would contradict them.

**Consequences.** No existing design changes: `measure` is a new key, and the goldens move only where a design or a fixture opts in. A host stops computing an axis's unit label (metresis's `y_labels/1` and its number formats can go once it passes `measure`, a follow-up there) and a source's unit reaches the screen in one spelling. Information defaults to decimal, so a host that wants `MiB` says so in its unit. Temperature, speed and ratio display in the unit they are given; °C/°F conversion, physical anchors and the `thermal` ramp a temperature selects (#533) are later proposals that can read the same measure.

## 139. D-139 — A theme is a stored document read like any other, edited in a theme mode of the builder whose checks are the library's contracts and whose color-vision simulation is a pure transform

**Context.** #534, from #531 and #533. Themes were code: `Visualize.Theme` is a struct, `default/0` and `dark/0` are functions, and a host that wanted its own look wrote Elixir and saw nothing until a chart drew. Once a theme carries meaning-bearing ramps and roles with contracts (D-135) and color comes from the theme rather than from a design's literals (#531), the theme is where a host sets its look, and it needs a tool. Five things had to be decided: what a theme is when it is stored and how it is read back; how a host ships one and a design finds it; where the tool sits in the builder; what it previews; and how a person judges what they cannot see — a contract broken by an edit, and a ramp's reading under a color-vision deficiency.

**Decision.**
- **A theme document is the inline `:theme` node of spec/14 §2.5, stamped.** `Visualize.Chart.Theme.to_map/1` is `%{version: v, theme: node}` with every field of the struct; `to_json/1` is the fragment document `{"$version": v, "theme": {…}}` that `Visualize.Chart.Fragment.to_json/1` writes for a theme fragment. There is no second schema and no second version: the theme node is a node of the design schema, so it is versioned by `Visualize.Chart.Migration.current/0` and read under D-137's one policy — the version gate before anything else, then the `depth` and `items` limits, then the migration of §9 (no step so far changes a theme node, and one that does will migrate stored themes with the designs that carry them), then per-kind validity, which includes the meaning contracts. A theme document holds no variable, since nothing applies it, and a name that is not built-in is not a theme; both are faults in the validator's shape, so one reader serves every error. The store keeps the fragment, `%{theme: node}`, which is already a library kind (§19.8); nothing about a library entry changes.
- **A host ships a theme as a value, found by its name.** `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Store.theme/2` loads the stored theme whose own `name` — else the last segment of whose entry name — is the one asked for, read through the codec. The builder's preview resolves a design's `theme: name` that is not built-in through it, which is the `theme:` option of `apply/2` (§2.5) supplied by the library rather than by the host's code. A host outside the builder does the same with `from_map/2` and `apply(…, theme: theme)`.
- **The theme mode is the builder's shape when the open chart is a theme** (§19.9: the editor's shape is the open fragment's kind). It is entered by opening a theme entry, by *New → theme*, or by *Theme* on a chart, which opens the chart's own theme — the stored entry when the design names one, else its built-in or inline theme as a new theme. In the mode the main column, which had nothing to show for a theme, is the **preview sheet** and the inspector is the **theme editor**: series colors, chrome (background, surface, text, axis, grid), fonts, each ramp's anchors with the interpolated ramp drawn live, the roles, and *derive from* — a built-in or a stored theme whose values replace the open theme's, so dark from light or color-safe from either is one choice and then a change of tones. Edits write the open site's `theme` node as every form writes a node, and saving is the existing *Save to library* under the kind's per-kind validity, which already refuses a broken contract.
- **The preview sheet is fixed samples, not the person's chart.** A theme is judged against everything it must serve, so the sheet draws one sample per thing a theme carries — categorical series, a status chart colored by the five roles, a thermal and a diverging chart, and axes, grid and text — each through `apply/2` and `render/2` in the theme being edited, built unchecked (`Visualize.Theme.build/2`) so a theme that breaks a contract still draws while it is fixed. Designs from the store can be added to the sheet, drawn the same way.
- **Live checks are the library's contracts, reported per ramp.** `Visualize.Theme.violations/1` is `check/1` run on each ramp and role alone, so every broken one is named, not only the first; the editor shows each under the ramp or role it concerns with the contract's own words. Role *positions* are the library's (D-135: `:good`, `:warning` and `:bad` stand at 0, 0.5 and 1 of `status`), so the editor draws each role at its position and sets the color there — the anchor that stands at it — rather than moving it: a role that moved would break the guarantee that a role and its position are one color.
- **Color-vision simulation is a pure color transform.** `Visualize.Color.Vision.simulate/2` applies the Machado, Oliveira and Fernandes (2009) matrices at severity 1.0 for protanopia, deuteranopia and tritanopia in linear sRGB, then re-encodes and clamps. The model is physiologically based and one matrix per deficiency, so it is cheap enough to run over every color of a preview on every edit; Brettel, Viénot and Mollon (1997) is more exact for tritanopia but is two half-planes per deficiency, and Viénot, Brettel and Mollon (1999) covers dichromacy only by a projection that does not grade severity. The sheet is simulated by mapping every literal color of its rendered markup through the transform, so an interpolated ramp color is simulated as it is drawn, not as an interpolation of simulated anchors. Each ramp carries a lightness strip — the CIE L* of each step as a gray — because lightness is what a reader with no hue at all still reads, and it is what the monotonic-lightness contract protects.

**Consequences.** A theme is data a host stores, exports in a bundle and ships, and a theme from another host is refused by name when it is newer and bounded when it is large, as a design is. `Visualize.Chart.Fragment.read_json/2` returns the version a fragment document was stamped with, which `from_json/2` drops, so a reader that migrates what it reads has it. A design that names a stored theme previews in it in the builder; outside the builder the host still passes `theme:`, because `apply/2` takes no store. The editor cannot save a theme that breaks a contract as a valid one, and cannot move a role off its position; a host that needs another meaning proposes a ramp or a role (D-135). The simulation is a model of the commonest dichromacies, not a measurement of a reader: anomalous trichromacy (a severity below 1) is not offered, and the transform is not used for anything but judging by eye.

## 140. D-140 — A feed is a behaviour whose window, budget and push are requests visualize finishes; the loop is a value the board's process holds; live and history combine by window in visualize

**Context.** #537, from #531 (review F and the further considerations of 2026-10-11, §1–§5). D-136 gave a feed's answer a shape — a schema by version and a delivery — but nothing asked a feed for one: a host polled and re-applied by hand. Providers differ: some answer a window and a point budget, some push, and some can only hand over rows. Five things had to be decided: the contract's form and its minimum; what visualize does when a provider does less than it was asked; how pushes are ordered and absorbed at a feed's rate; who runs the loop, when the board process that will own it (#540) does not exist yet; and, from the acceptance, how one slot shows the last hour from Exio's `:telemetry` and older data from metresis.

**Decision.**
- **A behaviour plus messages** (spec/08 §11.1). A feed is a struct whose module implements `Visualize.Feed`: `describe/1` answers `{version, schema, supports}`, `supports` a subset of `[:window, :budget, :push]`; `fetch(feed, window, budget)` answers `{:ok, delivery}`; `subscribe/2` and `unsubscribe/2` are optional. A protocol was not taken: providers are modules and processes, not data types, and a protocol would dispatch on a value that is mostly a handle. Every callback answers `{:error, reason}` rather than raising, so a feed that is down is a cell's state.
- **The window and the budget are requests; only rows are required** (§11.2). Where `supports` lacks one, `Visualize.Feed.finish/3` does it: it trims to the window by the schema's one `:time` field, and reduces to the budget with `lttb/3` or `m4/3`, grouped by the `:category` fields so each series keeps its shape, every `:number` field reduced and the union of the kept rows kept whole, recording `reduced_by: :visualize`. A feed that names `:budget` is never reduced again. A schema with no time field is reduced against the row's position and not trimmed, so a feed without one honors its window itself.
- **Push is two messages, schema before data, with `describe` as the fallback** (§11.3): `{:visualize_feed, ref, {:schema, version, schema}}` and `{:visualize_feed, ref, {:delivery, delivery}}`, a pushed delivery an increment. The subscribe handshake sends the schema first. Ordering makes push cheap; the fallback makes it correct.
- **The loop is a value, not a process** (§11.4). `Visualize.Feed.Monitor` is held by the owning process — #540's board process, a LiveView until then — which calls `tick/2` per frame and `handle_message/2` from `handle_info/2`. Pushes buffer and the next tick drains them in one merge, one trim and one reduction: the data-rate/frame-rate split of #300 and #361, with no other backpressure. A monitor process was not taken: it would be a second owner of the window and of "now", which #531 gives the board, and would copy every delivery across one more process boundary. #540 builds the board runtime on the value, as the board will hold D-136's cache. Errors and staleness are the states `:loading`, `:live`, `:stale` and `{:down, reason}`, with "capped" and "no data yet" read from the delivery.
- **Live plus history is a combining feed in visualize** (§11.5), not the connector's job. Choosing the stores is the connector's — only it knows that Exio's telemetry and metresis's capture are one metric — but routing a window across feeds, merging and deduplicating the seam is the same for every host, and two connectors writing it twice is the drift #531 exists to remove. `Visualize.Feed.Combine` asks its feeds freshest first: each serves what it can, its delivery's `window` says where it starts, and the rest of the window goes to the next, each part trimmed to `[from, served)` so a seam row comes from the fresher feed. Every part must describe the same schema version, because a delivery names one; making two catalogs agree is the connector's. Pushes pass through from the first feed unchanged, which the shared version makes correct.
- **Two providers prove the contract** (§11.6). `Visualize.Feed.Signals` (pull, window honored, its tick the time field) and `Visualize.Feed.Telemetry` (push, one feed per metric: `t`, `value` and the tags, the unit through D-138's `Unit.from_telemetry/1`). The telemetry provider reads `Telemetry.Metrics` definitions by their fields, so `telemetry_metrics` is a test-only dependency, and `:telemetry` is optional under D-50's declaration. Its collector stamps each row on receipt with a strictly increasing clock, which makes the monitor's seam between a fetch and the pushes after it exact.

**Consequences.** A host stops polling and re-applying by hand: it holds a monitor, ticks it, and passes `deliveries/1` and `cache/1` to `apply/2` or a compiled chart. A small source implements two callbacks and works; a large one adds `:budget` to stop shipping raw points. A board never knows which provider fills a slot. Exio can show its own last hour live and metresis's history in one chart once a metresis feed (in `metresis_client`) describes the telemetry provider's schema. A metric becomes a slot, not a column, so a board with ten metrics binds ten feeds, which the monitor polls and drains independently. The telemetry provider stamps rows on receipt, so a row's time is the collector's, a few microseconds after the event; a host that needs the event's own time sends it as a measurement. Reduction is per tick, of the window's whole holding: a push feed's rows are held unreduced within the window, bounded by the window and the provider's retention. The conformance kit (#538) drives both providers through the behaviour alone.

## 141. D-141 — A board is a versioned 12-column grid of composite refs that never flattens; its sources are its cells' slots, namespaced per cell, and a connector's separate binding fills them

**Context.** #539, from #531 (§1, further considerations items 3–5, review H). A dashboard is many charts laid out together, sharing a time window and interaction but not geometry. A composite flattens into one picture, so it is the wrong tool (spec/14 §19.6). metresis holds its dashboards as panels on a 12-column grid, each a query plus a stored design, and Exio is to show the same boards with its own feeds. So the board has to be data that travels between hosts while its feeds do not. Five questions were open: what a cell refers to, the grid model, how board variables reach the components, how the sources a board needs are stated when one design appears in two cells, and where the knowledge of feeds lives. The runtime (#540) is separate. This decision is the value alone.

**Decision.**
- **A board is a value with a schema of its own** (spec/15 §1): `%Visualize.Board{version, meta, cells, window, zone, vars, sync, layout}`. Cells are a map by name, and a name is a cell's identity, as a frame's is in a design. A cell is `{x, y, w, h}` plus `ref`, a **composite id** in the store (spec/14 §19.2). The entry it names is a composite or a flat design, which `Composite.flatten/2` reads alike. A design has no id of its own (spec/14 §19.8), and a board holds no designs inline, so everything it shows is in the store, where the builder edits it and a bundle carries it. The board's schema is not added to `Visualize.Chart.Schema`'s node kinds. Those are the kinds of a design's fragments, and a board is not a fragment of a design.
- **Twelve columns, not generalized** (spec/15 §1.4). It is metresis's grid, so its migration is mechanical, and 12 divides every layout seen. A `columns` key is `:unknown_key` today. Adding it later is additive, under a newer version an older reader refuses by name.
- **Shared state** (spec/15 §1.5). The window is `nil`, `%{last: seconds}` or `%{from, to}` in `Etc/UTC`. Now comes from the caller, never from a feed, and `window/2` resolves it. `zone` is for display and is checked as a time scale's zone is. `sync` is the group every cell with `sync: true` joins. The board's group replaces a design's own, and a component that cannot join is a fault at the cell, never silently left out, so a gauge says `sync: false`.
- **Reflow stacks cells in reading order** (spec/15 §1.6). Below `breakpoint` (768 px), cells are taken by `y`, then `x`, then name, and stacked full width, each keeping its height. `layout`'s defaults are metresis's: 80 px rows and 8 px gaps. `boxes/2` is a CSS grid's arithmetic, and a cell's box is the size its component is applied at.
- **Variables cascade: cell over board over the component's default** (spec/15 §5). A board variable is a default and reaches only components that declare its name. One no component declares is not a fault, as an unused pool entry is not. A cell variable must name one its component declares. Nothing is renamed on the way in. Renaming is a use site's job (spec/14 §19.4).
- **Version and limits are D-137's** (spec/15 §2.3). The map form carries `version`, and the JSON form the stamp `"$version"`, as a fragment document does, so a reader gates any non-design document by one key. A newer board is `{:newer_schema, needs, this}` before a key is decoded or a cell is read, and an unstamped board reads as current. Boards begin at schema version 2, so a lower version is `{:unsupported_version, v}`. `depth` and `items` bound the board document, and every component is read through `Chart.from_map/2` with the board reader's limits, which also migrates a stored version 1 design. The JSON decoder leaves an unknown key a string, so a misspelling makes no atom.
- **Validation in the path-error shape, alone and given a store** (spec/15 §3). Alone, it checks keys, types, off-grid cells (`{:off_grid, 12}`), overlaps (`{:overlap, other}`, once per pair at the later name), the window and wrong-kind refs. Given a store, a fetch function or a map of id to fragment, it adds dangling refs (`{:dangling, id}`), cycles, component design faults under `[:cells, c, :ref | path]`, undeclared cell variables, and cells that cannot join the group. The store is a fetch, not the builder's `Store` behaviour, because that compiles only with Phoenix and a board must not.
- **Required sources are the aggregate of the cells' slots, keyed `{cell, slot}`** (spec/15 §4.1), each with `fields`, `types` and its whole contract from `Chart.contract/1` (D-136). One design in two cells is two sets of slots, bound independently.
- **The binding is a separate value, the connector's** (spec/15 §4.2–4.4). `%Visualize.Board.Binding{slots: %{{cell, slot} => feed}}`. `check/3` reports, by path `[:cells, c, :sources, s]`, every unbound slot (`{:unbound, :binding, s}`), every contract the bound feed's schema cannot meet (the reasons `apply/2` uses, so the words match), a feed with no schema to check (`{:undescribed, feed}`), and every binding to a slot no cell requires (`:not_required`). A slot with an empty contract is not looked up. `pools/2` turns a binding and a host's sources into one pool per cell. The binding has no JSON form here: it is the host's, and it travels with the host.
- **`realize/4` falls out and is kept** (spec/15 §6): every cell resolved, its variables cascaded and narrowed, its group written and its component applied at its box's size, purely, with the schema cache threaded across cells. `describe:` is called with `{cell, slot}`, which the connector maps to its feed. Faults are reported under their cells, and an unbound slot lands at the same path the binding check uses.

**Consequences.** A metresis panel maps to a cell field for field, and its stored design to a component. A host can check a board against its store, and its binding against its feeds' schemas, before any data flows, and sees the same reasons `apply/2` would give one design. The board holds nothing a provider owns, so one board document serves metresis and Exio unchanged. Boards add a spec document, 15. A cell whose component cannot sync must say so once the board names a group. That is a deliberate cost: a cell that dropped out of the group silently would be found only by hovering. The window is carried and resolved, but not applied to a component here. Windowing rows is the feeds' and the runtime's work (#540), as are cell states, processes and rendering. A bundle carrying boards with their components is #542's.

## 142. D-142 — A chart is accessible by construction: a summary and a data table generated from the design and its data, keyboard navigation inside one tab stop, reduced motion on both sides, and roles that read without hue

**Context.** #546, from #531 item 8 and its color item 6. Before it, a rendered chart was `role="img"` with a `<title>` and `<desc>` only when the design's `meta` carried them (D-56), so most charts were an unnamed image; `aria-labelledby` named both texts, which makes a long description part of the name; a canvas, and a hybrid chart's stacked layers, carried nothing at all — three `role="img"` SVG layers, none of them named. Nothing reached a chart's points from the keyboard. The motion a chart has is the server's — a scale's `transition` and a mark's eased readings (§12.5) — and the components' CSS `transition`; none of it asked whether the reader wants motion. And a status shown by the theme's roles (D-135) rested on hue alone, which a grayscale print and about one man in twelve cannot tell apart.

**Decision.**
- **The text alternative is generated, not written** ([14-declarative-chart](14-declarative-chart.md) §21.1). A design already says what a chart is — its kind follows from its marks, frame and layouts, its axes from its scales and measures (D-138), its series from its `series` channels — and the applied data says the rest: the domains and the extremes. `Visualize.Chart.Summary` reads both, so every chart, the gallery's included, is described with no author effort, and the description follows the data. Values are written as the axis writes them, in its measure's display unit, to four significant digits rather than at the ticks' step, so a summary never contradicts the axis and never rounds an extreme to a tick. A design's own `meta.description` comes first, so an author who knows what the chart is *for* still says it.
- **Every root is named, and the name is short** (§21.2, amending D-56). The title defaults to the summary's name — the design's name, else its kind — and `aria-labelledby` names only the `<title>`, `aria-describedby` the `<desc>`: a paragraph as an accessible name is read in full on every visit, a description is read on request.
- **The data table lives beside the chart** (§21.3). A `role="img"` element presents its subtree as one image, so a table inside the `<svg>` — a `<foreignObject>` — would be hidden by the very role that names the chart. The table is an HTML string, visually hidden by the clip pattern so it is still read, one table per data mark, columns from the fields a scale maps: what the reader would read off the axes, not the layout's pixels.
- **Whatever stands for the chart carries the summary** (§21.4). A canvas is named and described (`role="img"`, `aria-label`, and its fallback content as the description); a hybrid chart's SVG layers are `aria-hidden`, since none of them is the picture, and the page names the wrapper of the layers with `Summary.attrs/2`.
- **Keyboard navigation is one tab stop per chart, arrows inside it** (§21.5, #558). A chart that carries its points' readout is focusable once; the arrow keys move between its points and its series and a polite live region reads the point. A board (#539) adds the order across cells with the same rule one level up — a cell is one stop, Tab moves between cells — so neither level needs the other's details.
- **Reduced motion is honored on both sides** (§21.6, #559). The hooks read `prefers-reduced-motion` and tell the server, and a compiled chart told to reduce motion jumps every transition and eased reading to its target; the components' CSS transition stops under the same media query.
- **A role reads without hue** (§21.7, #560). Each of the five roles carries a glyph and a dash pattern the theme fixes, as it fixes their hue order, and a mark can draw them, so a status chart in grayscale still says which zone is which.

**Consequences.** Every root `Visualize.Chart.render/2` writes with `root: true` gains a `<title>` when the design had none, a longer `<desc>`, and `aria-describedby`: the charts golden changes on every line, and only in the root's first element and its `<desc>`. The two component fixtures with a title and a description change for the new attribute. The compiled chart's static, dynamic and backdrop documents gain `aria-hidden="true"`. A root render now also applies the summary's reading of each mark's rows, a second pass over the pipeline, measured against the gallery's cost budgets (#469); a caller that renders many roots per second and needs no summary passes `summary: false`. The later work items add the readout attributes, the reduced-motion option and the roles' glyphs and patterns, each with its own golden consequences recorded in its section.

**Amended 2026-10-20 by D-201 (#803, #805):** the short name is the root's `aria-label` rather than a `<title>` that `aria-labelledby` names; the description's wiring is unchanged.

## 143. D-143 — What a host drew around a chart is marks fed from sources: a horizontal rule, cut rules and bands, a mark's own color scale, a map of colors, polarity on a ramp, and the design's brush

**Context.** #543, from #531 §4 and review I. metresis drew its event markers, its threshold lines and its brush *around* the visualize group, inside its own `<svg>`, placed with the realized frame's scales and its own margins (`MetresisWeb.Charts.Frame.events/1`, `thresholds/1` and `svg/1`; `MetresisWeb.DashboardLive.brushed_window/5` turning the brush's pixels back into instants through a time scale it rebuilt). A second host — Exio drawing the same design — would lose all three, or copy them. The audit (#565) found the geometry already in the library: an instant is a `:rule`, a span an `:x_band` (#1). What was missing was the rest. A rule could stand at `y` only in a polar frame, so a threshold had no mark. Marks are unclipped, so a span that began before the window was drawn across the axis. A style's color binding always read the frame's `color` scale, so events colored by severity in a chart whose `color` scale is its series' would be colored as series — the spec even said so ("whatever the mark's `scales` say"), while a `series` channel's validation honored the rename and its paint did not. A feed's schema says what a severity means (`status`, D-136) and the theme draws meanings (D-135), but no scale read a map from a state to a role, and nothing read `polarity`. And the brush had no way to say where the plot was or what its pixels meant, so every host computed both.

**Decision.**
- **A `:rule` at `y` in a cartesian frame is a horizontal rule across the plot width**, the counterpart of the polar arc at a radius, labeled at its left end. It is what a threshold is, fed from a threshold source (`y: :value`) or from a feed's `target` (`y: fact(:primary, :latency, :target)` over `take(1)`). A new mark type was not added: the rule already means "a line through the plot at a reading", and the validator already allowed `y` without `x`, drawing nothing in a cartesian frame.
- **In a cartesian frame a rule and an x band are cut to the plot area.** A rule outside it draws nothing; a band is clamped to `[0, width]` and draws nothing wholly outside. A whole-mark clip path was considered and not taken: it would change every mark on every backend, the binary stream has no clip record, and only these two marks span the plot by construction.
- **A mark's color scale is `color` renamed by its `scales` node, for every color the mark reads** — its series paint, its style's color bindings, the inference over both, the validator's domain check and a compiled chart's regions. This reverses §4.3's "whatever the mark's `scales` say" for style bindings, which made a renamed color scale half work. No existing design changes: no gallery chart, preset or fixture renames `color` and binds a style color, and the one test that renames it reads a `series` channel, which the validator already treated this way.
- **An ordinal color scale's range may be a map of colors** (`:color_map`), a category to a color, the shape a schema's `status` is in, so `range: fact(:events, :severity, :status)` colors by the feed's meaning. The realized scale keeps the domain's members the map names; any other value is outside the domain and draws `unknown`, which on a map range defaults to the theme's `:axis` slot as on `color` (D-122). In JSON it is a `$colors` pair list, since an object's keys are strings and a category need not be.
- **`polarity` on a color scale over a ramp places the ramp by worth**: `:lower_is_better` reads it as it is, `:higher_is_better` reversed, `:neutral` folded about the middle (`|2t − 1|`). With a `:threshold` scale over the feed's `target` and `range: {:ramp, :status}`, a reading on the better side is `:good` and one beyond it `:bad`, whichever way the feed says is better. Polarity was put on the scale rather than on a mark because it says how values map to meaning, which is what a scale is; on any other range it is a fault, since a list or a map already says each color.
- **The brush is a design key, `interaction.brush: :x`.** The brush frame (the sync frame's rule) writes `data-vis-brush`, its `x` mapping (`data-vis-brush-x`, the sync group's five numbers) and its plot area in the chart's coordinates (`data-vis-brush-extent`). `BrushHook` reads them from a descendant when its element gives no type or extent of its own, re-reads them on `updated()`, and adds `domain` to every `brush_select` it can map, not only in a sync group. It is held to a cartesian frame with a linear or time `x`, as a sync frame is.
- **What stays the host's**, from the audit: the hover text and a click are a tooltip (§5.7); the compass needle is `:needle` (#392); a state timeline's row names are an axis on a `:band` scale; the legend is the frame's (§4.5). A "capped" note is page text, and belongs to a delivery's `capped` (D-136) when the board shows it.

**Consequences.** A host draws a chart's overlays by adding marks and a scale to the design, and every host that applies the design draws the same overlays in its theme's colors on SVG, the canvas, the binary stream and the hybrid page, since rules and bands are ordinary elements. metresis can delete its overlay components, its brush geometry and its inverse once it adopts them, a follow-up in metresis after visualize's release. A rule or an x band that a design placed outside its plot no longer draws, which no gallery chart does. A mark that renamed `color` and colored its style by a field now reads the renamed scale, as the spec said for its channels. `polarity`, `brush` and the `$colors` term are new keys and a new term: an older reader refuses a design that uses them only as an unknown key or term, and D-137's version policy is unchanged, since none of them changes a key that existed.

## 144. D-144 — A string naming a member of an enumeration alternative reads back as that member

**Context.** #554. spec/14 §11.2 promises `from_json(to_json(chart)) == chart` for every valid chart, and two gallery designs broke it. The first was a missing tagged term: `{:scale, :min}` and `{:scale, :max}` (§5.2, #420) had none, so `to_json/1` raised. The second showed once the first was fixed and every gallery design was held to the round trip. An axis's `format` is `{:one_of, [:string, {:enum, [:duration]}]}`. An enumerated atom is written as its string (§11.1), so `:duration` became `"duration"`, and the reader took the first alternative of a string's shape, `:string`. *Load age* therefore read back with a format specifier spelled `duration` in place of `Visualize.Format.duration/1`. An audit of every `{:one_of, …}` in the schema found no other key with both a string and an enumeration alternative. It also found no other tuple a validator accepts without a JSON form.

**Decision.**
- **A string that names a member of an enumeration alternative of a `{:one_of, …}` reads as that member, before any other alternative is tried.** Otherwise the reader takes the first alternative of the value's shape, as before. The rule is the codec's and holds for every key, so a later key that pairs a string with an enumeration needs nothing of its own.
- **The atom keeps its plain string.** Tagging the enumeration's member (`{"$atom": "duration"}`) was considered and not taken. It would make one key's enumeration the only one in a design not written as its string, and it would change the form of documents already written.

**Consequences.** The format string `"duration"` cannot be written: it reads as `:duration`. As a specifier it means nothing, and on a time axis it would print the literal word on every tick, so no design loses a meaning. A document written before this reads as it was meant: `"duration"` was only ever written for `:duration`. The property test's generator draws `{:scale, _}` channels and `:duration` formats from now on, and leaves out the string `"duration"`. The gallery holds every design to the JSON round trip and to the default limits through the JSON reader.

## 145. D-145 — The feed conformance kit is checks that answer findings plus a macro that writes the tests; it ships in `lib/` and references no ExUnit function, so it needs no guard

**Context.** #538, from #531 item 12. D-140 made a feed a behavior, and spec/08 §11 says what a provider must honor: a schema by content hash, deliveries that say truly what they served, capabilities that are true, the schema before the data, errors as values. Each provider reading that prose for itself is exactly the drift #531 exists to remove, so the reading had to be a test every provider runs — `Signals` and `Telemetry` here, metresis's feed in metresis. Three things had to be decided: how a shipped test is shaped so that it can itself be tested; how it compiles in a consumer, for whom ExUnit is a test-time tool; and what "honest" means where a single delivery cannot show it.

**Decision.**
- **Two layers** (spec/08 §12.1). `Visualize.Feed.Conformance.check/3` runs one of six checks and answers findings, `{check, reason}` terms with `format/1` to word them; `use Visualize.Feed.Conformance, opts` expands, inside the caller's `ExUnit.Case` module, to one `describe` block of six tests that `flunk` with the formatted findings. Findings are values, so the kit's negatives are ordinary assertions against a deliberately broken fake feed rather than a test suite run inside a test suite, and a host can call `check/2` anywhere — a health page, a connector's own probe.
- **No guard, because nothing to guard** (spec/08 §12.1). The kit lives in `lib/` and its module calls no ExUnit function: `describe`, `test` and `flunk` appear only in the quoted code the macro returns, compiled in the caller where `use ExUnit.Case` imported them. A guard like the optional dependencies' (`Code.ensure_loaded?(ExUnit.Case)`, D-45, D-50) was considered and not taken. ExUnit is an application of Elixir's, not a dependency, and whether it is on the code path when a dependency compiles is Mix's code-path pruning to decide (the trap D-45 records for Phoenix): the kit would exist or not depending on how a consumer's build happened to load it. Listing `:ex_unit` in `extra_applications` would start it in every host's release. A module with no reference to ExUnit compiles the same everywhere, and the consumer check asserts it loads in a `MIX_ENV=prod` consumer.
- **Every callback runs in a monitored process** (spec/08 §12.2), with the caller in `$callers`, under `timeout:`. A raise is then the finding `{:raised, call, reason}` — the contract says errors are values (§11.1), so a raise is a fault to report, not a crash of the checker — and a hang is `{:timeout, call}`. Process isolation, not `rescue`, is how the kit observes a raise. `$callers` lets a provider backed by a sandboxed repository answer from the probe's process, as from a `Task`.
- **Honesty is checked against a second fetch** (spec/08 §12.3). One delivery cannot show that it is complete, so the kit also fetches the **open** window `[nil, to]` with no budget and compares: rows the feed holds within the served window must be in the delivery (the served window is a claim of completeness), `capped: false` means the feed held nothing between the asked `from` and where it served from, `reduced_by` must agree with the row counts with and without the budget. `capped: true` must show a shortfall: the served window starts after the asked `from`. These read §10.4's words as checkable facts without any knowledge of the provider. A provider that is asked before its own data begins and has nothing there — a signal before tick 0 — is uncapped, and one that may have dropped older data — the telemetry collector before its start — may say capped; both are honest, and the kit accepts both.
- **What is declared is probed, and what is not declared is not done** (spec/08 §12.3). A capability the options give nothing to probe is a finding (`{:no_probe, c}`), so a provider cannot pass `:window` by being asked only `nil`. A feed that does not name `:push` must refuse `subscribe/2`.
- **The options are expressions** (spec/08 §12.2). `feed:` and `down:` are evaluated in each generated test, in the test process, so a provider whose feed is a process starts it with `start_supervised!/1` and gets a fresh one per check; `push:` is a function of the feed that makes it push, since only the provider knows how. `windows:` is a list because a window is itself a list.

**Consequences.** A provider's suite gains six tests with one `use`; metresis runs the same reading of the contract as this library does, and a host that wants a stricter rule proposes it here, where both providers are checked by it. The kit's module is compiled into every consumer, test or not — a few kilobytes of pure functions nothing calls in production. `Visualize.Feed.Signals` and `Visualize.Feed.Telemetry` pass unchanged. The kit fetches each probe window twice and every capability's probes on top, so a provider whose fetch is expensive should give it small windows. Resolution is checked only as "not finer than claimed", in the time field's own units for numeric times; a provider whose resolution is a name the kit does not know (other than `:second` … `:week`) is not checked on it.

## 146. D-146 — A board runs as one process that owns the shared state and the feeds and one heap-limited process per cell that the board restarts; the host places the supervisor; every cell state is drawn

**Context.** #540, from #531 (further considerations §2, §3 and §5). A board (D-141) is a value, and the feed loop (D-140) is a value its owner ticks. Something has to keep a board live: drive the feeds, hand each cell its data, draw many charts at once, and show loading, empty, stale and failed data on purpose. Four things had to be decided. The first was how many processes, and who owns what. The second was how a pathological design is stopped without a scheduler of our own. The third was who supervises, since #540 left that open and the policy is that a host is in control. The fourth was where time (the window, late rows, gaps) is handled, and how each state is drawn.

**Decision.**
- **One board process, one process per cell** (spec/15 §8.2, §8.3). `Visualize.Board.Server` owns the window, the zone, the variables, the sync group and the board's single now, read once per turn from its `clock:`. It also owns the `Feed.Monitor`: it ticks it at the board's data rate, routes pushes to it and re-asks every feed (`Monitor.refetch/1`) when a window is set. It sends each cell its slots' deliveries, schemas and statuses with a `send` only when they changed. `Visualize.Board.Cell` applies, compiles and steps its component, and sends its views **straight to the subscribers**, so no frame crosses the board's mailbox. A cell coalesces its data messages and draws only the newest, so one that falls behind skips to the present. Fairness is the BEAM scheduler's. A custom scheduler, or one renderer for all cells, was not taken: either would make the slowest design everyone's frame rate.
- **Runaway protection is `max_heap_size` with `kill: true`, and the restart is the board's** (§8.3). Each cell sets the flag first (`max_heap:` words, 25,000,000 by default), so a design that grows its heap without bound is killed alone. Cells are `:temporary` under a `DynamicSupervisor` the board links as their parent. The board monitors them, draws `{:crashed, reason}` at once and restarts a dead cell after a backoff (100 ms doubling to 10 s, reset after 30 s alive). A supervisor's restart was not taken: a design that dies on every frame exhausts any restart intensity and takes its siblings and the board down with it.
- **The library ships `Visualize.Board.Supervisor`; the host adds it to its tree** (§8.1). The library starts no process of its own and has no application. `Server.start/2` takes any `DynamicSupervisor`, and `Server.child_spec/1` places one board under any supervisor, so a host keeps control of where boards live. Leaving the host to write the supervisor was the alternative. It was not taken because shipping one names the place, gives one line to copy and lets the library state the board's restart policy (`:transient`), at no cost to the host's control.
- **A board is started whole or not at all.** A board that does not validate given its store, or whose binding names a feed `feeds:` lacks, is refused with every fault before any process runs.
- **Time: UTC in, the zone out, rows by timestamp, gaps by `continuity`** (§8.5). The board's zone is written into every `:time` scale of a component that names none, beside the group, by the new pure `Board.components/3` that `realize/4` now builds on. The monitor keeps a holding in time order, sorting only when out of order, and keeps a **late** pushed row rather than dropping it at the seam, unless the holding already has that row. A cell breaks a series where two readings are further apart than the field's `continuity`, by inserting a row with that field `nil`, which the line's `defined` already breaks on. Making each mark read `continuity` was the alternative. It was not taken because every mark that draws a run would need it, while a `nil` reading is already how every one of them draws a gap.
- **Every cell state is drawn from the theme, with a role and its glyph** (§8.4). The states are crashed, down, loading, no data, stale (the board's new `stale_after` key, seconds) and capped, in that order of precedence, then live. Each has a role (`:bad`, `:bad`, `:info`, `:neutral`, `:warning`, `:info`) whose color and cue glyph (D-142) it is drawn with. Where there is no chart, the state is the whole cell. Over a chart, stale, capped and down are a banner, so the last data stays readable with what is wrong with it.
- **A compiled chart's one-string group carries the frame's sync and brush attributes**, as the frame's own group does (spec/14 §12.4), so a cell's SVG joins its group's bus. With each frame the cell computes the crosshair and keyboard hooks' attributes from the frame realized over that step's sources.

**Consequences.** A host adds one supervisor to its tree, starts a board with its store, binding and feeds, subscribes, and receives `{:visualize_board, board, {:cell, name, view}}` with a frame or a drawn state for each cell. A heavy cell slows only itself, and a design that blows its heap or raises shows "Crashed, restarting" in its own cell while the rest of the board goes on. A board process draws at one width; a second viewer at another width sees the same frames scaled. Feeds are fetched in the board's process, so a feed whose `fetch` blocks still delays the board's turn. That is the feed contract's to bound, and not the runtime's. The board value gains the key `stale_after`, an additive key an older reader refuses as unknown (D-137). The monitor no longer drops a late row, so a push feed that resends history it already sent dedupes only rows that are exactly equal. A compiled chart's SVG gains its frame's sync and brush attributes, which changes no gallery chart, since none of them names a group or a brush. The LiveView component, the board hook (#575) and the examples page (#576) follow on this runtime.

## 147. D-147 — A board travels in a bundle with its components, their closure and the themes it names; the whole bundle is checked before anything is put, and the store's own import re-keys it

**Context.** #542, from #531 item 4. A board's cells name their components by store id (D-141), and an id is one store's (D-100). A board authored in metresis names ids that Exio's store does not hold, or holds as something else, so the board cannot be copied across as a value. A bundle already crosses stores for one composite (spec/14 §19.7, D-103): it carries the closure of an id and the store's `import/2` relocates it through one `old → new` table. Four questions were open: what a board bundle carries beyond the components' closure, what happens to a binding, how themes named rather than referenced by id travel, and what an import checks before it writes.

**Decision.**
- **A board bundle is the bundle of spec/14 §19.7 with the board's map form under a `board` key** ([15-boards](15-boards.md) §11.1). Its entries are the closure of every cell's component, and the closure of every stored theme the board or its components name. One bundle shape serves a composite and a board, so a store's `import/2` is unchanged: it relocates the entries and ignores the board. The board is in its map form rather than as a struct, so the importer reads it through `Board.from_map/2`'s version gate and limits like any board from elsewhere.
- **Themes travel by the name a design gives.** A design names a theme (spec/14 §2.5), and the store finds it by name (D-139). The flattened component's `theme` is read, and a variable is read through the cell's cascade (spec/15 §5). This is the one way a board names a theme, since it has no `theme` key. Built-in names are not carried. A name is not an identity (spec/14 §19.2), so import cannot re-key it. A carried theme whose name the receiving store already gives a different body is refused as `{:theme_taken, name}`, because one of the two boards would silently draw in the other's theme. The same body under the same name is not a fault. Renaming a theme on import was considered and not taken: the design names it, so renaming would mean rewriting a design's `theme`, and a variable's value in the board, on the way in.
- **Bindings never travel.** A binding names one host's feeds (spec/15 §4.2). A bundle has no field for one, and the importing host binds the board's `required_sources/3` to its own feeds.
- **Everything is checked before anything is put.** The order is the provenance `schema` (D-137), then the board read under the limits, then the entries' count and each entry's `depth` and `items`, then the board validated with the bundle's entries as its store and its components read under the limits, then the theme names against the receiving store. A cell whose component the bundle does not carry is `{:not_carried, id}` at that cell's `ref`. The check uses the bundle, not the receiving store, because the receiving store's ids mean something else. A refused bundle therefore leaves the store as it was, whether or not the store's `import/2` is transactional.
- **The store re-keys; the board follows the table.** The entries go through the store's own `import/2`, which issues every new id, so a new id never collides with one already held. The board's cell refs are rewritten through the table it returns. The board is returned to the host and is not put in the store, because a board is not a library entry. Where a host keeps its boards is its own.

**Consequences.** metresis can export a board with `export_board/3` and Exio import it with `import_board/3`, then bind its own feeds. A bundle gains a `board` key, `nil` for one `export/3` makes, so bundles made before this read unchanged. `Store.theme_name/1` becomes public, because the importer and `theme/2` must agree on what name a stored theme answers to. Three reasons are added: `{:not_carried, id}`, `{:theme_taken, name}` and `{:store, reason}`. A bundle still has no JSON form of its own. Its parts have one (a board's `to_json/1`, a fragment's `to_json/1`), and a host that ships a bundle as text writes them.

## 148. D-148 — A test waits on a message or a count, never on the clock; where the code reads time, the test drives it

**Context.** #520, #567, after #483 and D-114. D-114 kept the suite from *measuring* time, but tests still *waited* on it, and every flake since was a wait that a starved runner outlasted. #483's first force tick arrived 0.36–2.86 s after the subscribe, against a 500 ms `assert_receive`. #512's switch test counted one tick or two: the chart page's tick read `System.monotonic_time`, and the test only rewound the run's `last_tick`, so a tick's elapsed time was the rewind plus however long the test had taken. #567's drawer test measured a strip on the same kind of tick: 31,359 and 31,910 bytes against a 30,000-byte budget, which is some 0.7 s of samples and a hundred columns where the test meant 16 ms and two. #495's raster teardown and #469's comparison sat near their bounds. A byte count, a tick count and a mode are deterministic only if the elapsed time behind them is.

**Decision.**
- **Where code under test reads time to decide something a test asserts, it reads a clock it is handed.** `Visualize.Feed.Telemetry` takes `clock:`, the force simulation takes its subscribers at start (#483), and the chart page reads every instant of a run through its `run_clock` assign, a function of a time unit that is `&System.monotonic_time/1` from the mount (spec/14 §12.6). A test stops that clock and advances it by exactly the milliseconds a tick means. A measurement the page only shows, such as a frame's render time, stays on the real clock: it is shown, never asserted.
- **A test waits on a message or a count, with a generous bound.** An `assert_receive` waits at least 5 s, so its bound says when to give up on a defect and never how fast the machine is. A `refute_receive` asserts absence only after a synchronizing call, and then it is `refute_received`, which reads the mailbox without waiting. A sleep, a poll on the clock and a wait below the floor stay only on an allow-list that gives the reason for each.
- **The rule is checked from source** (spec/12 §6). `test/visualize/no_wall_clock_test.exs`, D-114's check, also reads every test file and test support module for receive waits and sleeps. It fails on an `assert_receive` below the floor, on one with no timeout (ExUnit's default is 100 ms), on one whose timeout is neither an integer literal nor a module attribute bound to one, on any `refute_receive` that waits, on a `receive … after` that waits less than the floor, and on any `Process.sleep` or `:timer.sleep`, unless the site is on its allow-list. An allow-list entry that matches no site fails too.

**Consequences.** A loaded box makes the suite slower, not red: the drawer's strip is 3,418 bytes on every run, and a tick count is the count the test asked for. Assertions that #512 had loosened to survive the clock, such as "the ticks plus what this tick counted" or "an origin of at least 8", are exact again. A wait whose condition never comes now takes the full bound to fail, which is the price of never failing a sound test. Whether a waiting `refute_receive` follows a synchronizing call cannot be read reliably from source, so the check does not try: a waiting refute is rewritten as a synchronizing call followed by `refute_received`, or listed with its reason. The check reads the code, not the strings in it: code a test evaluates on another node from a string is not checked, and the one such test counts ticks rather than waiting on them.

## 149. D-149 — Every inverse sine and cosine in Geo is d3's clamped one, and no point at the edge of a projection's domain raises

**Context.** #566. The rotation property of spec/07 §1.4 failed on some seeds for `:collignon` with `asin(-1.0000000000000004)`. It is not a rotation fault: `:collignon`'s inverse of its own south pole computes `α = 1.4142135623730951`, `α² = 2.0000000000000004`, and takes the arcsine of `1 − α²`. The property finds it only when a rotation carries its random point within about 10⁻⁸° of the south pole. Erlang's `:math.asin`, `:math.acos`, `:math.sqrt` and `:math.log` raise `ArithmeticError` outside their domains where JavaScript returns `NaN`, so d3-geo clamps its inverse trigonometry in one place (src/math.js) and d3-geo-projection its square root, and every projection calls those. Here, three modules had their own clamped `asin` (`Projection`'s rotation, `Circle`, `Clip.Circle`), two cylindrical inverses clamped inline, and the rest of the families called `:math.asin` and `:math.acos` raw. A survey of all 38 types at both poles and the antimeridian, ±1 ulp, with each projected point moved ±1 ulp, raised in nine: `:collignon`, `:albers` and `:conic_equal_area`, `:mollweide`, `:eckert2`, `:eckert6` and `:wagner4` (an arcsine past ±1), `:eckert3` (a square root of `−2.2e-16`), `:loximuthal` (the logarithm of zero at a pole) and `:mollweide`'s forward Newton step (a zero derivative at `−89.99999999999999°`). `PolygonContains` skipped a crossing whose normalized `z` drifted past ±1, where d3 counts it.

**Decision.**
- **One helper module, d3's definitions.** `Visualize.Geo.Math` holds `asin/1` (past `1` is `π/2`, past `−1` is `−π/2`) and `acos/1` (past `1` is `0`, past `−1` is `π`), as d3-geo's src/math.js, and `sqrt/1` (`0` at or below `0`), as d3-geo-projection's. Every inverse sine and cosine in `lib/visualize/geo/` goes through it, the local helpers are gone, and no family calls `:math.asin` or `:math.acos` directly. Clamping only where a survey found a failure was not taken: the sites that held were safe by an argument about rounding at each one, and that argument is easy to lose in a later edit.
- **The square root is clamped where its argument is zero at the edge and non-negative on the sphere** — Eckert III (`1 − 4φ²/π²`, both ways) and Hammer's inverse (`1 − x²/16 − y²/4`). The others stay raw: their arguments are bounded away from zero on the sphere.
- **A step outside the domain is clamped too, as in d3.** The clamp cannot tell a value one ulp past ±1 from one a whole unit past it, so `invert/3` of a point outside `:mollweide`'s ellipse or `:azimuthal_equal_area`'s disc returns a point, as d3's does, where it raised. The explicit `nil`s spec/07 §1.2 lists (`:orthographic` outside the unit disc, `:gnomonic` and `:stereographic` behind the horizon) stay: they are decisions about the domain, not rounding.
- **The two non-trigonometric failures follow d3's own guards.** `:loximuthal`'s inverse returns `lon = 0` within 10⁻⁶ radians of a pole, the same band its forward projection already maps to `x = 0`. `:mollweide`'s Newton step stops where its derivative is zero, as it already did at the pole itself.

**Consequences.** No point of the sphere, and no point of the plane within rounding of a projection's edge, raises `ArithmeticError` in `project/3`, `invert/3`, a rotation, a circle or the clip. Every value inside the domain is unchanged, so the projection and charts goldens and the d3 oracle cases (within 1e-9) are as they were. `test/visualize/geo/projection_edge_test.exs` is the recurrence guard.
