It drives every type at the poles, the antimeridian and ±1 ulp around them, with each projected point moved ±1 ulp, and it fails if any family calls `:math.asin` or `:math.acos` outside `Visualize.Geo.Math`. The rotation property also runs a fixed case at the south pole that used to fail. A future projection family that takes an inverse sine of a computed quantity is held to the same rule.

## 150. D-150 — A board is exported from a snapshot: one value of the board and its data that draws each cell as the cell does; the document embeds each cell's own SVG; print is a theme and a band layout, as SVG pages

**Context.** #547, from #531 item 11. A dashboard is shared and printed, not only watched. Three things were asked: a picture of a whole board (SVG and PNG), a frozen board that reopens without its feeds, and print. Four questions had to be settled. What does an export draw from, when a live board's frames are spread over one process per cell (D-146)? How does a document of many cells stay true to each cell? How does a frozen board relate to the bundle, which already carries a board to another host (D-147)? And what does print produce, when the rasterizer the library uses (D-121) makes PNG and not PDF?

**Decision.**
- **Every export draws a snapshot** (spec/15 §12.2). `%Visualize.Board.Snapshot{}` holds the board, its now and resolved window, the width, the theme, and for each cell its prepared component, its variables, the slots the board last handed it, and a crash's reason. A running board answers one with `Visualize.Board.Server.snapshot/1`, a call to the board process, which already holds every piece of it, so no cell is called and a slow cell holds nothing up. A board realized purely gives one with `Visualize.Board.Snapshot.realize/4`, whose faults are `realize/4`'s. Collecting the cells' last views from the subscribers was considered and not taken: views are pictures at one width in one theme, and print needs the same board at another width in another theme. A snapshot carries data, so it redraws at any width and in any theme.
- **A cell's drawing is one set of pure steps, shared by the cell process and the snapshot** (spec/15 §12.2). `Visualize.Board.Draw` places the rows, applies, compiles, steps and wraps the root, and decides the state. The cell process draws every frame through it, and `Snapshot.views/2` draws each cell once through it. A snapshot's cell is therefore the cell's own first frame of the same data, byte for byte, which a test holds against a running cell. A cell that eased between frames differs only by where its easing stood. The snapshot draws the data at rest.
- **The document embeds each cell's own SVG unchanged** (spec/15 §12.1). Each chart, banner and panel is the cell's own root, placed inside a viewport whose view box is that root's size, at the cell's box from `Visualize.Board.boxes/2`, below the header that `Visualize.Board.window_text/2` writes for the component too. Re-generating every chart into one shared tree was not taken. It would be a second drawing of each cell that could drift from the first. The chart's content-derived ids (a gradient's, a filter's, the root's) make embedding safe: two cells that share an id share its body.
- **PNG resolves the theme's references to their fallbacks** (spec/15 §12.1). A cell's SVG is in `:css` mode, and resvg reads no custom property. A theme writes each slot's literal as the fallback of its reference (spec/08 §7.3), so `Export.literal/1` replacing `var(--name, fallback)` by its fallback *is* the `:literal` drawing. Drawing every cell a second time in `:literal` mode would mean a literal path through the compiled step that the runtime never takes.
- **A bundle carries definitions; a snapshot carries data** (spec/15 §12.2). A bundle moves a board and the store entries it refers to into another store, to be re-keyed and edited. A snapshot carries each component already resolved, with its rows, refers to no id, needs no store and is never imported. It is versioned and read like a board (D-137): a `"$version"` stamp refused by name when newer, the `depth` and `items` limits before any key is decoded, then every key with each embedded document read by its own reader. Rows are written column by column, so a field name keeps its spelling as an atom or a string.
- **Print is a theme, a band layout and SVG pages** (spec/15 §12.4). `Visualize.Theme.print/0`, the built-in `:print`, is white paper, dark ink and the color-safe status ramp, since paper is often read in grayscale. `Export.paginate/2` arranges the board at the page's width, which reflows it as a narrow container does, and moves it down the pages in **bands**: cells whose rows overlap, the least that can move without cutting a cell. A band that does not fit starts the next page, and one taller than a page is scaled to fit, so no cell is split. `Export.pages/2` draws one SVG per page in the paper's unit, and `Export.html/2` puts them in one HTML document with an `@page` rule. **SVG pages, not a PDF.** resvg makes PNG and not PDF, no PDF writer is available without a native or large dependency, and every host that shows a board has a browser, whose print makes the multi-page PDF from the HTML.

**Consequences.** A host exports a running board in two calls (`Server.snapshot/1`, then `Export.svg/2`, `png/2` or `pages/2`), and a snapshot file reopens anywhere a matching library runs, with no store or feed. `Visualize.Board.Cell` now draws through `Visualize.Board.Draw`, unchanged in behavior. `Visualize.Theme.builtins/0` gains `:print`, so a design may name `theme: :print`, the builder offers it beside the other built-in themes, and a bundle does not carry it (§11.1). A snapshot holds rows, so its size is its data's: the `items` limit bounds each slot's rows on reading as it bounds any list, and a host that shares large snapshots raises it. A row value must be a term spec/14 §11.2 can write. A snapshot taken mid-easing shows the data at rest rather than the frame on screen. The examples' `/board` page posts to `/board/export/:format` for SVG, PNG, the snapshot and the printed pages.


## 151. D-151 — Interaction across a board's cells is the board's shared state: a brush is the board's window, debounced in the board; a selection is `{field, value}` through `links`, drawn as a per-datum `opacity`; a drill target is validated data that the page follows

**Context.** #545, from #531 items 8–10. A board's cells are separate charts in separate processes (D-146), and until now they shared hover and a brush band only on the client's sync bus. A brush already set the board's window (#575), but a click on a chart was a brush too small to keep and its clear restored the window, so a click could not mean anything else. Three things were open: how a selection is identified across cells over different feeds, how a cell draws it, and how a drill-down target is named and who follows it.

**Decision.**
- **The window is the board's, and rapid brushes are debounced in the board process** ([15-boards](15-boards.md) §13.1). `Server.brush/2` validates at once and applies after `debounce:` (250 ms), the latest winning, so a burst of brushes costs each feed one fetch. Debouncing on the client was considered: the hook pushes only on release, so the burst is of releases, and only the board sees all of them, from every viewer. Undo is `reset_window/1`, the window the board was started with, behind a *Reset window* button shown while `moved`. A cleared brush no longer restores the window, because a click is a cleared brush.
- **A selection is `{field, value}`, read through a board-level `links` map** (§13.2). A series key alone would not cross feeds, whose series are named by their own fields. A field and a value do, and the schema's meaning makes them comparable (D-136). Where two feeds name one thing differently, `links: %{name: :service}` says so once, for the board. A link is one step. The selection is held canonically, under the linked name, and each cell is sent every field that links to it.
- **A pick is resolved to its row in the cell process, from the keyboard's points.** The client names a point as `{series, index}`, the keyboard hook's own coordinates (D-142), and the cell answers the typed row, so a drill variable is a `DateTime` or an atom and never an attribute string. A click picks by the crosshair's snapping. The identity is the mark's series field, else the first `:category` field of its slot's schema. Writing datum attributes onto every element was not taken: only marks with a tooltip write them, and they are strings.
- **The highlight is the style grammar's `opacity`, bound per datum** (spec/14 §3.2). A cell that shows the selection adds a `:"$vis_dim"` column to its rows (`nil` for the selected, `0.25` otherwise) and appends `%{opacity: {:field, :"$vis_dim"}}` to each data mark's style stack. That is the grammar's own channel binding, so it needs no hook, draws on every target, and leaves a cell that does not show the selection byte for byte as it was. A client-side class toggle, as the legend hides a series, was considered: it needs the elements to carry the identity, which a category bar without a tooltip does not. #638 keeps the mechanism and replaces the column: the rows carry a view state, `:selected` or `:muted`, laid from the style palette through a scale in the stack (D-171).
- **A drill target is stored data, validated against the target board's declared variables, and followed by the page** (§13.3). `drill: %{board: ref, vars: %{var => {:datum, field} | value}}` names `:self` or a host's name for a board, since boards are not store entries. `validate/3` takes `boards:`, and the runtime refuses a board whose drill cannot be met. The component sends the page `{:visualize_board_drill, id, target}` rather than calling `push_navigate`. The library holds no route, and the host may follow a drill by a URL, a modal, or new variables on a board only one person sees.

**Consequences.** A board value gains two keys, `links` and a cell's `drill`, additive keys that an older reader refuses as unknown (D-137). The JSON form gains a board-only tag, `{"$datum": field}`. The runtime gains `brush/2`, `reset_window/1`, `select/2` and `pick/4`, two options (`boards:`, `debounce:`) and two fields of its info (`selection`, `moved`). `KeyboardHook` gains a pick event and a click, and `BoardHook` gains Escape for the selection. A selection is shared by every viewer of a board process, as its window is. A transform that derives rows without the dim column leaves its mark undimmed. The examples' board gains three cells that show all three interactions and a detail board, which the page starts per visitor.

## 152. D-152 — A board is a library kind the builder edits in a board mode; the server places every cell, the preview realizes each cell alone over sample data, and the binding is the host's, emitted and never stored

**Context.** #544, from #531 §1. A board (D-141) had a value, a runtime (D-146) and a bundle (D-147), but no tool: a host wrote one in Elixir. The builder is the tool for designs and, since D-139, for themes, and #568 split it so that a mode is one module. Five questions were open: where a board is kept while a person builds it, who decides where a dragged cell lands, how the builder learns the feeds a host can bind, where the binding a person makes goes, and what the preview draws. D-147 had said that a board is not a library entry, because no store kept boards then.

**Decision.**
- **A board is a kind of fragment, `:board`, kept in the builder's store.** The entry is the singular wrapper `%{board: map}` holding the board's map form, `version` included, so a board is versioned by its own `version`, as a theme document is (D-139), and read back by `Visualize.Board.from_map/2` under D-137's gate and limits. Per-kind validity is that reader, so *Save to library* cannot store a board that does not open again. `Fragment.to_json/1` writes the body in the board's own JSON form and `from_json/2` reads it back, so a store that keeps JSON keeps boards too. Because the cells' refs are ids inside the fragment, `export/3` of a board entry carries its components and an import re-keys them with no new code. This narrows D-147's "a board is not a library entry": `import_board/3` still returns the board rather than putting it, and a host that keeps boards in its store puts it there. Keeping boards outside the store, in an assign the host owns, was considered and not taken: the builder would then need a second library, a second save path and a second notion of identity for one more kind of document.
- **The board mode is a mode, and the chart holds the board, not a stack.** `Builder.BoardMode` is one entry of `Builder.Modes`, entered by opening a board entry or *New → board*. A board is not a design node, so the theme mode's way of writing a site of the stack does not fit; the board chart carries the `%Visualize.Board{}` itself, and every edit validates it alone before it is written, so the mode never holds a board that would not read.
- **The hook moves, and the server places.** `BuilderHook` in scope `board` turns a pointer's travel into squares and shows the rectangle as it goes, but it pushes the rectangle and nothing else. `Boards.place/3` writes it and validates the board, so an overlap, a cell off the grid or a rectangle that is not integers is refused with the board's own reasons, whatever a client sends. A new cell takes the first free place in reading order and is named `cell_<n>`, because a cell's name is its identity and the builder makes no atom of a person's text. Variable names are matched against the names the components declare, for the same reason.
- **The preview realizes each cell alone, over sample data.** Each cell is `Board.realize/4` of a board of that one cell, so the component, the cascade, the group and the zone are prepared exactly as the runtime prepares them, and one cell that cannot apply does not blank the others. The data is the builder's sample pool: by the slot's own name until a binding is made, and through the binding (`Binding.pools/2`) after. A live preview through `Board.Server` and the board component was not taken: it needs feeds the builder does not hold and a process per open board that the builder would have to own.
- **The host gives a connector catalog, and the binding is the host's.** The builder takes `catalog`, a map from each feed the host offers to its schema, which is what `describe` answers (spec/08 §10.3), and offers those feeds for each cell's slots. It shows every unbound slot and every unmet contract with `Binding.check/3`, so a person sees before any data flows what the runtime would report. With no catalog, the bindable feeds are the sample pool. The binding never enters the board: each change is sent to the host as `{on_bind, id, board_id, binding}`, and a host may seed one per board with `board_bindings`. This is D-141's and D-147's rule that a binding names one host's feeds, so it travels with the host and not with the board. A catalog feed previews over the host's sample rows of that name, else rows made from its schema, so the builder never fetches from a host's feed.
- **Edit component opens the component and returns.** The cell's composite opens as a chart of the workspace, as from the library, so saving it writes the entry every cell that uses it reads. *Back to board* closes that chart only when it holds no unsaved change, so returning never drops work.

**Consequences.** A board is made, placed, bound, previewed and saved without writing Elixir. `:board` joins `Fragment.kinds/0`, so `{:board, n}` ids exist, the library's kind view always shows a *Board* heading, and *New* offers `board`. A board entry dropped into a stack is refused, as a wrong kind is. The preview is as good as the sample data: a catalog feed with no sample rows previews over rows made from its schema, which are shaped like the feed's but are not its values. The builder emits a binding and does not check it again when the host stores it. A host that runs the board passes its own binding to `Board.Server.start/2`, which refuses unknown feeds as before.

## 153. D-153 — A bound field's schema type is checked against its scale's need before anything is drawn: in application, in the board's binding check and in the builder's previews, which draw a fault panel instead of raising

**Context.** #582, found in #580. Binding a time feed to a chart whose x scale is linear raised from the frame inside the builder's board preview, and took the builder page down. The single-chart preview behaved the same way, since spec/14 §18.4 said a fault the frame raises raises there too. The rule is right for a host's data: catching it would hide a bug. But a builder whose purpose is choosing bindings meets wrong choices all the time. D-136 already gave a feed's fields a `type`, and every scale declares a kind, so a wrong binding can be seen from the two descriptions before any value reaches a scale.

**Decision.**
- **Each field a mark reads through a scale has a need, read from the scale's kind.** A time scale needs `:time`. Linear, log, power, sqrt, symlog, radial, quantize, quantile, threshold, sequential and diverging scales need `:number`. Band and ordinal scales need `:category`, which a `:text` field also satisfies, since both are discrete. A read is a channel that names a scale, a style's `{:field, f}` color or an offset's field, with the mark's `scales` renames and a scale's adoption followed. It counts only where the mark's rows are its slot's own, or pass through steps that keep every field as it is (`:filter`, `:sort`, `:take`, `:window`, `:lttb`, `:m4`). A field whose type does not satisfy its need is `{:type_mismatch, slot, field, expected, given}` at the read's path.
- **A schema's type is meaning, and a rows' type is representation.** A schema's `:time` names the time field however the feed writes it. `Feed.Signals` describes its integer tick as `:time`, and a linear scale draws it, so a numeric scale takes a schema's `:time`. Rows typed by `Data.Table.column_types/1` say how values are written: `:time` there is instants, which no numeric scale takes, and a band or ordinal scale takes any value. Treating a schema's `:time` on a linear scale as a fault, as #582 first proposed, was not taken. It would refuse every tick feed on a linear axis, and a time scale cannot take ticks, so such a feed could not be drawn on a board at all. The case #582 found is caught where it happens: the builder's samples write a `:time` field as instants, and the previews check the rows they draw.
- **Application checks it where a schema is bound.** The schema step (D-136) resolves the schema of a slot with reads as well as of a slot with a contract. For a slot without a contract, a schema that does not resolve is not a fault, because nothing requires it, and that slot's types go unchecked. So a host that sends rows, or deliveries with no describe, sees no new fault. Rows of the wrong type with no schema still raise from the frame, as before. Only a schema makes the check possible, and no path rescues.
- **The board's binding check reports it.** `Board.required_sources/3` gives each slot its `reads`, and `Binding.check/3` checks them against each described feed. `Binding.check_types/3`, and `Chart.check_types/2` for one design, take a schema or a plain map of types. That is how the builder checks a sample that has no schema, by the types its rows show (`Data.Table.column_types/1`).
- **The builder's previews check first and draw a fault panel.** The single-chart preview runs `Chart.check_types/2` before `apply/2`, with each slot typed by its rows and, for a catalog feed, by its schema too. A board cell with a type fault is not realized, whether the fault comes from the binding's check against the catalog or from `Binding.check_types/3` against the preview's rows. Both draw the board runtime's cell-state panel, `Board.State.render/3` of a new state, `{:invalid, errors}`, with role `:bad` and words *Wrong binding* and the first fault, and they list every fault beneath it. The runtime draws the same state for a cell whose application reports a type mismatch, which it used to call *Feed down*, though the feed was up.
- **Rescuing the preview was not taken.** A rescue around `apply/2` would hide every bug the frame raises for, in the host's data and in the library alike. Checking what the preview can know first, and raising for everything else, keeps those bugs visible.

**Consequences.** spec/14 §18.4's "the preview raises" holds now only for faults no type predicts, such as a theme name nothing resolves. A wrong binding is a panel in the builder, a fault under its slot, and a deliberate state in the runtime. The need table is coarser than what a scale accepts. A `:number` field on a band scale draws, but it is reported, because a schema that calls a field a number says it measures something, and a band or an ordinal color reads categories. A host that means numbers as categories describes the field as `:category`. A slot without a contract now has its schema described when its delivery names one and its fields are read through a scale, so a describe function may be asked once more than before. A design read through steps that make fields, such as `:bin`, `:stack` or a layout, is not type-checked, and a wrong binding there still raises. At runtime a feed whose schema calls a field `:time` but whose rows are instants, bound to a linear scale, passes the schema check and still raises from the frame, as before. Only rows show that, and the runtime does not type its deliveries' rows.

## 154. D-154 — A `:time` field holds instants; a feed's windows are over its window field, a `:time` one or one it describes `window: true`; a schema's `:time` on a numeric scale is a type fault

**Context.** #585, found in #584 (D-153). `Visualize.Feed.Signals` (#537, D-140) described its integer tick column as `:time`, so that the window, the monitor and `Feed.Combine`, which found the field a window is over as the schema's one `:time` field, would work over ticks. That gave `:time` two meanings. It meant an instant, which is what a time scale needs and what every other feed writes. It also meant a tick count, which only a numeric scale can draw. D-153 had to let a schema's `:time` satisfy a numeric scale so that the tick feed could still be drawn. The cost was that a real feed whose `:time` field holds `DateTime`s, bound to a linear axis, passed validation and still raised from the frame. The data-to-axes milestone had already settled the question for designs (#369, spec/14 §2.3): a signal's time is its tick, an integer, and is typed `:number`, never `:time`. #537 diverged from that only because the feed contract had no other way to say which field a window is over.

**Decision.**
- **A `:time` field holds instants**: `DateTime`, `NaiveDateTime` or `Date` values, or ISO 8601 strings, which is what a time scale takes (spec/14 §4.3). It never holds numbers. A count of ticks, a sequence number or an offset is `:number`. `Feed.Signals` describes its tick as `%{type: :number, window: true, label: "Tick"}`.
- **A feed names the field its windows are over: its window field** (spec/08 §10.1, §11.2). It is a tenth description key, `window`, a boolean that a `:time` or `:number` field may carry. The window field is the one field described `window: true`, or else the schema's one `:time` field when it has exactly one and does not describe it `window: false`. `Feed.window_field/1` answers it and replaces `Feed.time_field/1`. `Schema.new/1` refuses `window` on a `:category` or `:text` field (`{:invalid_for, type}`), a value that is not a boolean (`{:type, :boolean}`), and two fields that both say `true` (`{:duplicate_window, other}`).
  - The key lives in the schema because `finish/3` trims a feed that does **not** honor windows. So which field to trim by is a fact about the data, not a capability, and every reader of it (`finish/3`, the monitor, `Board.Rows`, the cell states, the conformance kit) already holds the schema.
  - It is a per-field key rather than a schema-level one, which keeps the schema a map of descriptions. The content hash (spec/08 §10.2) covers it with no change to the encoding, and no existing schema's version moves.
  - The default keeps the common case silent. A feed stamped with instants that has one `:time` field (telemetry, metresis) declares nothing.
- **The window works over either kind** (spec/08 §11.2). A window applies to a window field of its kind (`Feed.Window.applies?/2`): instant bounds to `:time`, number bounds to `:number`, and a window with no bound to either.
  - `finish/3` trims, and reduces against, a numeric window field as it does a time one. It never reduces the window field itself as a value.
  - The monitor asks each feed for the window that applies to it. That is the request, or `nil` (the feed's own span) when the request is of the other kind. A feed asked `nil` is neither trimmed by the window nor refetched when it moves.
  - Late and out-of-order rows are placed along a numeric window field as along a time one.
  - `Feed.Combine` routes over either kind. Its describe error for a schema with no window field is now `:combine_window_field`.
- **On a board, a feed over ticks declares its own window domain** (spec/15 §1.5, §8.5). A board's window is in UTC. It applies to the feeds whose window field is `:time`. A feed whose window field is a number keeps its own span and its own present, and a brush does not move it. Its `continuity` gaps are measured in its own units.
  - The alternative was mapping ticks to time through the board's clock, and it was not taken. Only the connector knows what a tick is worth, and the board does not guess a mapping. A connector that wants ticks on the board's clock maps them to instants and describes the result as `:time`, as the examples' wall-clock signal (`Examples.BoardDemo.Wave`) already does. That is why the `/board` demo is unchanged.
- **The type check is strict.** This updates D-153's second bullet and its last consequence. A schema's `:time` no longer satisfies a numeric need, so `Types.satisfies?/3` loses its one clause for it. A `:time` field read through a linear, log, power, sqrt, symlog, radial or any other numeric scale is `{:type_mismatch, slot, field, :number, :time}` in application, in the board's binding check and in the builder's previews.
  - The case D-153 left open, a feed whose `:time` field holds instants and is bound to a linear axis, is now reported before anything is drawn.
  - D-153's reason for leaving it open, that it "would refuse every tick feed on a linear axis", no longer holds, because a tick feed's tick is a `:number`.
  - The rest of D-153 stands.
- **The conformance kit checks the declaration** (spec/08 §12.3). The `:describe` check reports a feed that names `:window` but has no window field (`{:no_window_field, times}`).
  - The `:deliveries` check holds the window field's values to its type. That is an instant for `:time` and a number for `:number` (`{:window_value, request, value}`, which replaces `:not_a_time`).
  - Every other `:time` field must hold instants (`{:not_an_instant, request, field, value}`). A tick written under `:time` therefore fails the kit, not a frame.

**Consequences.**
- **For a feed author:**
  - Every `:time` field must hold instants.
  - A feed whose window, ordering or `continuity` is over a numeric field types that field `:number` and describes it `window: true`.
  - A feed with several `:time` fields marks the one its windows are over.
  - `Feed.time_field/1` is gone; call `Feed.window_field/1`.
  - `Combine`'s error is `:combine_window_field`.
  - The kit's `:not_a_time` finding is `:window_value`.
  - A feed stamped with `DateTime`s that has one `:time` field changes nothing.
- **Versions move.** `Feed.Signals`' schema has a new version, since its tick's description changed.
- **Designs that drew a tick feed:**
  - A design that bound a tick feed's `:time` field to a linear axis now binds a `:number` field and is unchanged.
  - A design that bound it to a time scale never drew, and still does not, now reported as a type fault.
- **The builder's sample rows** give a `:number` window field its row's place, so a tick feed's preview counts as a tick does.
- **Gaps are measured in the window field's units.** spec/15 §8.5's "a numeric clock is read in seconds" is now "in its own units", since `continuity` is measured along the window field.

## 155. D-155 — One units vocabulary for every host: UCUM grows to the units hosts log, codes convert exactly, quantities carry their facts and unit systems, and currency is a marked extension

**Context.** #589, accepted with modifications. It amends D-138 and leaves D-138's text as it stands. D-138 made units UCUM codes over a small table, with twelve quantities, and put conversion between unit systems out of scope. metresis kept a second vocabulary (`Metresis.Units`, its spec §4.4): fifteen quantity families keyed by display symbols, exact conversion within a family (°C↔°F included), per-viewer `metric | imperial | as_posted` display, bounds, aggregation exclusions and currency. Neither covered the other, and metresis's symbols are ambiguous where UCUM is not: `KB` is 1,000 or 1,024 bytes, `gal`, `pt` and `floz` do not say US or imperial, and `cal` hides several calories. The owner decided on 2026-10-12 that the vocabulary lives in visualize, the library, and that metresis uses it from there and deletes its copy (metresis#429). A shared vocabulary that cannot convert would leave metresis keeping its own, so D-138's "conversion is out of scope" no longer holds.

**Decision.**
- **UCUM stays the vocabulary, and its table grows to the units hosts log** (spec/05 §4.1). Each new atom is UCUM's, with UCUM's definition, checked against the UCUM essence table (version 2.2, 2024-06-17):
  - SI and derived: `l`, `L`, `t`, `ar`, `V`, `A`, `C`, `lx`, `mol`, `bar`, `deg`, `rad`.
  - Customary: `[in_i]`, `[ft_i]`, `[yd_i]`, `[mi_i]`, `[nmi_i]`, `[kn_i]`, `[acr_us]`, `[oz_av]`, `[lb_av]`, `[stone_av]`, `[foz_us]`, `[pt_us]`, `[gal_us]`, `[foz_br]`, `[pt_br]`, `[gal_br]`, `[psi]`, `m[Hg]`, `[in_i'Hg]`, `[HP]`.
  - Energy and ratios: `cal` (UCUM's thermochemical calorie), `[Cal]`, `[ppm]`, `[ppb]`.
  - Logarithmic: `B` and `B[SPL]`.
  - The prefixes `c`, `d`, `h`, `da`, `T`, `Ti` and `p` join the table, so `cm`, `dl`, `hPa`, `har`, `TBy`, `TiBy` and `dB` read.
  - Arcminutes and arcseconds are left out until a host needs them.
  - The grammar is D-138's. A symbol may now mix letters and a bracketed part, as UCUM's do (`mm[Hg]`, `dB[SPL]`), and it is still matched whole before it is split.
- **New dimensions follow UCUM's base units.** The plane angle has base `rad`, and `deg` is π/180 of it, so a factor is an exact rational times an integer power of π. Electric charge has base `C`, and luminous intensity sits under `lx`. The mole is UCUM's dimensionless 6.02214076 × 10²³.
- **A level is not a linear quantity.** UCUM gives `B` the dimension of its argument. visualize gives each logarithmic reference a level dimension of its own instead: `:level` for `B`, a bare ratio level, and `:sound_pressure_level` for `B[SPL]`. So `dB` converts to `B` (a decibel is a prefixed bel), and never to a linear unit or to a sound level. A bare `dB` is a dimensionless level ratio, not a sound level.
- **Aliases are a separate door, not part of `parse/1`.** `Visualize.Unit.parse_alias/1` reads the symbols people type (`°C`, `lb`, `mph`, `inHg`, `kWh`) as codes. It refuses an ambiguous symbol and names its choices, so `KB` gets "1,000 bytes is `kBy`, 1,024 is `KiBy`" and `gal` gets `[gal_us]` or `[gal_br]`. Its table wins over a UCUM reading of the same spelling, because a person who types `B` or `MB` means bytes and not bels. Only a spelling the table does not hold falls through to `parse/1`. A host never keeps its own alias table.
- **Conversion is in scope and exact.** It is computed from the codes' exact factors, so a value is rounded once.
  - The affine units `Cel`, `[degF]` and `K` convert with their offsets, standing alone.
  - Logarithmic units convert only to their own level.
  - Currency never converts, because an exchange rate is data and not a fact about a unit.
  - `convert/3` keeps D-138's contract of a number or `ArgumentError`, which the display scaling relies on. `conversion/2` answers `{:ok, {scale, offset}}` or a reason, so a host can migrate stored rows with one `value × scale + offset`, as metresis does.
- **Quantities carry the facts a host needs** (spec/05 §4.2).
  - Every quantity has a base unit, a `kind` (`:linear`, `:affine`, `:logarithmic` or `:cyclic`) and bounds.
  - **Bounds are a display hint, never validation.** They tell an axis where to anchor and whether to include zero. A value outside them is never refused, because differences are legitimately negative. A field's schema may override them with its own `bounds`, a new optional key of D-136's description.
  - **Additivity depends on the aggregation.** A quantity carries two physical defaults, `additive_across` (summing series at one instant) and `additive_over_time` (summing one series over time). A field's schema may state a `metric_kind` (`:gauge`, `:counter` or `:rate`), a new optional key of D-136's description, and that refines them. A gauge does not add over time. A counter's increments do, when the quantity adds at all. A rate integrates rather than adds over time. A host derives its aggregation exclusions from the two together.
  - The list grows to the union of the two vocabularies plus what hosts already log: `area`, `volume`, `mass`, `pace`, `angle`, `currency`, `electric_potential`, `electric_current`, `electric_charge`, `illuminance`, `sound_level`, `mass_concentration` and `amount_concentration`. A percent stays a `ratio` written in `%`.
- **Unit systems are `:metric`, `:us`, `:uk` and `:as_posted`**, because "imperial" is ambiguous in the way `gal` is. The UK uses stones, imperial pints and °C. A system picks the display family a quantity is shown in, at display time and never in storage. The default is `:as_posted`, which is D-138's display scaling: the data's unit, rescaled only within its own family. A preference that crosses dimensions is not a system switch. That covers mg/dL against mmol/L, which needs a molar mass, and pace against speed, which is a reciprocal. Such a preference stays a per-field choice.
- **Currency is a marked extension, `{iso4217:XXX}`.**
  - It is valid UCUM syntax, an annotation. visualize gives each code of a fixed ISO 4217 table, which carries minor units (JPY 0, BHD 3), a currency dimension of its own.
  - Mixing two codes is refused. The codes compose (`{iso4217:USD}/h`, `{iso4217:EUR}/kW/h`). They never convert.
  - The price per kilowatt-hour is `{iso4217:EUR}/kW/h` because UCUM's `/` is left-associative. #589 wrote `{iso4217:EUR}/kW.h`, which UCUM reads as euro-hours per kilowatt.
  - The spec says plainly that to any other UCUM reader `{iso4217:USD}` and `{iso4217:EUR}` are both the dimensionless `1`, and so equal. visualize never hands these codes to a UCUM consumer without that caveat.
- **Formats per quantity go through `Visualize.Format` with `locale:`.** These are currency by code and its minor units (`$1,240.00`, `¥1,240`), angle (`350°`), and the compound duration (`1h 24m`, `840ms`). The compound duration is for labels and tooltips only. An axis keeps D-138's single scaled unit (`1.4 h`), one unit per axis.
- **metresis's vocabulary is the acceptance test.** A parity table in visualize's tests lists every unit of metresis's spec §4.4, taken from a named metresis commit, with its code here. It checks that each is expressible. Then, as the work items land, it checks that each converts and formats as `Metresis.Units` does, and it names every difference and why. metresis deletes its copy only against that table.

**Consequences.**
- A host stops keeping a unit table: metresis's ingest, profiles and forms call `parse_alias/1`, its migrations `conversion/2`, and its views `display_unit/3`.
- `B`, `kB`, `MB` and `pt` are now valid codes: the bel, the kilobel, the megabel and the picotonne. That is UCUM's reading, and it is why a person's input goes through `parse_alias/1`, which reads them as bytes or refuses them.
- `factor/1` answers a three-tuple for a code that holds π.
- A design or schema that names none of the new quantities, keys or units draws as before.
- Where UCUM's definition differs from metresis's (mm Hg, inch of mercury), visualize keeps UCUM's and the parity table records the difference.

## 156. D-156 — An axis shown in another unit picks its ticks in that unit, and the unit system is a render option

**Context.** #594, found in #589 (work items #591 and #593). D-138 scales an axis into the member of its family that fits the domain, and D-155 added unit systems, which show a measure in another family altogether. Both changed only the *labels*: the ticks were still round numbers in the data's unit, each converted for its label, so 0–40 `kg` under `:us` read `0 lb`, `22.046226 lb`, `44.092452 lb`, … and 0–100 `s` in minutes read `0.166667 min`, `0.333333 min`, …. Unit systems are #589's headline feature, for metresis's per-viewer display, and they read badly that way. Evaluation found a second gap: nothing carried a system to an axis. The frame's furniture asked `Quantity.formatter/3` and `display_unit/2` with no `system:`, so a design could not be shown in `:us` at all.

**Decision.**
- **The ticks are chosen in the display unit.** `Visualize.Quantity.ticks/3` takes the scale's domain converted into the display unit, takes `Visualize.Scale.ticks/2` of that at the axis's count, and converts each tick back into the data's unit with `Unit.convert/3` for placement. The scale itself is not touched: its domain is the data's and is not niced again in the display unit. `Quantity.formatter/3`, given the same count and system, labels each placed value with the round number it came from, never with the value converted back again, so the round trip's last bit (`19.999999999999996`) never reaches a label.
- **What still wins.** Explicit tick values win. An explicit `format` keeps the scale's own ticks in the data's unit, because its labels are in that unit. An explicit count only sets the count. Log and symlog scales keep their powers of ten, each tick in its own unit, as D-138 wrote them. A currency never converts, so its ticks are the scale's.
- **The unit system is a render option, never a design key.** Which system to show is the viewer's preference, like the size of the container (spec/14 §4.2), not a fact of the chart. So `system:` is an option of `Chart.apply/2` and `Chart.compile/2`, default `:as_posted`. The realized frame carries it, and a compiled chart keeps it for each realization per tick. Every axis's display unit, ticks, format and title decision read it, and so does the text alternative. The schema gets no key for it, so a stored design can never pin one.
- **Boards pass it through.** `Board.realize/4`, `Board.Server` (to every cell) and `Board.Snapshot.views/2` take `system:`, and so do `Board.Export.svg/2` and `pages/2` through `views/2`. A snapshot does not record it: a reader picks it when drawing, as they pick a theme for paper.

**Consequences.**
- An axis that shows another unit now has different tick positions as well as different labels. A chart rendered without `system:` and on a domain whose display unit is its own unit draws byte for byte as before.
- A host with viewers of several systems renders per viewer. For a board that means a board server per system. A board server draws once for all its subscribers, so the system is per board.
- An axis's ticks no longer always land on the scale's own `ticks/2`. Code that read `Scale.ticks/2` to find an axis's tick positions should read the axis's own tick values, as the grid does.

## 157. D-157 — A feed schema's unit and quantity are checked against the vocabulary

**Context.** #595. D-136 gave a feed's schema `unit` and `quantity` as loose seams: any non-empty string was a unit and any atom a quantity, to be tightened once the vocabulary existed. #589 (D-155) provides it: `Visualize.Unit` reads UCUM codes, the currency extension included, and `Visualize.Quantity` has the fixed list. A schema could still name a misspelled unit or an unknown quantity. That failed later, when an axis resolved the measure and raised, or never, when nothing drew the field.

**Decision.**
- **`Schema.new/1` checks the measure where the feed describes itself**, as the validator checks an axis's measure (spec/14 §10.1):
  - `unit` must be a code `Unit.parse/1` reads, else `{:unit, code, reason}` at `[field, :unit]`;
  - `quantity` must be one of `Quantity.all/0`, else `{:unknown_quantity, quantity}` at `[field, :quantity]`;
  - a field giving both, each good, is checked through `Quantity.resolve/1`, and a unit whose dimension is not its quantity's is `{:quantity, quantity, code}` at `[field, :quantity]`.
  The reasons are `Quantity.resolve/1`'s own, so a schema and a design say the same thing about the same mistake. The shape checks stay first: a non-string unit is still `{:type, :unit}` and a non-atom quantity `{:type, :name}`.
- **Codes, not symbols.** A schema holds a code. A provider that reads units from people or from another system maps them first, through `Unit.parse_alias/1` or `Unit.from_telemetry/1`, as Telemetry's feed already does.
- **The conformance kit names the field.** A measure fault is the finding `{:measure, field, reason}`, one per fault, and no longer part of `{:schema_invalid, errors}`, which keeps the schema's other faults. A provider learns which field's measure is wrong without reading a list of schema errors.

**Consequences.**
- A schema that named a unit visualize does not read, or a quantity outside the list, is now refused. That includes a symbol such as `"KB"` or `"°C"`. Such a schema could not draw its measure on an axis before either, since resolving the measure raised.
- `Visualize.Feed.Signals`, `Visualize.Feed.Telemetry` and every example's schema already pass, and the tests hold them to it.
- The seam comment at `Schema.unit/2` is gone. The unit check is one call to `Unit.parse/1`.

## 158. D-158 — A unit system picks a pressure's unit within its convention

**Context.** #598, found by metresis#433 while moving metresis's units onto visualize's vocabulary. D-155's unit systems show a quantity in the family its table names for the system, and `:pressure` had one table: `Pa`, `kPa`, `MPa` for `:metric` and `[psi]` for `:us` and `:uk`. So a catalog's barometric `hPa` became `[psi]` for a US viewer, `mbar` became `kPa`, and a blood pressure in `mm[Hg]` became `kPa` or `[psi]`, a number no clinician reads. Pressure is one dimension read in several conventions, and a viewer's system chooses within a convention, not across them.

**Decision.**
- **Conventions are a fact of the quantity table.** A quantity holds its display families per convention, as data in `Visualize.Quantity.Facts`: each convention has its system-tagged ladders and the codes it claims, its ladders' members and any it holds without a ladder. Every quantity but `:pressure` has one convention, its former flat list, so none of them changes.
- **`:pressure` has three** (spec/05 §4.2): **gauge** — `metric: Pa kPa MPa`, `us: [psi]`, `uk: [psi]`, also claiming `bar` — first, so that a unit of no convention keeps today's display; **barometric** — `metric: hPa`, `us: [in_i'Hg]`, `uk: hPa`, also claiming `mbar`; and **clinical** — `mm[Hg]` in every system, so no system moves it.
- **Exact code first, then shape.** `hPa`, `Pa` and `kPa` are one shape, as are `mbar` and `bar`, so the shape that picks a family (D-138) cannot pick a convention. A unit's convention is the first that claims its exact code, else the first claiming a code of its shape (`dPa` is gauge), else the first. Then the system picks within it as before, and `:as_posted` keeps the unit's own ladder inside its convention.
- **`bar` stays out of the metric ladder.** It is a gauge unit, but adding it would turn 300 kPa into `3 bar` for every metric viewer, which nothing here argues for. A stored `bar` is still shown in `bar` under `:as_posted`.

**Rejected.**
- Splitting pressure into a quantity per use (blood pressure, barometric pressure): they measure one dimension and convert, and a quantity per use is an enum of uses.
- Leaving it to each host: that is the duplicate table #589 removed.
- Extending conventions to energy (food `[Cal]` against `kW.h`) and volume now: a follow-up when a host shows a real case.

**Consequences.**
- Under `:us`, `hPa` and `mbar` are shown in `[in_i'Hg]` where they were `[psi]`; under `:metric`, `mbar` in `hPa` where it was `kPa`, and `[in_i'Hg]` in `hPa`; `mm[Hg]` keeps its unit in every system. Gauge units (`Pa`, `kPa`, `MPa`, `[psi]`) display as before.
- Under `:as_posted`, `hPa` stays `hPa` at every magnitude; it was rescaled into `kPa` with `Pa` and `MPa`.
- `family/2` and `family/3` of a pressure answer its convention's ladder. Every other quantity's answers are unchanged, and the tests hold them to origin's.
- Shipping it to metresis needs a visualize patch release.

## 159. D-159 — Combine takes compatible schemas, and the first feed's schema wins whole

**Context.** #605, from metresis#440. D-140's `Visualize.Feed.Combine` required every feed of a combination to describe the same schema, one content hash, and left making two catalogs agree to the connector. The case it was written for, a process's live `:telemetry` feed in front of a store's history of the same metric, can never meet that rule. The two describe one metric in different words: the field names are atoms in one and strings in the other, as are the rows' keys; the labels come from different sources; and the store knows facts the process does not (`polarity`, `bounds`, `metric_kind`). No connector can make the versions equal without throwing away what the store knows, and binding the two feeds to separate slots draws two series where there is one.

**Decision.**
- **Compatibility, per field of the first schema** (spec/08 §10.1). `Visualize.Data.Schema.compatibility(schema, other)` is `:ok` when, for each field of `schema`, `other` has a field of the same name compared as strings, of the same `type`, and for a `:number` field a unit `Visualize.Unit.conversion/2` converts into `schema`'s. Two absent units are compatible; one absent facing a present one is not, since its dimension is unknown; two currencies are not, since conversion refuses them. Otherwise it is `{:error, [{field, reason}]}` with every fault, the reasons `:missing`, `{:type, a, b}` and `{:unit, a, b}`. Nothing else is compared: labels and facts are what each feed knows. It is public, so a connector can check before it combines.
- **The first feed's schema wins whole** (spec/08 §11.5). The combination's `{version, schema}` is the first (freshest) feed's: its version, labels and facts, nothing merged from the later feeds.
- **Later rows are read in the first's words.** Before routing and the seam trim, a later feed's rows are re-keyed to the first schema's spellings and their numbers converted exactly into its units through `Unit.convert/3`; columns only the later schema describes are dropped, and columns no schema describes are left alone. A `:number` window field in different units has the window converted on the way to the later feed and its served `window` on the way back. The first feed's rows, and those of a later feed of the first's version, are never touched.
- **The window field is the first's.** Routing asks each later feed for a window over its own window field (`Feed.window_field/1`), so a later feed whose window field, compared as a string, is not the first's would be fetched over the wrong field and answer silently wrong rows. `describe/1` refuses it as `{position, first_window_field, {:window, first, later}}`, `later` `nil` for a feed with no window field. It is the combination's rule, not `Schema.compatibility/2`'s, which compares field by field and knows nothing of routing.
- **Errors list everything.** `describe/1` answers `{:error, {:combine_schema, [{position, field, reason}]}}`, `position` the later feed's index in the list, with every incompatibility of every later feed, reasons `:missing`, `{:type, a, b}`, `{:unit, a, b}` and `{:window, a, b}`, so a connector fixes its mapping in one pass. This replaces `{:combine_schema, versions}`, which said only that the versions differed.

**Rejected.**
- **Merging the later feeds' facts into the combination's schema.** A merged schema needs a version of its own, but a push feed sends its own `{:schema, version, schema}` and deliveries, and `Combine.subscribe/2` passes the first feed's pushes straight to the monitor, so the monitor would see two schemas for one slot. Fixing that needs a relay process per subscription, a heavier change than the case argues for. Getting a store's facts into the live feed's schema is the live feed's question: `Feed.Telemetry` reading them from a definition's options is its own proposal.
- **Requiring equal versions and leaving the mapping to the connector.** That was D-140's rule, and the case it was written for cannot meet it without the store's facts being thrown away.
- **Comparing every attribute.** A label or a fact differing is the point of the case, not a fault; only what changes how a row reads (name, type, unit) is compared.

**Consequences.**
- A live `Feed.Telemetry` feed and a store's history of the same metric combine in one slot, the history's rows coming back in the telemetry schema's names and units, `s` converted into `ms`.
- A combination that was refused for differing versions now combines whenever the schemas are compatible; the error a refusal carries has a new shape, which is a breaking change for code that matched `{:combine_schema, versions}`.
- The combination's schema has only the first feed's facts. A store's `polarity` or `bounds` reaches a chart only when the live feed describes it too.

## 160. D-160 — Feed.Telemetry's schema carries a definition's facts

**Context.** #608, found evaluating #605. D-159 makes a `Visualize.Feed.Combine` describe itself with its first (freshest) feed's schema whole, since its pushes name that schema's version. When an in-process `Feed.Telemetry` feed sits in front of a store's history of the same metric, the facts only the store knows — `polarity`, `bounds`, `metric_kind` — never reach the chart: the live feed's schema had a label, a unit and a quantity, and nothing else. The application already describes its metric in one place, the `Telemetry.Metrics` definition, which `Feed.Telemetry` reads for its `unit`, `description` and `tags`.

**Decision.**
- **`metric_kind` is inferred** for the `value` field from the definition's struct (spec/08 §11.6): `Counter` and `Sum` are `:counter`, since their rows are running totals; `LastValue`, `Summary` and `Distribution` are `:gauge`, since each row is a measurement.
- **Facts come from `reporter_options[:visualize]`**, Telemetry.Metrics' place for a reporter's own options, read by `Map.get` like every other field, so the library still takes no dependency on `telemetry_metrics`. The keys are exactly `polarity`, `bounds`, `target` and `metric_kind`, all facts of `value`; an explicit `metric_kind` overrides the inference. Any other key is refused rather than ignored, as `{[:value, key], :unknown_key}`: a misspelled `polarty` silently dropped would be a fact the author believes is shown. The label stays the definition's `description` and the unit its `unit`, so neither has two sources.
- **Validation is the schema's.** The schema is built with `Schema.new/1`, and any fault makes `init/1` answer `{:stop, {:telemetry_facts, metric, errors}}`, the metric's name as `feeds/1` keys it and §10.1's faults, so `start_link/1` fails naming the metric. No rescue: `new!/1` is not used for a schema the definition's options shape.

**Rejected.**
- **Ignoring unknown keys.** It hides typos and makes an option that does nothing look accepted.
- **Overriding `label` or `unit` from the options.** The definition already has a place for each; two sources for one fact need a precedence rule nobody asked for.
- **Facts for the tag fields (`order`, `status`).** A possible follow-up; the case is the `value` a store knows facts of.
- **Merging a later feed's facts in `Combine`.** Rejected in D-159; the live feed carrying them is this decision.

**Consequences.**
- Every `Feed.Telemetry` schema now has a `metric_kind` on `value`, so every such schema's version changes; a consumer that pinned one sees a new version.
- A connector that knows a store's facts puts them on the definition, and a combination of the live feed in front of the store's history shows them.
- A definition with an invalid or unknown fact stops the collector at startup with the metric named, rather than serving a schema that lies.

## 161. D-161 — Board cells carry mark tooltips and click events, and a host may own the brush

**Context.** #611, from metresis#443. metresis wants its dashboards on the live board runtime (`Visualize.Board.Server`, `Visualize.Components.Board`), and its charts depend on two interactions a board's cells did not offer. A mark's `tooltip` node (spec/14 §5.7) is how it names an event marker on hover and opens it on a click; a cell's SVG carried the `data-tooltip-*` attributes, but no `TooltipHook` was mounted, and a click had nowhere to go but the page's own `handle_event`, which a board's page does not hold (spec/15 §9.2). And metresis keeps a brushed window in the page's URL, so a link reproduces the view; with the brush turned straight into `Server.brush/2`, the board's window and the URL drift apart.

**Decision.**
- **A cell mounts `TooltipHook` around its SVG**, innermost of its hook elements, with `data-tooltip-target` naming the cell's component (spec/15 §9.1). The element carries no sync group; the crosshair already shares the board's hover.
- **`TooltipHook` takes `data-tooltip-target`** (spec/10 §12.3), mirroring `data-keyboard-target` and `data-brush-target`: set, a `data-tooltip-event` click goes to that component with `pushEventTo`; unset, to the LiveView as before. It is read at each click.
- **The cell forwards a mark's click to the page** as `{:visualize_board_event, id, %{cell: cell, event: event, fields: fields}}`, a parallel of the drill message (spec/15 §9.2). Every event the cell receives other than its own `pick` is a mark's; `pick` is reserved. `fields` are the strings the hook read.
- **`Visualize.Components.Board` takes `brush: :server | :host`**, default `:server`, anything else an `ArgumentError` at update. Under `:host` a brush sends the page `{:visualize_board_brush, id, %{domain: [from, to]}}`, the ends as UTC `DateTime`s, instead of calling `Server.brush/2`, and the header's *Reset window* sends `%{domain: nil}` instead of calling `Server.reset_window/1`. The page applies either with `Server.window/2`: the domain as `%{from, to}`, `nil` as its own default window. The header still shows the board process's window and `moved`, so it follows what the page applied.
- **The debounce is the applier's.** The hook pushes once per gesture, at the drag's end. Under `:host` the board's debounce is bypassed, since `window/2` is never debounced; a page that wants rapid re-brushes coalesced applies them with `Server.brush/2`, which takes the same window.

**Rejected.**
- **`brush: {:host, event}`**, the proposal's first shape. The message is fixed, so the event name in the tuple would carry nothing.
- **Routing a tooltip click to the page directly.** The page holds no event of the board's (spec/15 §9.2), the click would arrive without its cell, and two boards on a page could not be told apart.
- **A callback assign** for either message. A function in the assigns, where a message is how a LiveView already talks to its page, as D-151 decided for drills.
- **Leaving *Reset window* on the board process under `:host`.** The board would move back while the URL kept the brushed window.

**Consequences.**
- A design's tooltips show in a board's cells, and a board's page can open what a datum names.
- A page can keep a board's window in its URL; the board stays the one place the window is held and drawn from.
- A click on a datum with an event also picks its nearest point, so it may select or drill as well; the two are independent.
- Existing boards are unchanged: `:server` is the default, and a mark without an event pushes nothing.

## 162. D-162 — The builder's graph is the box the design is drawn at

**Context.** #386, work item #613. A design records no size (spec/14 §4.2, #427): a frame's `box` is a fraction of whatever box the host's container gives at render. The builder's preview kept drawing at the render's default 600×400 and classified a drop against one frame's margins at that size, so the person laying a chart out saw neither the size it would be shown at nor where a frame's margins really fell. Worse, the preview matched a frame named `main`, so a design whose frames were `history` and `now` — the gallery's *Load* and *Context* templates — raised in render and crashed the builder. `ResizeHook` already measures a container, but pushes only to the LiveView, and the graph element already carries `BuilderHook`; an element carries one hook.

**Decision.**
- **The chart sits in a box inside the graph that hosts `ResizeHook`.** Its measured size is the builder's `graph_size`, and the preview applies the design with `size:` at it; before a measurement, the render's default. A measurement under 100 pixels on a side keeps the last one.
- **`ResizeHook` gains `data-resize-target`**: present, it pushes with `pushEventTo`, so a component can host it. The same contract `BuilderHook` has with `data-builder-target`.
- **The box is sized by its column, the chart by the box.** The `<svg>` fills the box by the stylesheet, so the measurement never depends on what was drawn (the loop #434 met on the chart page).
- **Every frame is drawn**, and the `<svg>` carries `data-builder-frames`, each frame's pixel box and margins in drawing order. A point is read in the topmost frame whose box holds it, else the nearest — by the hook for the region it shows, by `Place.spot/3` on the server for what the drop means.
- **A cell resolves in one place, made public**: `Visualize.Chart.Frame.box_of/3`, which `Frame.new/2` and the builder both read.

**Rejected.**
- **A `ResizeObserver` inside `BuilderHook`'s graph scope.** It would duplicate `ResizeHook`'s settle and its motion preference, and a second implementation of "measure, settle, push once" is a second place for it to be wrong.
- **Taking the width and keeping an aspect, as the chart page does (#434).** The page's chart sizes its own container, so its height cannot be measured; the builder's graph is a column of the workspace whose height the shell fixes, so both sides are real measurements and the design is shown at exactly what the box is.
- **Asking the hook which frame a drop is in and trusting it.** The server knows the design and the measured size, and computes the frame itself; the hook's frame reading is for what it draws while the pointer moves.

**Consequences.**
- Opening a multi-frame template no longer crashes the builder; the graph shows every frame at the graph's size, and a window resize redraws at the new size with the same fractions.
- A drop near a frame's own margin is that frame's band, not the whole graph's.
- `data-builder-frame` (one frame's `w h top right bottom left`) is replaced by `data-builder-frames`; `Place.node/4` and `Place.moved/5` take a spot as well as a bare point, which keeps their single-frame readings.

## 163. D-163 — A frame is placed by dragging it on the graph; the hook snaps, the server checks

**Context.** #386, work item #614. A frame's `box` is fractions of the box the render is given (spec/14 §4.2), and once the graph is that box (D-162) a person should place frames the way they see them, never by typing four numbers. The builder already had two pointer gestures to learn from: a drawn node moved between regions (#277), whose meaning the server decides from a point, and a board cell dragged by squares (§22.3), where the hook shows the rectangle and the server accepts or refuses it. A frame is neither: its placement is continuous, and a person lining two frames up wants them to meet exactly, which pixels from a pointer never do.

**Decision.**
- **The open frame wears an outline and four corner handles**, drawn by the server over the chart; only the handles take pointer events, so the chart's own nodes stay clickable under it. A frame is selected by a click on its empty space, the hook picking the topmost frame under the point, since a `<g>` has no area of its own.
- **The hook snaps while the pointer moves**: an edge within 8 pixels of 0, ⅓, ½, ⅔, 1 or another frame's edge lands on it. Snapping is feedback — the outline must show where the frame will land — so it lives where the pointer is.
- **The server checks and writes**: `frame_box` carries the box as fractions; the server keeps it inside the whole, each side at least 0.05, rounds to four places and writes `box` through the ownership rule, a locked owner refusing. It never re-snaps: the snapped number is the person's choice, and a second snapping rule on the server would be a second place for the two to disagree.
- **A box replaces a cell**: dragging a frame that said `cell` and `span` writes `box` and takes the cell away, since both is a fault. (Dragging *between cells* is the layout's own gesture, a later work item.)
- **The keyboard has the gesture**: arrows move the open frame a twelfth of the chart, Shift with an arrow stretches its right or bottom edge a twelfth — a twelfth lands on halves, thirds and quarters.
- **The frame's page shows its placement as a line, `z` and `background` as fields** (*place*), so what the drag wrote is readable, and the two keys a drag cannot express are editable.

**Rejected.**
- **Snapping on the server.** The outline would show one place and the frame land in another.
- **Four number boxes for the box.** The proposal's acceptance is that a person never types a size; the line says what the box is, the keyboard twin reaches it without a pointer.
- **Handles drawn by the hook.** They must survive the re-render every edit causes; drawn by the server they are part of the markup the patch keeps.

**Consequences.**
- Two frames are laid side by side, or one inset over another, by dragging; their edges meet exactly at thirds, halves and each other.
- A stored design records boxes like `[0.3333, 0, 0.3333, 1]`: four places, the precision of a pixel on a 10 000-pixel box.
- A frame placed on a layout's cell loses its cell when dragged freely, until the layout gesture lands.

## 164. D-164 — In a design of several frames, what is placed belongs to the frame it was dropped on

**Context.** #386, work item #615. The builder addressed "the frame" through `Fragment.frame_path/1`, which names `frames.main` whenever a design has several. In a two-frame chart a placed axis became an axis site, and `Composite.place/2` puts an axis or scale site under `frames.main` — a frame that may not exist, which then had no `kind`; a placed mark said no `frame`, which the validator requires once there are two; the mark page's axes section, the scales derived for a bound channel and the *add axes* step all read and wrote `main`. With the graph read in the frame under the pointer (D-162), the builder knew which frame a drop was on and threw it away.

**Decision.**
- **A mark or a label placed in a design of several frames says `frame:`** the frame under the pointer; in a design of one it says nothing, as before.
- **An axis placed in a design of several frames is written into that frame** — appended to its `axes`, its scale declared in its `scales` when missing — through the ownership rule, not inserted as sites. An axis or scale site has no way to say its frame (its key is not a frame, §19.3), and giving sites one would be a change to the composite model for a builder convenience. A legend is that frame's.
- **What the builder reads of a frame it reads of the node's frame**, `Fragment.frame_of/2`: a mark's or a label's `frame`, else the design's one, else — several and none said — `main` if present, else the first by name. Axes rows, `toggle_axis`/`toggle_grid` (now carrying `frame`), derived scales (`Scales.derive/3`, each frame from its own marks) and the axes step follow it.
- **The keyboard twin places in the open node's frame**, since it has no point.

**Rejected.**
- **Changing `Fragment.frame_name/1` to answer the first frame for several.** It is also the migration's and the composite placer's name for "the frame" and changing it would move where a stored scale site lands.
- **Keying axis and scale sites by frame.** A site's key is the name of what it declares (§19.3); overloading it for a frame would make `frames.<key>.scales.<key>` ambiguous.

**Consequences.**
- In a two-frame chart a dropped axis, a ticked side, a guessed mark's scales and a legend land in the frame a person was working on; nothing creates a stray `frames.main`.
- A design of one frame behaves byte for byte as before; the builder goldens did not change.
- An axis placed into a multi-frame design is part of the frame's layer, not a row of its own in the tree: it is removed by unticking it, or edited on the frame's page.

## 165. D-165 — Where a chart shows a frame is the chart's: a frame's placement is the site's own

**Context.** #386, work item #616. A drop from the library is a link since #381: the site references the entry, and every write into it is refused — the library's word is edited in the library. The proposal asked that a library frame dropped on the graph land at the drop point, and that frames be dragged into place (D-163). Under the lock, a linked frame could only land wherever the entry said — usually the whole box, over every other frame — and could never be dragged without unlocking it, which copies the entry and stops it following the library. A composite dropped linked (a saved chart, a house look) had the same problem the moment it shared a design with another frame.

**Decision.**
- **A frame's `box`, `cell`, `span` and `z` written at the frame (`[:frames, name]`) go into the owning site's own `local` even when the site is linked.** They say where *this chart* shows the frame — a property of the chart, not of the frame, exactly as a design records no size (spec/14 §4.2) and as a site's mask is its own (§19.3). Every other key of a linked frame is still refused with the notice.
- **The site's linkage becomes *edited*** (ref over local, #381): the row says so, the library's later changes to the frame still flow under the placement, and *revert* takes the placement back with the rest of the local.
- **A library frame is placed under a fresh key**, so it lands as `frames.<key>` beside the frames the chart has instead of merging into one of the same name (§19.6, #438). `Stack.local_path/3` maps `frames.<key>` back to the entry's own frame name inside the local.
- **New frames come from the palette's *frame*, *+ frame* and the library**, each a quarter of the graph centered where it was put; adding a second frame gives the first one's name to the marks and labels that said none.

**Rejected.**
- **Unlocking a dropped library frame.** It would copy the entry and cut it off from the library for the sake of a position.
- **Keeping placement in the site's `vars` or a new `Use` field.** The placement keys are ordinary design keys and `local` is where a site's own values already live; a new field would be a second override mechanism.
- **Refusing the drag with a notice.** The proposal's acceptance — a person lays frames out by dragging — would not hold for the frames a library supplies.

**Consequences.**
- A library frame, or a linked composite's frame, is placed and dragged like an inline one; its row shows *with local changes* once placed.
- A design's stored structure can hold a linked frame site whose local is only a box.
- The lock is no longer absolute for frames; a test holds that any other write to a linked frame is still refused.

## 166. D-166 — On a layout, a frame placed by a cell is dragged by cells

**Context.** #386, work item #617. A design's `layout` (spec/14 §2.8, #384) is a grid of weights, and a frame on it says `cell` and `span` rather than a box. D-163 made a frame's box draggable, but dragging a celled frame freely and writing a `box` throws the layout away — the frame stops following the grid when its weights change, which is the point of a layout.

**Decision.**
- **A frame placed by a cell stays placed by a cell.** Its drag moves it by whole cells and its corners stretch its span to cell edges; the hook pushes `frame_cell`, and the server checks the cell is on the grid, keeps the span to what fits, and writes `cell` and `span` (a `[1, 1]` span removed as the default). The keyboard steps a cell, or with Shift the span.
- **The frame's page offers the grid**: a select of every cell, a select of the spans that fit; choosing a cell for a frame placed by its box replaces the box.
- **The grid is drawn only while a frame moves**, faintly, so the chart is not covered with lines the rest of the time; a frame placed by its box snaps to the cells' edges too.
- **The grid's geometry is the frame's own reading**: `Place.grid/2` asks `Frame.box_of/3` for each column and row, so the gap comes out of the cells exactly as the render takes it.

**Rejected.**
- **Converting a dragged celled frame to a box.** It would quietly undo the layout.
- **Snapping a box frame to cells and writing a cell.** A person who placed a frame by its box chose to; the cells' edges are offered as snap lines, nothing more.

**Consequences.**
- A layout's frames are rearranged on the graph and stay a layout; changing the weights afterwards still moves them.
- A drag of a celled frame can never produce a cell off the grid; a hand-crafted push of one is refused with a notice.

## 167. D-167 — Identity is declared in the schema, and a pick reads it before it guesses

**Context.** #622, work item #625. The scenes umbrella (#620) and metresis both need to know which rows are about the same thing: a car across frames, a service across cells, each entity's latest row (#626). Until now nothing said so. A board inferred identity at pick time (spec/15 §13.2): the mark's `series` field, else the schema's first `:category` field. That guess is harmless for a one-off pick a person sees, and wrong for anything that follows entities, since two entities that share a color merge and one whose label changes splits.

**Decision.**
- **`identity: true` is a field fact** (spec/08 §10.1), the thirteenth description key, on a `:category`, `:text` or `:number` field and never a `:time` one (`{:invalid_for, :time}`). It is a boolean; `new/1` drops `identity: false`, so false and absent are one schema of one version, and `requires: [:identity]` (spec/14 §7.4) means "declared".
- **Several fields together are one composite identity.** `Schema.identity/1` is the declared fields, ordered by name as a string; `Schema.identity/2` a row's identity value: bare for one field, a map keyed as the schema spells the fields for several, `nil` when none is declared or the row lacks one of them. It is the one function everything that groups by entity calls, `latest` (#626) first.
- **Compatibility compares identity** (D-159's one exception to "facts are not compared"): a field either schema declares as identity that the other does not is `{:identity, a, b}`, `a` and `b` booleans, so a `Combine` of a live feed and a history that count different things is refused rather than reduced wrongly.
- **Conformance holds rows to it**: every row of every delivery of a feed that declares identity has a non-`nil` value for each declared field, `{:no_identity, request, field}` otherwise. That a later schema version keeps the same identity fields cannot be seen in one run, and compatibility covers the case where it matters.
- **A pick reads the declared identity first**, `{field, value}` for one field and a map for several, and infers as before only when the slot's schema declares none. A declared identity the row lacks is `nil`, never a guess. A selection is the pair or the map; a one-field map is held as the pair. Each field is read through `links`, and a cell holds a composite selection when one row has every field's value at once.

**Rejected.**
- **Declaring identity on the design** (a mark's `key:`). Which rows are about one thing is the feed's knowledge, as its units are, and every design over the feed would have to repeat it.
- **Identity as a list of fields at the schema's top level.** A schema is a map of field descriptions and nothing else; a field fact keeps the content hash, the codec and `requires` as they are.
- **Falling back to inference when a declared identity is missing from a row.** The feed declared what names a row; a row without it is the feed's fault, which conformance reports, and a guess would hide it.
- **Refusing a pick over a source without declared identity.** Plain charts and existing boards pick by inference today, and a pick is visible; the refusal belongs to what tracks entities over time (#626).

**Consequences.**
- Boards over feeds that declare no identity pick, select and highlight exactly as before.
- `Schema.attributes/0` has thirteen keys; `{:identity, a, b}` joins the compatibility reasons `Combine` reports.
- The examples' service feeds declare their `service` and `name` fields as identity, so the board demo's picks read the declaration rather than the inference.

## 168. D-168 — A feed window is a box over ranges, and may ask for each identity's latest row

**Context.** #622, work item #626. A window was `nil | [from, to]` over a feed's one window field (spec/08 §11.2, D-154). The scenes umbrella (#620) needs a feed asked for a *region* — the cars within a map's view — and for each entity's *current state*, and metresis will build on whatever shape the request has, so the shape is settled before either. Identity is declared since D-167, so "each entity" has a meaning that is not a guess. The schema already has a `bounds` fact (spec/08 §10.1), a display hint that never filters, so the window's map cannot use that word for "filter".

**Decision.**
- **A window may be a box**, `%{ranges: %{field => [from, to]}, latest: boolean}`. Any `:time` or `:number` field may be ranged, ends inclusive, `nil` open; `[from, to]` stays as shorthand for the window field's range and `nil` as no window, so every existing feed, monitor and board asks as before. A shorthand that does not apply to the window field is still the feed's own span (D-154); a box is checked instead.
- **`latest: true`** is, of the rows the ranges select, each identity's row with the greatest window field — so at or before the end of the window field's range, or the present when it is unranged — ties to the later row, rows of `nil` identity or time dropped. One function, `Feed.Window.latest_rows/3`, does it for a feed and for a design. It needs a declared identity and a window field.
- **`:region` and `:latest` join `supports`.** A feed that lacks them is still asked for the box, and `Feed.finish/3` trims to every range and reduces to the latest rows, so a provider opts in only for speed. Signals, Telemetry and Combine name neither; Combine routes by the window field's range alone, since a part's other fields may be in its own units and a part's latest rows are not the union's.
- **A box is checked against the schema it is asked of**, `Feed.Window.check/2`, every fault at its path, with the board's own words where they are the same fault (`{:window, :inverted}`) and the schema's where they are (`{:invalid_for, type}`, `{:type, type}`, `:unknown_key`). `finish/3` raises for a box that does not hold — the request is the caller's — and the monitor checks before asking, showing the feed `{:down, {:window, faults}}`.
- **The monitor finishes a box into its holding**, region and `latest` included, so a live feed asked for the latest rows holds one row per entity however much it pushes; a push feed refetches when a box moves in a way its holding cannot answer — its other ranges or `latest` changed, or under `latest` an earlier `to`.
- **A design asks for the latest rows on its `:window` step** (`latest: true`, spec/14 §5.4.1), the mark's own window. `Visualize.Chart.apply/2` refuses it over a slot whose feed declares no identity: `{:identity_required, source}` at the mark. The step is given the slot's schema through the pipeline's context.

**Rejected.**
- **`bounds` as the box's key.** The schema's `bounds` is a display hint that never filters; one word meaning "filter" in a request and "never filter" in a description invites the wrong reading.
- **Checking the shorthand.** A board asks one window of instants of feeds over ticks too, and D-154 made that "the feed's own span", not a fault. The shorthand keeps that meaning; only a box, which no existing caller writes, is strict.
- **A feed contract for "gone".** Dropping the entities whose latest row says they left is a `:filter` after the latest rows; the feed never knows what gone means.
- **Inferring the identity for `latest`.** An inferred identity silently merges two entities or splits one. A pick and a click, which are one-offs, still infer (D-167); following entities over time does not.
- **The refusal for marks that declare events.** A mark's tooltip `event` exists today over sources with no identity, and a click is a one-off (#622's acceptance); the refusal is for `latest` now, and transitions, bindings and keyed dynamic items join it when #620 adds them.

**Consequences.**
- `Feed.capabilities/0` is `[:window, :region, :latest, :budget, :push]`. A provider that is asked a box and reads only its window field's range, through `Feed.Window.range/2`, is correct; one that names `:region` or `:latest` is held to them by the conformance kit.
- The conformance kit takes boxes among its `windows:` probes; it holds a `:region` feed's rows to its ranges, and a `:latest` feed to at most one row per identity, the last within the box. A probe box the schema refuses is `{:probe, window, faults}`, the kit run's fault.
- The applied chart's schemas reach the frame (`Frame.new/2`'s `schemas:`) and a compiled chart, so a `latest` step reads the declared identity on every tick.
- The board's window is unchanged: it is the shorthand, the window field's range of every feed it applies to.

## 169. D-169 — A top or bottom legend wraps to its plot, and its margin grows to hold it

**Context.** #623, work item #630. A `:top` or `:bottom` legend was one row centered on the plot's width, whatever its width. A row wider than the plot spilled past both edges and the SVG clipped what was past them: on metresis's cn2 instance a six-series legend sat at `translate(-1173.6,-20)` on a 532-wide plot. §4.5 already promised that a legend is never clipped silently, but its check for a top or bottom legend counted only the height.

**Decision.**
- **A top or bottom legend wraps** (`wrap`, `:boolean`, `:geometry`, default `true`). The title and the entries flow in order, a cell that would end past the plot's width starts a row 16 below, and each row is centered on its own. A legend that fits one row is that row, unchanged.
