- **A top or bottom margin grows to hold its rows.** In the margin, the frame takes the larger of the given margin and the legend's need, (rows − 1) × 16 + 12 + 8, from the plot's height, and realizes itself once more with it. This replaces the height raise for those two sides: whether rows fit a margin is a question the library can answer by making room, and only it knows the row count. The rows depend on the plot's width alone, which the growth leaves alone, so one more pass is all it takes.
- **What cannot be fitted still raises**, naming the side and the width needed: with `wrap: false`, a row wider than the plot; with wrapping, one cell wider than the plot on its own. A side margin keeps its width raise, since a vertical list does not narrow by wrapping.
- **A label can be cut** (`max_label`, `:integer` characters, `:geometry`, default `nil`), at every position, to `max_label − 1` characters and `…`. The entry keeps the full text as a `<title>` child and an `aria-label`, so hover and a screen reader still give it, and every width the layout estimates reads the cut label.
- **The extent is exposed, not set.** `Visualize.Chart.Frame.legend_extent/1` returns the legend's box, `%{width:, height:}`, for a host that lays out around a chart.

**Rejected.**
- **A margin setting the host computes.** The host would repeat the text estimate to know the rows; the library has it.
- **Raising when the rows outgrow the top or bottom margin.** It leaves the author to guess a number the library knows.
- **Capping the entries ("+k more").** It hides series a reader may be looking for.
- **Cutting the legend's title by `max_label`.** The title is one string the author wrote for the space; a title too wide raises like any cell.
- **Wrapping side legends into columns.** They are vertical lists; their width is their longest label, which `max_label` addresses.

**Consequences.**
- A legend that fits — in one row, and in the margin it was given — renders byte for byte as before; no golden of such a chart moves.
- A chart whose top or bottom legend wraps in a margin draws a shorter plot than its margin asked for, and its scales, axes and marks follow the plot.
- A label with more characters than `max_label` shows `…`, and its entry gains a `<title>` and an `aria-label`; `data-series` and `LegendHook` are unchanged.
- metresis (#464) can stop sizing its legend margins by hand and read `legend_extent/1` where it lays out around a chart.

## 170. D-170 — Every interaction is one `Visualize.Event`, resolved on the server to the row that was drawn

**Context.** #622, work item #627. Interaction reached a host in four shapes (D-161, spec/15 §9.2): a plain chart's mark click as `handle_event(event, %{"field" => "…"})` pushed straight from `TooltipHook`, a board cell's as `{:visualize_board_event, id, %{cell, event, fields}}` with the same strings, a drill as `{:visualize_board_drill, id, %{cell, board, vars}}`, and a host-owned brush as `{:visualize_board_brush, id, %{domain}}`. Two of them carried strings scraped from `data-datum-*` attributes, which lose every type a row had. The scenes umbrella (#620) adds many more sources of interaction, and metresis builds on whatever shape exists, so the shape is settled first, in 0.6.0. An element carried only its datum's strings, so a server could not tell which row a click meant; and a chart whose data moves can be clicked on a frame that is no longer the one the server holds. Identity is declared since D-167.

**Decision.**
- **One struct, `Visualize.Event`, delivered as `{:visualize_event, id, %Visualize.Event{}}`**, `id` the chart component's or the board component's. Every field is always present: `event`, `cell`, `frame`, `item`, `path`, `row`, `at`, `value`, `modifiers`, `nil` (or `[]`, `%{}`) when it does not apply.
- **Declared names are strings; runtime names are atoms.** A mark's tooltip `event` may be an atom or a string in a design and is delivered as the string, so designs round-trip through JSON and a declared name never collides with `:drill`, `:brush` or `:select`, the events the runtime raises. On a board a declared `pick` is still never forwarded.
- **A mark with an event keys its elements.** Its group carries `data-frame` (the frame's name) and `data-mark` (the mark's position among its frame's marks), and every element that carries a datum carries `data-row`, the datum's ordinal in the mark's realized rows — the rows of `Visualize.Chart.Mark.rows/3`, before a facet's panel is taken. A mark without an event writes none of them, so a design without one renders as before.
- **`TooltipHook` pushes one wire event**, `visualize_event`, with `event`, `frame`, `mark`, `row`, `drawn` (the hook element's `data-tooltip-drawn`), `at` (the pointer in the chart's user units) and `modifiers`, to `data-tooltip-target` or the LiveView. It never sends a datum's strings.
- **The server resolves the click to the row that was drawn, or drops it.** The holder of the drawing — `Visualize.Components.Chart` for a plain chart, the cell process for a board — keeps a token for the drawing on screen, `data-tooltip-drawn`, new at each drawing. A click whose token is not the one held is dropped, never re-resolved against newer rows, so a click never names a row other than the one drawn. A click whose frame, mark or ordinal the drawing does not have, or whose name is not the event that mark declares, is dropped too: the client never invents an event.
- **`item` is `{mark, identity}`**, `mark` the mark's `id`, else its type; `identity` the declared identity (D-167), bare for one field and a map for several, else the inference a pick uses, else `item` is `nil` and `row` is still given. `path` is `[item]`, innermost first, `[]` without one. `at` is the pointer in the frame's plot area, pixels from its top-left; `nil` where there was no pointer. `modifiers` are `:shift`, `:alt`, `:ctrl` and `:meta`, in that order.
- **`Visualize.Components.Chart` is the plain chart's holder**: a thin LiveComponent over a `%Visualize.Chart.Applied{}` that renders its SVG inside `TooltipHook`, holds the drawing and its token, resolves a click with `Visualize.Event.resolve/3`, and sends the page the event. The decoding is public (`Visualize.Event.decode/1`, `resolve/3`), so a host can resolve a click itself.
- **The board's three messages are replaced**: a mark's click is the declared event with `cell` and a typed `row`, resolved by the cell process through `Visualize.Board.Server.event/3`; a drill is `event: :drill, value: %{board, vars}` with the picked row and item; a host-owned brush and Reset window are `event: :brush, value: %{domain: [from, to] | nil}`. **`:select` is new**: `value: %{identity: identity | nil}`, the board's selection in its selection form, sent whenever it changes, so a host can keep it in its URL as `brush: :host` lets it keep the window. The D-161 option names (`brush:`, `data-tooltip-target`) are unchanged.

**Rejected.**
- **Sending the old messages beside the new one.** Every click would arrive twice. Pre-1.0 with one consumer, a clean break with a migration table costs less than a deprecation cycle.
- **Resolving by the datum's strings.** A string is not the row: a `DateTime` is lost, two rows may print alike, and the client could name a row that was never drawn.
- **Re-resolving a stale click against the newest rows.** On a live chart the ordinal now names another row; a dropped click is visible and harmless, a click on the wrong entity is neither.
- **The drawing's token inside the SVG.** The SVG is a pure function of the design and its data, and a golden of it would change at every render. The token is the holder's and sits on the hook element, which the same patch replaces.
- **Requiring a declared identity for events.** A click is a one-off a person sees (#622's acceptance); `item` infers as a pick does, and `nil` when it cannot.

**Consequences.**
- A host migrates its `handle_event` for a mark's event to `handle_info({:visualize_event, id, %Visualize.Event{event: "…"}}, socket)`, rendering the chart through `Visualize.Components.Chart`; a board page matches `{:visualize_event, id, %Visualize.Event{event: :drill | :brush | :select | "…"}}` (CHANGELOG, 0.6.0).
- `Visualize.Board.Server.pick/4` answers `item` and `frame` beside `row` and `identity`, and a view carries its `drawn` token.
- `Visualize.Chart.Compiled.rows/3` gives a mark's rows as a compiled chart's tick draws them, which is what a board resolves a click against.

## 171. D-171 — The theme carries a palette of whole styles, with the color palette's contracts

**Context.** #621, work items #636 (the palette), #637 (assigning it by name and by data), #638 (view states) and #644 (the cleanup #641 decided: a style named by data, in place of #637's scales of styles). The theme's colors are a palette (spec/08 §7.1, §7.5, D-135): positions promise *distinct*, roles promise a meaning, contracts keep the meaning fixed, and the theme renders it. Whole styles had none of that. Four built-in styles came from the theme's slots (spec/14 §3.3), each role carried a glyph and a dash (D-142), and everything else was spelled out key by key in each design's `styles`, where no theme could reach it. A status drawn as a whole look, a category kept apart without hue, and what "selected" or "muted" looks like had no home a theme could restyle — and #620's interaction states need exactly the last.

**Decision.**
- **`%Visualize.Theme{}` gains `styles`**, a map from a palette name to a style node, in four groups: style series `:style_1` … `:style_n` (`{:style, i}` cycles), style roles `:good` … `:neutral`, state styles `:hover`, `:focused`, `:selected`, `:active`, `:disabled`, `:muted`, `:emphasis`, `:breakpoint`, and furniture styles `:axis`, `:grid`, `:label`, `:title`, `:caption`, `:value`, `:annotation` (spec/08 §7.7).
- **The names are a fixed set.** A theme chooses how many style series it has and how every entry looks; it cannot add a name.
- **Entries are flat style nodes written in slots.** No `extends`; `:cue` on a role's dash or symbol takes the role's cue.
- **Contracts are checked when a theme is built**, by `new/1` and `merge/2`, raising `ArgumentError` naming the theme, the entry and the term: a role paints with its own role; a state style changes a non-hue key (stroke width, outline, opacity, dash or effect); neighboring style series, the wrap included, differ in a non-hue key; a text furniture entry resolves a font family and a size; `:grid` resolves a stroke.
- **A missing name falls back to the default theme's entry, whole**, as a missing ramp or role does.
- **The four built-in styles of spec/14 §3.3 are the furniture styles of the chart's theme.** The built-in themes' four are the nodes they always were, so nothing drawn under a built-in theme moves.
- **`Visualize.Theme.style/2` and `style_names/1`** read the palette.
- **A palette name is a style reference** (#637). At a site, in a stack and under `extends`, a name resolves against the design's `styles` and then the palette of the theme the chart is drawn with, so a design's entry of the name wins and a theme swap restyles every other use (spec/14 §3.3). The validator checks a name against `Theme.style_names/1` of a built-in or inline theme, as it checks slots, and reports `{:undeclared, :style, name}`.
- **A style is named by data through a field entry in the stack** (#644, from #641's questions 4 and 6, spec/14 §3.6). A stack entry `{:field, f}`, alone or in a list (`style: [:base, {:field, :state}, :bold]`), lays the style **whose name is the datum's value** of `f`, where it stands, later layers winning. The value resolves as any reference does — the design's `styles`, then the theme's palette — as an atom, or as a string spelling a name, matched against the names there are and never made into an atom. A value that names no style, `nil` included, lays nothing, and the mark keeps its own look. It is resolved by §3.2's binding rule: per element for a mark that draws one per datum, from the series' first datum for a path, the per-series and placement keys always from the first datum, `symbol`/`symbol_size` per symbol, and a mark's inline label from the datum it labels. Every style site accepts it (mark, label, mark_label, axis, legend), its field checked as any binding's; a site without a datum lays nothing for it. The mapping from a reading to a style name belongs to the data — a column a host computes, or a virtual source (#643) — not to a scale.
- **What #637 shipped for this is removed, unreleased** (#644): `{:scale, name, field}` stack entries; the `{:styles, :roles}` and `{:styles, :series}` ranges, style names in list and map ranges, and `fact(…, :status)` as a style range; the faults `{:style_range, kind}`, `{:style_scale, name}` and `{:style_color, name}`, and `{:invalid_for, kind}` for a scale entry off a mark; the JSON tags `$styles` and `$scale`. Each removed form is now an ordinary fault: a `{:scale, …}` entry is no style type, a styles range is no range type, and a style name in a map range is an undeclared slot.
- **A stored theme carries its palette** (#637): the inline `:theme` node and the theme document (spec/14 §2.5, §9.3) accept `styles`, each entry checked as a style node and by the palette's contracts.
- **The board's selection is drawn as view states, laid per datum** (#638, spec/15 §13.2). A cell that shows the selection gives its rows a state column, `:"$vis_state"`, `:selected` for a row that holds the selection and `:muted` for every other, and ends every data mark's stack with `{:field, :"$vis_state"}` (#644; under #638 a hidden `:ordinal` scale of styles of the same name in every frame read the column). That is the field entry above with nothing of the board's own: the names resolve against the design's `styles` and then the theme's palette, so a design restyles its board's selection by declaring `selected:` or `muted:`. The built-in `:muted` is `opacity: 0.25`, the fade the board drew before, and `:selected` is `stroke_width: 2` alone: a selected line or rule keeps its color and draws heavier, and a bar, a point or a text with no stroke of its own draws as it is, shown by the fade of the rest. A cell that does not show the selection gets no column and no entry, and draws byte for byte as before.
- **The hooks get their view states as data** (#638, spec/10 §4.4). The server resolves `:hover` and `:focused` in `:literal` mode against the frame the hook's attributes are written for — the design's entry, then the theme's — and writes them as `data-vis-hover` (`Visualize.Hooks.Crosshair.attrs/3`, the new `Visualize.Hooks.Tooltip.attrs/1`) and `data-vis-focused` (`Visualize.Hooks.Keyboard.attrs/1`): a JSON object of SVG presentation attributes, only the keys an overlay shape draws. `CrosshairHook`'s markers, `KeyboardHook`'s focus marker and a new hover outline of `TooltipHook` lay those keys over their own defaults, still in their overlays outside the patched subtree. A hook element without the attribute draws as before.

**Rejected.**
- **`:grid` as a text style.** It draws lines, not text; the group is furniture, and the font contract binds its text entries only.
- **Open names, as `styles` in a design are.** A design that names `:selected` must mean the same thing on every theme; a theme-only name would be a design entry living in the wrong place.
- **`extends` inside the palette.** A chain inside the theme is a cycle or an override a design cannot see; flat entries read the same wherever they are used, and a design's style may still extend one.
- **Merging a missing entry key by key over the default's.** A partial entry laid over another theme's entry is a look neither theme chose. Entries fall back whole.
- **Literal colors in the built-in entries.** Written in slots, one palette serves every built-in theme and a stylesheet still switches it (spec/08 §7.3).
- **A scale whose range is styles** (#637's mechanism, removed by #644, #641). `{:styles, :roles}`, `{:styles, :series}`, lists and maps of style names and `fact(…, :status)`, laid by `{:scale, name, field}` entries, put the mapping from data to a look in a scale declared somewhere else: what a datum gets was hard to see and hard to change, a scale of styles read as color needed a fault of its own (`{:style_color, name}`), and an `:ordinal` scale's `unknown` — the theme's `:axis` color — needed a special rule so that it never laid the axis's whole look. A field whose value is the style's name says the same thing with nothing between the datum and the style, and the mapping moves to the data (#643).
- **`{:style, i}` as a style reference in a design.** In a design `{:style, n}` is a store id (spec/14 §19.2, D-100): `Visualize.Chart.Refs` replaces it with the stored style's body, and a missing one is a mask. It cannot also mean the n-th style series without making every stored reference ambiguous and turning a missing id into a palette entry. A site names a position as `:style_k`, and a field whose value is `:style_k` assigns positions by data; `{:style, i}` stays an argument of `Visualize.Theme.style/2`, a theme function, and is no design reference.
- **An outline in a fixed color as the built-in `:selected`** (#638; the first palette had `stroke: :text`, `stroke_style: :outside`). On a stroked mark the outline's color *is* the mark's paint, so a selected line lost its series color to the theme's text color, and a text mark was stroked. Which key a state should change for which kind of mark is #640's question; the built-in entry changes only `stroke_width`, which every kind either draws heavier or ignores, and a design that wants an outline declares one.
- **Resolving `:hover` and `:focused` in the browser** (#638). The hooks would need the theme's slots, the palette and the design's styles, and the stack's rules, in JavaScript: a second resolver that drifts from the server's. A resolved map of attributes is what the hooks already receive for everything else they draw (D-75).
- **Resolving the hooks' states in `:css` mode** (#638). The overlays are appended to `document.body`, outside the chart, where a page's stylesheet may define none of the theme's custom properties; a `var(--vis-text, #333333)` there is its fallback at best. The literal is the color the server's theme means.

**Consequences.**
- A host theme can restyle the furniture: a heavier `:title` in its theme changes every title drawn under it, and a design's own `:title` still wins.
- `check/1` and `violations/1` report palette faults under `{:style, name}`, after the ramps and roles.
- A stored theme carries its palette: `Visualize.Chart.Theme` writes and reads `styles`, and a theme read back draws a palette name as the theme that was written does.
- A design names whole looks by meaning and position — `style: :warning`, `style: [:node, {:field, :health}]` — and draws them in every theme's own looks. A design that names no palette entry and lays no field entry renders byte for byte as before.
- #638 draws view states from the palette: a board's selection is `:selected` and `:muted`, so a selected line or rule now draws 2 units wide in its own color, a selected bar or point draws as before, and a muted datum fades as it did; the hooks' hover and focus markers are `:hover` and `:focused`, so a chart whose hook attributes come from the library's helpers draws its markers in the theme's `:text` — the focus ring dashed — and `Visualize.Components.Chart` outlines the hovered datum. #639 shows the palette in the builder.

## 172. D-172 — A style is compound parts, merged part by part and projected onto the element keys

**Context.** #649, part 1 of the set shaped from Research #641 ("The style grammar, reshaped"), work item #657. A style was 29 flat keys (`fill`, `fill_opacity`, `stroke`, `stroke_width`, `stroke_dasharray`, `font_size`, `margin_x`, `symbol_size`, …), grouped only for the builder's form. A fill or a stroke could not be passed, merged or replaced as one thing; a gradient was only a `{:paint, name}` reference into `defs`; and the palette #636–#638 shipped (unreleased) was written in those keys. Hardly any users exist yet, so the change is silent: designs are rewritten on read, wherever they come from.

**Decision.**
- **A style holds compound parts** (spec/14 §3.1): `fill` and `stroke` (each a **paint**: `type` — `:none`, `:solid`, `:linear`, `:radial` or `:paint` — then `color` or `stops` with a gradient's `angle`, `center` and `radius`, or a `defs` `name`, then `opacity`; a stroke adds `width`, `dash`, `join`, `cap` and `placement`), `font`, `text` (`anchor`, `baseline`, `offset`, `angle`), `effect`, `marker` and `curve`, beside the whole element's `opacity`, `blend` and `class`. New keys are spelled American (`color`, `center`).
- **Shorthands are normalized on load, and the stored form is the full shape**: `fill: <color>` is a solid paint, `:none` is `%{type: :none}`, `{:paint, name}` a `:paint` paint, a gradient's `color` list its evenly spaced stops, `curve: :step` and `effect: :shadow` typed parts.
- **The merge rule** — `extends`, a stack at a site, and the fragment cascade: a part merges key by key; when two parts name different `type`s the later replaces the earlier, keeping only what no type decides (a paint's `opacity`; a stroke's `width`, `dash`, `join`, `cap`, `placement`).
- **The parts live at the design layer only** (#649's acceptance, modification 1). `Visualize.Chart.Style` merges in parts and **projects** the result onto the flat keys the frame, the marks and the backends always read (`Style.project/1`, inside `node/2` and `resolve/3`), so the IR and every backend are unchanged. A gradient written in place projects to `{:paint, name}` under a hash of what it draws, and the frame holds it beside the declared paints, so it draws exactly as a declared one does.
- **`defs` holds paints of the same shape**, a gradient's version 2 words (`kind`, `centre`, a stop's `offset` and `colour`) read as `type`, `center`, `at` and `color` (modification 5). <!-- spelling: allow -->
- **Schema version 3** (modification 2). The 2 → 3 step rewrites a stored design's styles and paints; `Migration.canonical/1` runs the same rewrite at every version, so a design a host builds at version 3 in the flat keys is rewritten too — safe because no version 3 key is named like a flat one. The JSON codec reads a flat key by the type it had. The validator reads a design with its styles in parts (`Migration.parts/2`), so a fault in a flat key is reported at the part it means.
- **The theme palette is written in parts**, its contracts restated for the shape (a role's paint colors, a state's non-hue change, the series' non-hue difference, the furniture's font and grid stroke), `:cue` on a role's `stroke.dash` and `marker.shape`. A **modifiers** group joins it — `:bold`, `:italic`, `:thin`, `:thick`, `:dashed`, `:dotted`, `:outline` — each changing exactly one thing, a contract of its own. `:selected` is `%{stroke: %{width: 2}}`.
- **The builder's form keeps editing the flat keys** (`Schema.describe(:flat_style)`, a kind described but not of `kinds/0`) until #655 gives the parts a form; what it writes is read in parts.

**Rejected.**
- **Parts in the IR and the backends.** Every backend would change at once for no drawing that changes; the element keys are a projection, and the backends move when a part needs something they cannot say (#652's canvas gradients).
- **Keeping both shapes as stored forms.** A tool would read two forms forever; the flat keys are read and never written.
- **Replacing a whole stroke on a type change.** A stroke's width and dash are not its paint's; a solid stroke turned into a gradient keeps its line.
- **A shadow's offset and color now.** The element carries one radius and the filter derives its offset from it; adding them changes the IR and the canvas backends, which this decision keeps fixed.
- **Atoms for the names of gradients written in place.** A design from elsewhere could make atoms without bound; the name is a string.

**Consequences.**
- Every design and golden renders as before through the migration. The one byte that moves is a declared gradient's id prefix, a hash of `defs`, which changes with the paint's new words; the gradient drawn is the same.
- `Visualize.Theme.style/2` answers entries in parts; `Visualize.Chart.Style.node/2` still answers the flat keys, now a projection.
- A board, a theme document and a fragment are stamped version 3; a version 2 one is read and migrated.
- The later work items of #649 amend this decision: lists of styles anywhere a style goes and the precedence order (#658), each mark kind drawing only its own parts and the filled line's migration to an `:area` (#659), sanitized strings and checked URLs (#660), and `explain` per part (#661).

**Amended by #658** (work item B of #649). **A list of styles goes anywhere a style goes**: a `styles` entry, `extends`, a theme palette entry and every site, stacking left to right by the merge rule; a `styles` entry may also be a bare name. A cycle through names, lists or `extends` is `:cycle`. **A bare atom in a style position is a style**, and `:none` is the one bare atom a paint's place reserves. **The precedence order** — theme root defaults, container defaults, mark-kind defaults, named and stacked styles, the field-chosen style, generator results, view states, the site's local edit — is the order the library keeps where it adds a layer itself: a board's selection state goes before a stack's trailing inline node, so a local edit beats a view state and a view state beats the data's choice. A stack an author writes is laid in the order written (#644). *Rejected:* reordering an author's stack into the layers — a stack's order is the author's statement, and #644 laid a field entry where it stands.

**Amended by #659** (work item C of #649). **Each mark kind draws only its own parts**: `line` and `rule` the stroke; the closed kinds the fill and the stroke as an outline; `symbol` also the marker; `text` and labels the fill, a stroke halo, the font and the text placement; `opacity`, `blend`, `effect`, `class` and `curve` wherever they apply. A part a kind does not draw is ignored, never an error, because styles are shared across kinds. **The filled line is removed**: the area under a line is an `:area` mark beneath it. The 2 → 3 migration inserts that area for a stored line whose style had a fill, and pins the default paint of every mark the insertion moves, so a stored design renders as before; a version 3 line's fill is not drawn (#649's acceptance, modification 2). *Rejected:* keeping the filled line beside the area — two ways to draw one thing, and a role style's fill would keep filling lines it was never meant for (#640).

**Amended by #660** (work item D of #649). **Strings from a design or a theme are untrusted**: a font family refuses `<`, `>`, `/`, `;`, `{`, `}`, `\` and control characters, a class must be CSS class names, and every string reaching SVG, a canvas's JavaScript or a stylesheet is escaped for that context. **URLs are checked at validation** (today a tiles `url`): `https:`; `http:` only when the host allows; `data:` only as a PNG, JPEG or WebP image; every other scheme and a relative URL refused; an optional host allowlist. The policy is application config, `config :visualize, urls: [http: false, hosts: :any]`, and a read's `urls:` option can only make it stricter (#649's acceptance, modification 4). A refusal is `{:url, reason}` and never quotes the URL. *Rejected:* a looser per-read option — one tenant's design must not be able to ask its way past the application's rule; and quoting the URL in the fault — a refused URL is the attacker's text.

**Amended by #661** (work item E of #649). **`Visualize.Chart.explain/2` explains an element's look part by part**: for a node of an applied chart, and a datum and a view state when given, each part's resolved value, whether the kind draws it, and the chain of contributions — the layer of the precedence order, the style's name and the names it was reached through, the field and the reading that chose it — lowest first. It is a second arity beside `explain/1`, which explains a stack of fragments by path; the two answer different questions about different inputs. *Rejected:* folding it into `explain/1` by the shape of the argument — a list of layers and an applied chart are not alternatives of one question.

## 173. D-173 — A color is a typed value, a name from the theme's color table or a transform of colors, resolved against the theme at render

**Context.** #650, part 2 of the set shaped from Research #641 ("The style grammar, reshaped"), work item #662. A color was a bare atom (a theme slot such as `:series_1` or `:axis`, or a role color such as `:bad`), a literal, or `{:ramp, name, t}`. After #649 a bare atom is a style (D-172, amended by #658), so a color written the same way was ambiguous to read even where its key settled it. Also, `:bad` was both a role color (D-135) and a role style (D-171). Colors could not be derived from other colors: mixed, faded or darkened in a way that follows the theme. A host that wanted a pale red behind its bad rows had to write a literal, and a theme swap left that literal behind.

**Decision.**
- **A color is a typed value**: `color(:red)` is `{:color, :red}` and `ramp(:thermal, 0.7)` is `{:ramp, :thermal, 0.7}`. A literal string and `{:series, i}` stay colors as they were.
- **Transforms are typed values too**: `intensity/2`, `blend/1`, `alpha/2`, `lighten/2`, `darken/2`, `saturate/2` and `contrast/1`. Each takes the color first and every number is in `0..1`, so colors pipe. The helpers build data and compile nothing, and a color resolves against the theme at render (spec/08 §1.9).
- **The color space is OKLab, and OKLCH for its polar form.** `blend` follows CSS's `color-mix(in oklab, …)` for any number of colors: intensities are weights, premultiplied alpha is used, a remainder below 1 is transparent, and weights above 1 are scaled to sum to 1. `lighten`, `darken` and `saturate` move OKLCH lightness or chroma toward its end by `t`. Chroma's end is 0.4, CSS's 100%.
- **`alpha` sets the opacity and `intensity` multiplies it.** They are the same on their own and differ inside a blend, where an intensity is a weight.
- **An out-of-gamut result is gamut-mapped as CSS Color 4 does** (§13.2, chroma reduction in OKLCH within a ΔEOK of 0.02). So a literal the library computes is the color a browser draws for the same CSS.
- **`Visualize.Color.resolve/3` is the one door** for a typed color, as `Visualize.Theme.resolve/3` is for a slot. A name or a position resolves exactly as the slot did, so nothing drawn today changes.

**Rejected.**
- **HSL for the transforms.** Equal steps in HSL lightness look unequal from hue to hue, which is why Sass deprecated `lighten`/`darken`. CIE Lab, which the ramps use (D-135), bends blue toward purple when mixed; OKLab does not.
- **Multiplying in `alpha`.** After a blend whose remainder is transparent, the author who writes `alpha(0.65)` means the opacity they see, not 0.65 of a fraction they would have to work out.
- **Clipping each channel when out of gamut.** It shifts the hue of a saturated color, and it disagrees with what a browser draws for the same CSS.
- **A separate module for the helpers.** The proposal names `Visualize.Color`, and `saturate/2` reads its argument to keep the released meaning on a `%Visualize.Color{}`. `import Visualize.Color, only: [...]` is the documented import, because a bare import would also bring `to_string/1` beside `Kernel`'s.

**Consequences.**
- A color can be derived from the theme's colors and still follows a theme swap.
- The later work items of #650 amend this decision: the color table (#663), typed colors in the grammar and the codec (#664), CSS output for the transforms (#665), the contracts (#666), `shade` (#667) and automatic dark mode (#668).

**Amended by #663** (work item 2 of #650). **A theme carries a color table** (spec/08 §7.1). `color(name)` is looked up in the theme's own `colors`, then the default theme's, then the CSS named colors. The furniture colors and `:series_k` are names of it, read from their fields. The built-ins name their status hues `:red`, `:amber` and `:green`, their `:info` and `:neutral` hues `:blue` and `:gray`, and the five roles by use (D-135, amended). A name resolves as a slot does, `var(--vis-red, #c23238)`, and `stylesheet/1` declares the theme's own names, so a stylesheet switch carries `color(:red)` too. *Rejected:* adding the table's names to `slots/1`. The JSON codec reads a slot's name as that slot, and `"red"` in a stored design has always been the CSS literal; reading it as the theme's red would recolor stored designs.

**Amended by #664** (work item 3 of #650). **Typed colors run through the grammar.**
- **Canonical form.** A `:color` position of a stored design holds a literal string, `:none` or a typed color, never another bare atom. `Migration.canonical/1` rewrites `fill: :bad` to `{:color, :bad}` and `:contrast` to `{:contrast, :under}` (`contrast()`, the ink over whatever the text is drawn on). The rewrite runs at every version, as #649's flat keys do, so it is silent everywhere (#650's acceptance, modification 1). The validator therefore has no "a style name where a color goes" fault: the stored form is always typed.
- **Validation.** Every `{:color, name}` is checked against the theme's color table, as `{:undeclared, :color, name}`. A transform's shape and its literal operands are checked as `{:type, :color}`.
- **The JSON codec gains a tag per typed color**: `$color`, `$intensity`, `$alpha`, `$lighten`, `$darken`, `$saturate`, `$blend` and `$contrast`. A color name is made an atom only when it is plain words. An unknown tag in a color position is read as the object it is, which the validator refuses: #645's rule, for these tags.
- **The palette is written in typed colors**, and the role contract reads `{:color, role}` as the role's own.
- **The status ramp is defined from the theme's green, amber and red.** A ramp anchor may be `{:color, name}` of the table.
- At render a bare atom met in a color position (a scale's range, a component) is still read as a name, so nothing that builds colors in code breaks.

*Rejected:* reporting a bare atom in a color position as a fault, as the proposal first had it. After #649 a `fill` can only hold a paint, so the atom has one meaning; refusing it would break every stored design for a spelling the library can rewrite. *Rejected:* keeping `:contrast` as a reserved word beside `:none`. `contrast(c)` over a chosen color is the general form, and one value with an operand reads better than a word and a function.

**Amended by #665** (work item 4 of #650). **In `:css` mode a transform is written as CSS.**
- `intensity` and `blend` become `color-mix(in oklab, …)`, a blend of three or more nested.
- `alpha`, `lighten`, `darken` and `saturate` become relative `oklch(from … )`.
- Every operand is written in its own `:css` form, so a stylesheet's theme switch carries through every derived color without a re-render.
- The contrast ink stays a literal (D-123).
- The literal a browser computes for the CSS is the literal `:literal` mode computes. An evaluator written from the CSS specifications alone holds the two together.
- **`stylesheet/1` respects forced colors.** An `@media (forced-colors: active)` block maps the furniture to the user's system colors (`CanvasText`, `Canvas`, `GrayText`). In that mode a browser forces data fills and strokes itself, and a role still reads without hue by its dash and marker.

*Rejected:* writing the forced-colors block into every chart's SVG. A declaration on the `<svg>` beats an ancestor's theme class (D-55), so the block belongs with the stylesheet the host includes. *Rejected:* `@supports` fallbacks to the literal inside the SVG. A presentation attribute cannot carry two values, and the browsers that lack relative colors predate 2023.

**Amended by #666** (work item 5 of #650; D-171's contracts amended). **The role styles' contracts are rewritten for typed colors** (#650's acceptance, modification 2). "A role style paints with its own role" is gone: a theme restyles what a meaning looks like and may paint `bad` in any color. The library now holds the five roles as a set:
- they are distinct from each other without hue (by dash, marker or weight, cues in place);
- their colors are at least ΔEOK 0.02 apart;
- each one's solid `fill` or `stroke` reaches **3:1** against the theme's background (WCAG 1.4.11), and its `fill` reaches **4.5:1** when the entry carries a `font` (1.4.3).

Every `{:color, name}` in a palette entry's paints must resolve in the theme's color table. A refusal names the measured ratio and the rule that failed. Every built-in theme passes; the lowest ratio is the color-safe theme's `good`, at 3.11:1 on white. *Rejected:* checking a role against every possible background, or against the surface. The background is the one the theme promises its marks are read on (spec/08 §7.4), and a stricter rule would refuse the built-ins' light-page status greens.

**Amended by #667** (work item 6 of #650). **`shade(c, n)`, with `n` from 0 to 1000 and 500 the color itself**, is the one transform on the designers' own scale. One OKLCH curve, defined once in `Visualize.Color`, moves lightness toward 0.97 or 0.2 by `u = |n − 500| / 500`, eases chroma by `1 − u²` and keeps the hue. Both modes use it: `:literal` computes it, and `:css` writes it as a relative `oklch(from …)` with the same numbers. **A theme may pin steps** of a name, `colors: %{red: %{500 => …, 100 => …}}`. Its step 500 is the name's color. A pinned step resolves to the pinned literal in `:literal` and to its own custom property, `var(--vis-red-100, …)`, in `:css`, declared by `stylesheet/1`. A theme's `colors` holds a color or such a map, the schema's `:theme_color`, written `$steps` in JSON.

*Rejected:* a table of steps per hue in every theme, as Tailwind ships. One function covers every color, including a host's and a derived one, and a brand pins only the steps it has tokens for. *Rejected:* a 0–1 step like the other transforms. 100 … 900 is the vocabulary designers and token files already use, and a translation would be one more thing to get wrong.

**Amended by #668** (work item 7 of #650). **Automatic dark mode in `:css` output.**
- A theme declares its counterpart, `dark: :dark` on the default and `light: :default` on the dark theme. A theme from `new/1` declares none unless its fields say so.
- A chart's `:css` root carries `Visualize.Theme.color_scheme/2`: an `@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark)` rule scoped to the root's id, declaring the counterpart's properties, in a `<style>` element, a new IR type that only the SVG backend writes.
- Names and the transforms written as CSS follow the viewer's preference with no host code.
- The rule is on only while the theme is implicit: no `theme:` option to apply and the design's `theme` `:default`. `color_scheme: false` turns it off.
- `:literal` output uses the theme it was given.

*Rejected:* `light-dark()` in every fallback. It would rewrite every `var(--vis-…, literal)` the library has ever written, every golden with it, for a property only 2024 browsers read. *Rejected:* emitting the rule under an explicit theme. A declaration on the `<svg>` beats an ancestor's theme class (D-55), so a host that chose a theme, in Elixir or in CSS, must not have it overridden by the viewer's preference. A host that switches by class keeps doing so, and is the one case the library cannot see; `color_scheme: false` is its switch.

## 174. D-174 — Defaults are inherited from containers, nearest last, over a theme root that is every part

**Context.** #651, part 3 of the set shaped from Research #641 ("The style grammar, reshaped"), work item #670. After D-172 a style says only what it changes, so something has to say the rest: the width of a stroke given only a color, the family of a font given only a size. That came from the mark's own behavior and the backends' initial values, nowhere a design or a theme could reach, so "every stroke here is 2 wide" was a style named at every mark. #641 settled that any container may declare defaults, that the theme supplies the root, and the precedence order (D-172, amended by #658) gave them layers 1 to 3.

**Decision.**
- **A `:defaults` node** holds a style's parts (`fill`, `stroke`, `font`, `text`, `effect`, `marker`, `curve`, `opacity`, `blend`) and one entry per mark type but `:text`, of the same parts (spec/14 §3.8). It has no `extends` and no `class`.
- **Containers.** The design and each frame carry `defaults`; a composite — a layer or a group of the builder's tree — carries them beside its `uses`, and `Composite.flatten/2` writes them onto each mark, label, axis and frame it places, under the node's own. A mark, a label and an axis therefore carry `defaults` too: the flat design keeps the scoping as data.
- **Resolution is container by container, nearest last**: the theme's own defaults, the design's, the frame's, the element's own; each container lays its part defaults and then its entry for the element's kind, by the merge rule; the site's stack is laid over all of it. A mark takes its type's entry; a text, a label, an axis and a legend take the part defaults alone. The `:text` mark has no entry: `text` is the part's key in a style, and the parts only a text draws, `font` and `text`, reach every text already.
- **The theme's root is every part**: `Visualize.Theme.defaults/1` is the library's base — a 1-wide, butt-capped, miter-joined stroke, opacities 1, the theme's font family and size, no effect, a 64-square circle marker, a linear curve — under the theme's own `defaults` field. **The base is never written onto an element**: it is what an absent key already draws, so writing it would change every golden and no drawing. A theme's own `defaults` are laid.
- **`explain/2` shows layers 1 to 3**: `:theme` under every part, `:defaults` and `:kind` with the container that gave them.

**Rejected.**
- **Layers 2 and 3 as two global layers**, every container's part defaults under every container's kind entries. Then a design's `line:` entry would beat a frame's "every stroke 3 wide", and the farther container would win over the nearer; #641 asks for the nearest ancestor's default.
- **A layer's and a group's defaults as an inline style at the head of each placed mark's stack.** It would sit at layer 4, beat the design's and the frame's kind entries for the wrong reason, be read as the site's local edit by `with_state/2` when the stack held nothing else, and be explained as a style nobody wrote.
- **Writing the base onto every element** so the root is visible in the output. Every chart's bytes would change for no change in what is drawn, and a text that takes its font from the page would be pinned to the theme's.
- **Mark-kind entries for the furniture** (`axis:`, `legend:`). The furniture has named styles already (§3.3); an entry per furniture kind would be a second way to say the same thing.

**Consequences.**
- A design without `defaults`, under a theme without them, draws byte for byte as before.
- A default is under every style, the furniture's named styles included: a default font family reaches a text whose style names none.
- The next work item of #651 amends this decision with units and non-scaling sizes, which resolve against what these layers give.

**Amended by #671** (work item 2 of #651). **Sizes are written in units, and a stroke may keep its width on screen.**
- **`Visualize.Units`** (spec/08 §13): `px`, `pt`, `pc`, `in` (`inch/1`), `cm`, `mm` at CSS's fixed ratios, `em` and `pct` relative, stored `{n, unit}`; and the table of world lengths #620 converts by. A bare number keeps its meaning, user units.
- **The schema's `:size` takes a unit value**, and a new `:length` type — a number or a unit value — types a dash's and a text offset's members. JSON writes `{"$unit": [n, "unit"]}`.
- **Every unit resolves on the server**, so both backends get a number. **`em`** is measured at compile time (#651's acceptance, modification 2): at `font.size` against the inherited font size — each container's relative size against the one before it, the theme's `font_size` at the root — and anywhere else against the element's own resolved font size, which is CSS's rule.
- **`pct` only where a size has a box** (modification 1): a text offset against the labeled box, a marker size against its band, a font size against the inherited font. At a stroke's `width`, a `dash` member or an effect's `radius` it is `{:no_box, key}`; a unit at an opacity is `{:no_unit, key}`.
- **A unit-valued marker size is the side** of the square of the area, since a bare marker size is an area (d3's) and "6 pt" means a length.
- **`stroke.non_scaling: true`** projects to `vector_effect: :non_scaling_stroke`. SVG writes `vector-effect="non-scaling-stroke"` on each shape, pushing a group's down because the property does not inherit; a canvas divides the width by `√|det|` of the transform current when it is set (modification 3), a `nonScalingLineWidth` command in `:json` and `:js` and bit 4 of the binary style record.

*Rejected:* writing `em` as CSS. A canvas has no cascade, so the two backends would draw different widths (modification 2). *Rejected:* `pct` of the plot at a stroke's width. A stroke's width is not a length of any box the reader sees, so the percentage would mean nothing a person could predict (modification 1). *Rejected:* a non-scaling marker and text now. SVG has no attribute that keeps a glyph's size under a scale (SVG 2's `non-scaling-size` is drawn by no browser), and nothing in the library zooms a canvas yet; they come with #620's zooming scenes. *Rejected:* a `:length` type for every size. A `:size` takes a slot too (`:font_size`), and a dash or an offset member never did.

## 175. D-175 — Paints are drawn on every backend: a canvas builds each gradient over the element's box, and the binary stream gains a version

**Context.** #652, part 4 of the set shaped from Research #641 ("The style grammar, reshaped"), work item #672; it absorbs Research #357 and #358. Since #356 a canvas drew a gradient as its first stop's color (spec/14 §3.2), because the binary stream had no way to say a gradient and a canvas gradient is a different object from an SVG one: it lives in absolute coordinates, where SVG's `objectBoundingBox` gradient is in the units of the element's own box. Part 1 (D-172) made gradients a first-class paint of every `fill` and `stroke`, so the fallback would now be wrong everywhere a canvas draws. The acceptance of #652 settled that the binary stream gains its records under a new stream version that old decoders are not asked to read, and that the JSON stream gains the paints too.

**Decision.**
- **The frame writes the paint, not a color.** In both modes `{:paint, name}` resolves to a `Visualize.IR.Paint` (spec/02 §2.1): its id, its kind, its geometry in box units and its stops resolved in the render's mode. SVG writes `url(#id)` either way, so a `:literal` render rasterized by resvg draws the gradient too. The first stop survives only as the canvas fill of a `:text`, whose box the server cannot measure, and as the color a label's ink is chosen against.
- **A canvas builds the paint over the element's tight bounding box** (`Visualize.IR.Paint.bounds/1`: a curve and an arc by their extremes), per element, so a group's gradient is built over each child's box as SVG builds it. A linear gradient maps **exactly** into user units — the box is an affine map, and an affine map keeps a linear gradient's isolines parallel, skew included — from `P₀`, the image of its start, to `P₀ + g/|g|²` with `g` its gradient in user units. A radial **fill** is drawn under the box transform, set between building the path and filling it, which draws SVG's ellipse exactly. A radial **stroke** is the one approximation, the circle of the same area: the box transform would scale the line's width unevenly. A box without area draws no paint, as SVG draws none.
- **The commands.** `:commands`, `:json` and `:js` replace the `fill`/`stroke` of an element drawn with a paint by `save`, the paint's `linearGradient` or `radialGradient` command (with the box transform for a radial fill), the draw and `restore`, so the gradient is never state a sibling inherits. The two commands join `nonScalingLineWidth` as the ones that name no Canvas 2D method; `:js` writes them as self-contained statements, since a `:js` script runs with no library beside it.
- **The binary stream's version 2** adds a `version` record (`0x00 0x02`, first), a `paint` record (`0x50`: an index, the kind, the geometry in box units as f32, the stops as offset and RGBA) written once per distinct paint before every other record, a paint index in the style record (bits 5 and 6, after the four fields of version 1), and a `box` record (`0x51`, four f32) between an element's geometry and its draw records. A stream without a paint is version 1, byte for byte. The hooks keep the fill and stroke paint as state scoped by `save`/`restore`, and build the gradient at the draw; the server measures the box.

**Rejected.**
- **A header on every stream.** It would change every stream's bytes, every golden and every consumer's decoder for the charts that draw no paint, which is nearly all of them; a version record that only a version 2 stream carries leaves them alone, and still makes an old decoder throw at the first byte of a new stream rather than draw black.
- **The gradient inline in each style record.** Every element of a mark carries its own style, so a hundred bars of one gradient would carry it a hundred times; defined once and referred to by two bytes is SVG's `defs` again.
- **The hooks measuring the box from the decoded coordinates** (#357's sketch). A second implementation of the tight box in JavaScript, beside the server's for `:json`, would drift from it — an arc's or a curve's extremes are where two hand-written versions differ — while the server has the geometry already and writes 17 bytes. The box record is written only for an element drawn with a paint.
- **A pattern from an offscreen canvas for a radial stroke**, to draw the ellipse under a stroke exactly. It costs a canvas per element and a margin for the stroke's overhang, for a paint nothing in the library draws on a stroke; the circle of the same area is exact on a square box.

**Consequences.**
- spec/14 §3.2's "first stop on canvas" rule is gone; #357 and #358 are answered.
- A canvas still ignores `fill_opacity` and `stroke_opacity`, for a gradient as for a color (spec/09 §3.3.3 reads `opacity` alone): a paint's own `opacity` is the element's, so a translucent gradient is opaque on a canvas until those keys are read there. *Answered by #675, D-180: every canvas draws them.*
- The hatch paint (work item #673) and the shadow's offset and color (#674) amend this decision.

**Amended by #673** (work item 2 of #652). **A hatch is a paint.**
- `type: :hatch` paints an area with lines, a crosshatch or dots of one `color` over an optional `background`, at an `angle` (`45` when absent), a `spacing` and a `width` in #651's units, resolved on the server (an `em` against the theme's `font_size`; a `pct` has no box and is `{:no_box, key}`).
- **SVG** draws it as a `<pattern>` in the frame's `defs`, keyed by a hash of what it draws as a gradient is, in user units (`userSpaceOnUse`) and turned by `patternTransform`, so hatched neighbors line up. **A canvas** draws a `CanvasPattern` from an offscreen tile four times the period, scaled back by the pattern's transform, so a period that is not a whole pixel keeps its length; the `:json`/`:js` command is `hatchPattern` and the binary stream's paint record kind `0x03`. A hatch needs no box: it is drawn on an element without area, as SVG draws it.
- **A hatch is a fill.** A stroke's `width` is its line's, so the stroke node has no hatch keys; a stroke may still name a declared hatch.
- **The print theme's role fills are hatched** — `:good` and `:warning` lines at 45° and 135°, `:bad` a crosshatch, `:info` dots, `:neutral` solid — so a role keeps its meaning in grayscale. The role contracts read a hatch's color as a solid paint's.
- **A frame declares a paint written in place only when its tree draws it.** The theme's palette is among the styles the frame searches for inline paints, so hatched roles would otherwise put five patterns in every print chart. A declared paint is always declared; a compiled chart declares every one, because a later tick may draw a style the first did not.

*Rejected:* a hatch on a stroke with a second width key (`line_width`). A stroke is too thin to show a hatch, and two widths on one node is a trap. *Rejected:* a tile of the period rounded to whole pixels. A `pt(4)` hatch is 5.33 px, and a 5 px tile drifts a third of a pixel per period from the SVG beside it. *Rejected:* hatching every palette entry of the print theme. The series promise *distinct* and already differ by marker and dash; a role promises a meaning, which is what must survive the photocopy.

**Amended by #674** (work item 3 of #652; deferred here from #649's work item #657, D-172). **A shadow takes an offset and a color, and every backend draws an effect.**
- The `effect` part gains `offset` (`[dx, dy]`, `:length` members in #651's units — `em` against the element's font size, a `pct` `{:no_box, :offset}`) and `color` (a `:color`), projected to the element keys `effect_offset` and `effect_color`. Absent, a shadow is what it was: offset half its radius each way, black at 0.35.
- **SVG:** a shadow with an offset or a color has a filter of its own, `vis-effect-shadow-<radius>-<h>` keyed by a hash of the two, whose `feDropShadow` takes the offset and `flood-color` with `flood-opacity` 1 — a color given carries its own alpha. One without keeps its id and filter, byte for byte.
- **A canvas** sets `shadowColor` and the two offsets from them in the `:json`/`:js` commands, and the binary stream gains an `effect` record (`0x52`, version 2) carrying a shadow's radius, offset and color or a blur's radius. Before it the binary canvas dropped every effect.

*Rejected:* a color's opacity in a separate `effect.opacity`. A typed color already carries alpha (`alpha/2`), and a second opacity would multiply with it. *Rejected:* the effect inside the style record as more flag bits. The style record has one bit left, and a shadow is 17 bytes a reader sets as four properties: a record of its own reads as what it is.

## 176. D-176 — Motion is a part of the style: CSS moves SVG, the hooks move a canvas, both keyed by identity along one sampled curve

**Context.** #653, part 5 of the set shaped from Research #641 ("The style grammar, reshaped"), work item #676. A datum's style that changes — `:ok` to `:error`, into the board's `:selected` — jumps, though a scale's domain and a mark's readings ease (#360, #417). The acceptance of #653 settled four things: CSS interpolates two colors in OKLab when either is not a legacy sRGB color, so `:css` output writes a moving element's colors as `oklch()` and no `color-interpolation` hint is needed (modification 1); enter and exit go through the hooks, which draw outside the patched subtree (D-75, modification 2); canvas transitions run in the hook's frame loop keyed by identity and cost nothing when no element has one (modification 3); and three work items, of which this is the first.

**Decision.**
- **`transition` is a style part** (spec/14 §3.10), merged, stacked and defaulted as every part is: `duration` and `easing` for all three moving properties — the fill, the stroke and the opacity — or per property, `%{fill: %{duration: 400}}`. The easing is a `Visualize.Ease` name, the vocabulary a scale's and a mark's `transition` already use. `Style.resolve/3` writes it on the element as the list of `{property, duration, easing}` it moves.
- **One curve, sampled.** Every backend eases along the same 21 samples of the `Visualize.Ease` curve (`Visualize.IR.Motion.samples/1`): SVG as CSS `linear(…)`, a canvas hook by reading them piecewise linearly. CSS has no cubic in-out of `Visualize.Ease`'s polynomial, and the hooks have no copy of the Elixir functions, so the server's curve is the one both draw.
- **`:css` output: a class and a rule.** Each distinct transition is a class `vis-transition-<h>` and one rule in a `<style>` first in the frame's group, applied to the element and, through `:where()`, its descendants, so a mark's group moves its children and a child's own class wins; the CSS properties are listed, never `all`, which would also move geometry. A moving element's colors, and its descendants', are written `oklch()` — a literal converted, a slot or a transform as the relative `oklch(from … l c h / alpha)`, which a stylesheet still overrides — so the browser interpolates in OKLab.
- **Identity.** Every element of a mark that moves — its stack carries a motion part, or names a style by data while any style the frame knows carries one — carries `data-vis-id`, `<frame>.<mark>.<identity>`: the declared identity (D-167), else the board's inference (spec/15 §13.2), else the element's ordinal. A canvas keys what it shows by it.
- **A canvas moves in its hook.** The `:json` commands and the binary stream wrap every element carrying `data-vis-id` in a motion span, one whose style moves nothing with an empty list, so the hook knows what each identity shows before a stream moves it (`motion`/`motionEnd`, the records `0x53`/`0x54` under version 2, the binary record carrying the JSON of the command's arguments). `VisualizeMotion` keeps per identity, draw and property the value shown, starts a motion from it when the target changes, mixes colors in OKLab, and the hook replays the kept stream in `requestAnimationFrame` while any motion runs.
- **Reduced motion is the browser's**: a `!important` media rule in the frame's `<style>`, and the media query read by the hooks at each replay. A design cannot override it.

**Rejected.**
- **An inline `style="transition: …"` on each element.** A hundred bars would carry a hundred copies of a 200-character easing, and the reduced-motion rule would need `!important` against each; a class per distinct transition is the `defs` pattern again.
- **CSS's `ease-*` keywords** as the vocabulary. They are cubic Béziers the hooks would need a solver for, and they are not the curves a scale's transition already names; one vocabulary, sampled, draws the same on both backends.
- **`all` as the transitioned property.** In SVG 2 `x`, `y`, `width`, `r` are properties too, so a style transition would move geometry the mark's own motion (#417) owns, twice.
- **Writing every color of every chart as `oklch()`.** It would change every golden for charts that never move; only a moving element needs it.
- **Keying a canvas transition by draw order.** A row inserted before the others would move every element after it from its neighbor's color; identity is what the change happened to.
- **A JavaScript copy of `Visualize.Ease`.** Twenty-five functions written twice drift; 21 samples are exact at every sample and within the curve's own curvature between.

**Consequences.**
- A design without a motion part draws byte for byte as before: no `data-vis-id`, no class, no `<style>`, no span.
- A raster, a `:js` script and the incremental canvas's strips draw the end state.
- spec/14 §21.6's "no hook animates on its own" now has this one exception, stopped by the same media query.
- The next work items amend this decision with `animation` and with `enter` and `exit`.

**Amended by #677** (work item 2 of #653). **A style may animate, and reduced motion stops it.**
- **`animation` is a style part** (spec/14 §3.11): a `name` — `:pulse`, `:blink`, `:shake`, `:spin`, or a string naming the host's keyframes — with `duration`, `easing`, `iterations` and `delay`. A state's style carries it, so an element runs it exactly while its data choose that state.
- **The built-ins are data**, `Visualize.IR.Motion.keyframes/1`: offsets and the values of `opacity`, `x`, `y`, `rotate`, `scale`. SVG writes them as `@keyframes` with the individual transform properties; the motion span carries them to the canvas hooks, which hold no copy. Each segment between keyframes is eased by `easing`, as CSS does, on both backends.
- **An animated element is wrapped** in `:css` output, `<g class="vis-animation-<h>">`, so a spin turns about the element's box as drawn, its own transform included; on a canvas the span's box is the element's as drawn for the same reason, and every span saves and restores the context.
- **Host keyframes** are a string name: the host's stylesheet defines them for SVG, `VisualizeMotion.register/2` for a canvas. The name must be a CSS identifier (`{:unsafe, :animation}`), since it is written into a stylesheet.
- **Reduced motion** stops every animation: `animation: none !important` under the media query, and the hooks run none while it matches.

*Rejected:* the class on the element itself. CSS composes `transform-origin` outside an element's `transform` attribute, so a symbol placed by `translate(540,0)` would spin about the plot's corner; the wrapper's box already holds the translation. *Rejected:* easing the whole iteration with one curve. CSS eases each keyframe segment, and a canvas that eased differently would pulse on another rhythm than the SVG beside it. *Rejected:* a copy of the built-in keyframes in the hooks. They would drift from the CSS the server writes; carried in the span, the server's table is the one both draw.

**Amended by #678** (work item 3 of #653). **Rows enter and exit by identity, and a deleted row can explode.**
- **`enter` and `exit` are style parts** (spec/14 §3.12): `:fade` and `:grow` entering, `:fade`, `:shrink` and `:explode` leaving, or a host's keyframes; a row's identity (D-167) says which rows appeared and which are gone.
- **Enter is a CSS animation** on the inserted element's wrapper, as the acceptance settled; `MotionHook` restarts it where a positional patch handed an element to another identity. A canvas enters an identity it has not drawn.
- **Exit goes through the hooks** (D-75): `MotionHook` copies each element with `data-vis-exit` before a patch and, for an identity the patch removed, plays the copy in an overlay on `document.body` over the chart, then removes it. A canvas keeps a way to redraw each identity with an exit and draws it as a ghost over the stream until its exit ends.
- **`:explode` is the hook's**: sixteen clipped pieces flown outward on fixed, deterministic paths, turned and faded, the same on SVG and on a canvas.
- **A board's cell and `Visualize.Components.Chart`** put `MotionHook` around a drawing that names identities, so a feed that loses a row explodes it with no host code.

*Rejected:* keeping the removed element in the drawing for its exit's duration (the server re-sending it, marked leaving). The server would need the client's clock to know when to stop, and every patch in between would carry rows that are no longer data. *Rejected:* `id` attributes for keyed patching. An id must be unique in the page and the frame cannot know what else the page holds; restarting an enter where an identity changed hands gives the same picture without it. *Rejected:* random explosion paths. A test could not hold them, and two renders of one deletion would differ.

## 177. D-177 — A generator is a call a design stores and the frame resolves at render: registered by module, checked by name when the chart is built, its result checked and never rescued

**Context.** #654, part 6 of the set shaped from Research #641 ("The style grammar, reshaped"), work item #680. A design holds what is known when it is built: a plain Elixir call in host code (`style: my_style()`) runs then and stores its result, so it cannot depend on the row, the viewer or anything learned later. The user's example: in a multi-tenant chat, every viewer sees each message in its sender's tenant theme. The acceptance of #654 settled four things: a generator that raises is a bug and is not rescued — only a wrong-typed result is a fault (modification 1); registration is by module, on the host's configuration and on the chart's options, `use Visualize.Generators` compiling `__generators__/0`, and an unknown name is a validator fault when the chart is built (modification 2); the tenant example runs in the examples app as a board with two tenants (modification 3); one work item (modification 4). Generators written as stored source text are #643's, not this decision's.

**Decision.**
- **A design stores a call**, `generate(:name, args)` → `{:generate, name, args}` (spec/14 §3.13): as a **style stack entry**, laid where it stands like a field entry and returning a style (a name, a node or a list), and in a **`:color` position** of a style — a solid paint's `color` or a paint written short, an effect's `color` — returning a typed color (D-173) or `:none`; a gradient's stops are held in `defs` before a frame draws, so a generator there is refused. The position decides what the result must be; the generator does not declare it.
- **Arguments are terms or field bindings**, read per datum by the binding rule of spec/14 §3.2, and the generator's last argument is the context, `%{theme, vars, assigns}`: the frame's theme, the variables' values at application, and the host's `assigns:` — the viewer's.
- **Defined in host modules.** `use Visualize.Generators` imports `defgenerator/2`, which is a plain `def` recorded under `__generators__/0` (`{name, arity}`, in definition order, a function of several clauses once). A generator is ordinary, tested Elixir.
- **Registered by module.** `config :visualize, generators: [...]` for the host, and `generators:` on `from_map/2`, `from_json/2`, `apply/2`, `compile/2` and a board's reads, realization and runtime (spec/15 §6, §8.2). `Visualize.Generators.registry/1` is the options' modules then the configuration's; a module without `__generators__/0`, or a `{name, arity}` two modules define, is an `ArgumentError` — a host's mistake, surfaced when it is made.
- **Checked when the chart is built.** The validator reports `{:undeclared, :generator, name}` for a name the registry lacks and `{:arity, :generator, name, n}` for one registered at another arity, at the generator's path, as it reports any undeclared reference; `apply/2` validates again with its own registry. A fragment validated alone does not check names, as it checks no reference.
- **The result is checked, never the call guarded.** A style result is checked as a `styles` entry is, against the frame's styles and its theme's color table; a color result as a typed color against the table. A result that fails is a fault at its site: it applies nothing — a stack entry lays no style, a leaf leaves its key unset so the layer under it shows — and is logged at `:warning` with the site's path, the generator's name and the result's fault. A generator that raises raises through the render with its own exception: nothing rescues it.
- **Cached per (name, arguments)** within a site's drawing: a mark memoizes its generators' results over its rows before it draws them, and each distinct choice of style is resolved once, as the field-chosen style's are (#644); furniture calls once. The context is fixed within a render, so a generator must be pure in its arguments and its context.
- **Precedence layer 6** (spec/14 §3.7): an author's stack lays the entry where written; a board's state entry goes after it; a color generator in a part sits at the layer of the style that holds it.
- **The frame carries the runtime.** `apply/2` builds the registry and the context's variables and assigns and gives them to every frame; a compiled chart keeps them, so every tick's frame calls the same generators. Every site the frame draws resolves them — a mark and its label per datum, an axis, a legend and a frame label once with no datum; where the library reads a style without drawing it (domain and type inference, the builder's stack panel) a generator lays nothing, as a field entry does.
- **JSON:** `{"$generate": [name, [args…]]}`; a name that is not plain words, or another shape, is left as read and refused by the validator — no atom is made from it (#645's rule).
- **`explain/2` attributes every generator** on the contribution it shaped: `generators`, each `%{name, args, at, result}`, the arguments as the generator received them, `at` `[]` for an entry and the leaf's path for a color, `result` the value or `{:fault, reason}`; a generator entry's contribution is layer `:generator`.

**Rejected.**
- **Rescuing a generator's raise into a fault.** A raise is a bug in trusted host code; turning it into a logged fault hides it on a page that still draws (the project's rule: no try/rescue). The acceptance said so.
- **Registration by name in the design**, or a `generators` map in the design. A design is untrusted (D-172, #660) and must never name code; it names a generator the host has registered.
- **An unknown name found at render.** It would draw a chart with a hole in it and say so only in a log; the registry is known when the chart is built, so the validator says so then, at its path.
- **A process-wide cache.** A cache that outlives a render must be invalidated when the viewer, the theme or a variable changes, and a cache keyed by those is the per-render memo again with a lifetime to manage. The per-site memo needs no state outside the drawing.
- **Generators at any leaf.** A width or a dash computed per tenant is expressible as a style generator returning `%{stroke: %{width: …}}`; one call shape per position — a style or a color — keeps the result check exact.
- **A style result holding a generator or an inline gradient.** The first would recurse without bound; the second would draw a gradient the frame did not hold before it drew (spec/14 §3.2). Both are faults of the result.

**Consequences.**
- A design without a generator draws byte for byte as before: no runtime is consulted, and the per-datum path is taken only by a mark whose stack or styles hold a generator or a field entry.
- `%Visualize.Chart.Frame{}` gains `generated`, the runtime; `Visualize.Chart.Compiled` carries it.
- spec/14 §3.7's layer 6 is implemented; every layer of the precedence order now is.
- #655's builder shows a generator as a stack entry and a color, and #643's stored-source generators, when they come, are a second kind of registration behind the same stored call.

**Amended by #681** (D-181). **A board's export runs its generators as the board does.** A board's snapshot export (spec/15 §12) resolved `generate(...)` with the configuration's generators and no assigns, so a generator registered only on the board was an unknown name in the export and a viewer-dependent one drew for nobody. `Snapshot.views/2` and the `Export` functions take `generators:` and `assigns:`, and `Board.Server.export/3` passes the board's own; the layer-6 resolution above is unchanged.

## 178. D-178 — The builder edits a style in its parts: a section per part, the paint's type first, a control named by its path, a write that is only what was set; a named style is a site of the design

**Context.** #655, part 7 of the set shaped from Research #641 ("The style grammar, reshaped"), work item #682. After parts 1–6 a style is compound parts (D-172) with typed colors (D-173), defaults and units (D-174), paints on every backend (D-175), motion (D-176) and generators (D-177), but the builder still edited the 29 flat keys of version 2 through a hidden schema kind (`describe(:flat_style)` behind `Builder.FormSchema`), its fill as a "fill style" that declared a gradient under `defs`. Two rules of spec/14 also disagreed: §18.14 wrote a created style into the selected layer, §19.11 said a declaration in place is values and a name is the library's. #655's acceptance settled the second: an override writes `styles.<name>` in the design, never into a layer (modification 2).

**Decision.**
- **One section per part.** A `:style` node's form is the whole element's keys, then one fieldset per compound part in the schema's order — fill, stroke, font, text, effect, marker, curve, transition, animation, enter, exit — each part's controls its own kind's keys (`Schema.describe/1` of `:paint`, `:stroke`, …), a node-typed key a nested fieldset and a list of nodes rows. The form keeps no list of style keys.
- **The paint's `type` is its first control** and decides which keys follow; choosing one replaces what only the old type meant, by §3.5's merge rule, and a gradient chosen over a color starts from it (two stops, 0.8 to 0.1).
- **A control is named by its path** (`stroke.width`, `fill.stops.1.color`, `transition.fill.duration`) and read back through the schema (`StyleForm.locate/2`), never through a table of names; the suffixes `.slot`, `.range`, `.other` keep their meaning.
- **A write is the part it is in, computed from the node it lands in** — the layer's node, or the site's local style — so a local edit holds only what was set (`%{stroke: %{width: 7}}`). A node found in the flat keys is rewritten in its parts by its first edit, so no stored node mixes the two forms.
- **A part the element does not draw is grayed** with a note where the form knows the element's kind (a mark's site, a label); a named style, shared between kinds, grays nothing.
- **The hidden flat-key form is removed**: `Builder.FormSchema`, `Builder.Fills` and the key spec's `group` (with `Schema.group/2` and `groups/1`) go. `:flat_style` stays described for the migration, which reads it.
- **A named style is a site of the design.** Creating one is a style site keyed by the name at the chart's top level, never a write into the selected layer's fragment; an existing name is opened, not redeclared. §18.14 and §19.11 now say the same.

**Rejected.**
- **A form per flat key mapped onto parts.** It would keep the 29-key vocabulary in the builder forever and hide what a part is: a paint's type, which no flat key has.
- **Writing the resolved part into the local style.** It would copy the entries beneath into the site, so a later change to the named style would no longer show through.
- **Writing a created style into the selected layer** (the old §18.14). The layer may be the library's, its fragment gains a declaration a person did not place, and a declaration in place is supposed to be visible as a site (§19.11).
- **Graying a named style's parts by the kinds that use it.** A style is shared; the set of kinds naming it changes with every edit elsewhere.

**Consequences.**
- Builder goldens change where a style form is drawn; chart goldens do not.
- A gradient written by the builder is a paint in place (§3.2), not a `defs` entry named for the style.
- #683–#686 build on the form: the color picker and its transform chain, units and the defaults editor, the palette panel, the stack editor and explain in the inspector.

**Amended by #683** (work item 2 of #655). **A color is picked by name and built as a chain.**
- **The chooser's patches are the theme's color table by name**, grouped by use — series, roles, furniture, the theme's own colors, the CSS names folded — each in the color the theme gives it. A name picked writes `color(:name)`, the typed form (D-173), never a bare atom.
- **A typed transform is a chain**: a base and the steps applied to it, in the order they read in a pipe, each previewed as the color reached so far through the design's theme. Steps are added at the end, changed and removed one at a time; `blend` makes the color the first operand of a blend, whose operands are chains of their own at their paths.
- **A picked color replaces the base and keeps the steps**, so a person changes the hue of `color(:red) |> shade(700) |> alpha(0.8)` without rebuilding it.
- *Rejected:* a text box for the color expression. It is the term a person would have to learn to spell, and the reader would need a second grammar; the chain is the term, shown and built by its parts. *Rejected:* inserting steps anywhere. Order matters in a pipe; appending and removing say every pipe there is, one gesture at a time.

**Amended by #684** (work item 3 of #655). **Sizes in units, defaults where their containers are.**
- **A size is a number and a unit select** — user units, px, pt, pc, mm, cm, in, em, % — writing `{n, unit}` or the bare number; `%` only where §3.9 gives the size a box, and no unit at an opacity, from one table the validator and the builder share (`Validator.Units.units/2`).
- **A defaults node is the style form's**: its parts, then one fieldset per mark kind it has an entry for, kinds added from a select and dropped by ×. A design's and a frame's are edited on the frame's page; a layer's or a group's in the site's context, written into the composite's own `defaults`.
- **The builder composes a group's defaults as the flattening does**: `Stack.placed/2` lays a group's `defaults` under what its objects place, so the preview matches what Save stores and Deploy flattens.
- *Rejected:* all fourteen mark kinds shown open in every defaults node. A node with an entry for one kind would be a page of empty fieldsets; an entry is added when it is wanted.

**Amended by #685** (work item 4 of #655; the redo of #639). **The palette panel in parts, and palette names resolved in the design's theme.**
- **The panel lists the theme's palette beneath the design's styles**, in spec/08 §7.7's five groups — style series, roles, states, furniture, modifiers — each entry previewed as a sample shape, its stroke and an `Aa` label resolved through the theme the preview draws with; states and modifiers over a neutral sample.
- **A shadowed entry is marked** on both rows; selecting it opens the design's entry.
- **Override writes `styles.<name>` in the design**: a style site keyed by the name holding the theme's entry, as §18.14 creates a style — the acceptance's rule, here applied.
- **References are grouped**: a style select and the stack's Add offer the design's names, the palette's groups and the library's ids as `<optgroup>`s.
- **The context panel resolves names against the design's theme**, not the default theme (#639's finding).
- *Rejected:* overriding with an empty node, which would replace the theme's look with nothing; and writing the override into the selected layer (the old §18.14, #639's draft).

**Amended by #686** (work item 5 of #655). **Every stack entry is made in the builder; an element's look is explained in the inspector.**
- **The style stack panel makes every entry §3.6 allows**: names (grouped, #685), `{:field, f}` from the site's data columns, `generate(name, args)` from the generators the builder knows (its `generators` assign and the host's configuration, checked for arity), and the local node; each row says what it is. Rows move by drag and by `ArrowUp`/`ArrowDown`; the local entry stays last.
- **The inspector explains the selected element's look** with `Visualize.Chart.explain/2` of the preview's chart: per part, the chain of contributions with their layer, container, style and `extends` path, field and value, generators and their results, and the keys set.
- *Rejected:* a text box for a whole stack. The panel's rows already are the stack; a literal for the whole would be a second editor of the same value. *Rejected:* explaining with a sample datum. A field entry would then show one row's choice as if it were the element's; the inspector says a field entry is per row instead.

## 179. D-179 — A canvas leaf's transform and state are its own: a leaf that sets either is drawn inside save/restore, on every canvas stream

**Context.** #679, found by #653 (work item #678).
