The canvas encoders wrote an element's transform and style as a prefix of its commands and wrapped only groups and clips in `save`/`restore`, as if a group always stood around a transformed element. A leaf's `translate`/`rotate` therefore stayed applied when its next sibling was drawn: each symbol of a scatter is a path placed by a translate (`Visualize.Chart.Mark.Shapes`), so on a canvas the second symbol landed at the sum of the first two offsets and the rest off the plot. Its style leaked the same way — a translucent leaf left `globalAlpha` for the next, and a leaf's own fill replaced the group's color its unstyled sibling should fill with. The JSON, the script and the binary canvas all did it; `CanvasIncremental` encodes through the binary encoder and did too. It went unnoticed because the parity tests drew untransformed leaves or a single one, the recording stub restored no property on `restore`, a moving mark's motion span happened to save and restore, and no `:js_resvg` test ran in CI.

**Decision.**
- **A leaf with a non-empty setup is scoped.** On the `:json`/`:js` commands (spec/09 §3.3.1) and the binary stream (§4.4), a leaf that writes a transform or style state — a style record, text style, a transform — is `save`, setup, geometry, draws, `restore`. A leaf that writes neither has no pair. Groups and clips are unchanged.
- **Style state is scoped with the transform**, because the sibling test showed it leaks: alpha, colors, width, blend, effect and font are a leaf's as much as its transform.
- **The hooks are unchanged.** Each reader already executes `save`/`restore` and scopes its fill and stroke paints with them.
- **The stub behaves as a browser's context**: `save`/`restore` scope its properties, and every draw call is recorded with the transform and state it ran in, so a test asserts what each element was drawn with, not only which calls were made.
- **`verify:hooks` runs the `:js_resvg` tests**: it downloads the pinned resvg from the `.resvg` job the `test` job extends too, and runs `mix test --only js --include js_resvg` (spec/12 §4); `mix vis.check` mirrors it.

**Rejected.**
- **Undoing a leaf's transform with its inverse after the draw.** It is cheaper than a pair for a translate, but a singular matrix has no inverse, a rotation's inverse accumulates rounding, and it leaves the style state leaking.
- **Wrapping every leaf.** A leaf with nothing to scope gains two commands and nothing else; a line mark's paths have their style on the group.
- **Leaving style state unscoped until #675.** The sibling test fails on alpha and fill today, and #675's per-paint alpha is set inside the same pair.

**Consequences.**
- A transformed or styled leaf costs one `save` and one `restore`, two bytes on the binary stream. No golden changes: the charts and builder goldens hold SVG.
- #675 sets each paint's alpha inside a leaf's pair.
- `test/visualize/backend/canvas_scope_test.exs` is the guard: siblings on three streams, and a symbol scatter against resvg in CI.

## 180. D-180 — A paint's own opacity is drawn on every canvas: its draw at the element's alpha times the paint's, and the binary stream's version 3 carries it

**Context.** #675, recorded as out of scope in D-175 and found by #652 (work item #672); work item #690. Only the SVG backend wrote `fill_opacity` and `stroke_opacity`. `grep` over the canvas encoders and hooks at c36a6198 found neither key: the canvas command set never carried a paint's own opacity, only the element's `opacity` as `globalAlpha`. So a `fill: %{color: …, opacity: 0.4}`, a theme's translucent band or a percentile band's 0.2/0.4 fills (`Mark.Shapes`) drew **opaque** on every canvas and translucent in SVG. It went unseen because the goldens that hold `fill-opacity` (the charts, designs and builder goldens) are SVG, the gallery charts that use one render through SVG, and the canvas parity checks — the hook harness and the raster comparisons — drew only opaque paints. The parity test this decision adds found the same gap for `stroke_dasharray` on both canvases and for the cap and the join on the binary canvas, which reads the paint keys from one table.

**Decision.**
- **The draw is at the element's alpha times the paint's.** On the `:json`/`:js` commands, a fill's draw is preceded by `set_globalAlpha` of `o · f` and followed by `set_globalAlpha` of `o`, `o` the effective `opacity`, `f` the effective `fill_opacity`; a stroke's likewise, a line's and a text's included (spec/09 §3.3.3). A gradient's stops keep their own alpha, which the context multiplies.
- **The binary stream's version 3** adds a `paint_state` record (`0x55`) after a style record that sets a paint's opacity, a cap, a join or a dash (spec/09 §4.3.17), under D-175's version-record rule: only such a stream carries it and begins `0x00 0x03`; every other stream is byte for byte what it was. The hook keeps the two alphas as state `save`/`restore` scope, multiplies `globalAlpha` at each `fill`/`stroke` and sets it back.
- **The dash, the cap and the join ride with them.** The parity guard is driven by the fill and stroke parts' keys (`Style.Parts.paint_keys/0`); a canvas that dropped any key SVG writes fails it, so `stroke_dasharray` becomes `setLineDash` on every canvas and the cap and join reach the binary one.
- **A canvas's `globalAlpha` stays the element's** between draws: the paint's opacity belongs to its draw alone, as SVG's attributes do.

**Rejected.**
- **Folding the opacity into the color's alpha**, as the SVG-element encoder does. It does not reach a gradient or a hatch, whose colors are built in the hook, and a color that is `currentColor` has no alpha to fold into.
- **Multiplying in the hook for `:json`** with a command of its own. The server knows the effective style; a `set_globalAlpha` of the product is a Canvas assignment every reader, `:js` included, executes as it is.
- **More style-record flag bits.** The style record has one bit left (D-175, #674); five fields of mixed widths read as a record of their own.
- **Leaving the dash, cap and join to a later issue.** The guard the acceptance asks for fails on them, and a dashed grid line drawn solid on a canvas is the same class of defect as an opaque band.

**Consequences.**
- A stream with a translucent paint, a dash, a cap or a join is version 3; a page still running a version 2 hook throws on it rather than drawing it wrong.
- `test/visualize/backend/paint_opacity_test.exs` is the guard: the key-driven parity test, the hooks on three streams, and a translucent band against resvg (`:js_resvg`, in CI since D-179).

## 181. D-181 — A board's export draws with the board's generators and assigns: explicit options over the running board's, over the configuration

**Context.** #681, found by #654 (work item #680, D-177) and left out of it; work item #691. A board's live rendering resolves `generate(...)` with the `generators:` and `assigns:` it was started with (`Visualize.Board.Server`, `Board.realize/4`), but its snapshot export (spec/15 §12) drew every cell through `Snapshot.views/2` with neither: only `config :visualize, generators:` and no assigns. A board started with generators that are not in the configuration exported every generated style as an unknown name — each cell its fault's panel — and a generator that reads the viewer, the tenant chat's "view as", exported as if there were no viewer. The export disagreed with what the viewer saw.

**Decision.**
- **`Snapshot.views/2` and every export take `generators:` and `assigns:`**, passed to each cell's drawing as the cell process passes them (`Board.Draw`). Their defaults are today's: the configuration's generators and `%{}`.
- **The snapshot does not record them.** A snapshot is data that reopens anywhere (§12.2–§12.3); a module list is the host's and an assigns map is a viewer's, neither a property of the frozen board, and neither has a codec. A reader passes them, as it passes `system:` (D-156).
- **`Board.Server.export/3` passes the running board's own** `generators:`, `assigns:` and `system:` with its snapshot, in one call to the board process, and draws with `Export.svg/2`, `png/2`, `pages/2` or `html/2`. An option given wins: the precedence is the explicit option, then the server's, then the configuration and no assigns.
- **The gallery's export buttons use it**, so the demo board's exports are drawn as its cells are.

**Rejected.**
- **Fields on the snapshot that its codecs do not write.** `from_json(to_json(s))` would no longer be `s`, and a viewer's assigns would ride in a value meant to be sent to someone else.
- **Calling the generators when the snapshot is taken and freezing their results.** A snapshot redraws at any width and in any theme (§12.2), and a generator reads the theme; frozen results would be right only for the theme they were taken in.
- **`system:` left out of `Server.export/3`.** It is the same kind of value — the viewer's, held by the board, not recorded by the snapshot — and an export drawn in another unit system than the live board disagrees with it the same way.

**Consequences.**
- A snapshot exported with the board's generators and assigns draws each cell byte for byte as the live cell does (`test/visualize/board/export_test.exs`).
- A host exporting a snapshot it reopened elsewhere passes its own `generators:` and `assigns:`, as it passes `system:`.
- D-177 is amended; spec/15 §12 says the options and the precedence.

## 182. D-182 — A one-key `$` object is always a tag: one table writes and reads them, an unknown or retired tag is refused at its path, and data's `$` keys are escaped

**Context.** #645, surfaced by #644 (MR !450); work item #696. The design codec wrote tagged terms as one-key `$` objects, but on reading, a one-key `$` object whose tag it did not know fell through to the plain-map path: a misspelled tag (`"$feild"`), a retired one (`"$styles"`, removed by #644) or one from a newer library loaded silently as data, and in a color-range position passed validation as a color map. The fall-through was not decided anywhere: the codec's rule that decoding never fails on a wrong shape is deliberate (the validator names the fault), but in a `:term` position the generic map clause simply read the object as a map. The deep dive found the converse too: a `:term` value is any value, so a host's data map may carry a `$` key — and one that spells a tag, `%{"$atom" => "x"}`, came back as the atom `:x`. No test read an unknown tag, wrote a `$` key in data, or tied the tags written to the tags read. Board (`$datum`) and snapshot (`$absent`) documents had tags of their own, in separate codecs, with no shared list.

**Decision.**
- **One table** (`Visualize.Chart.Codec.Tags`) of every tag a JSON document of this library writes — design, fragment, board, snapshot, and the `$version` stamp — with the tags it has **retired** (each with what replaced it) and **renamed** (old → new). Encoders write a tag only through `Tags.tag/2`, which refuses at compile time a name the table does not list as live; the table is what the round-trip property test enumerates.
- **Every JSON reader holds the document to the table** after the version gate and the limits, before decoding: an unknown tag is `{:unknown_tag, tag}` and a retired one `{:retired_tag, tag, why}`, at the JSON path; a renamed tag is rewritten to its new name. In `Fragment.from_json/2` they are `{:error, {:tags, errors}}`, beside `{:limits, errors}`.
- **An escape for data.** Every map key the codecs write that starts with `$` gets one more `$`; a key starting `$$` reads with one fewer. A one-key object with a single-`$` key is then never data.
- **`$styles` is retired; `$scale` is not.** The retired list is built from what the encoder writes now, not from #644's list: `{"$scale": "min" | "max"}` is still written for a channel at its scale's own end (#554, #649).
- **Version 2 reads as it did.** A document before version 3 predates the escape, so its `$` keys are data: an unknown one-key `$` object in it is read as data, as before; known, retired and renamed tags are handled as in version 3.

**Rejected.**
- **Failing inside the decoder**, position by position. The decoder is type-directed and deliberately passes a wrong shape through for the validator; an unknown tag is wrong in every position, so one walk over the parsed document says it once, with the document's own path, before any atom is made.
- **Refusing every `$` key, with no escape.** A term is any host value; refusing a data column named `$price` would make a valid chart unwritable.
- **Escaping only one-key maps.** A rule that depends on a map's size changes a key's spelling when a column is added or dropped; escaping every `$` key is one rule.
- **Retiring `$scale` with `$styles`**, as #644's list had it. It is still written; retiring it would refuse the area baselines every version 3 design may hold.

**Consequences.**
- A misspelled, retired or future tag fails at its path, with its name, and never loads as data.
- Retiring or renaming a tag is a table entry: #642's `$colours` → `$colors` is the first rename. <!-- spelling: allow -->
- `test/visualize/chart/codec_tags_test.exs` is the guard: every live tag round-trips, its samples cover exactly the table's live tags, and any other `$` key is refused.
- spec/14 §11.2 says the rule; spec/15 §2.2 and §12.3 name the board's and the snapshot's tags as the table's.

## 183. D-183 — American spelling throughout: released names are renamed outright, stored names migrate on read, and the gate refuses a British form

<!-- spelling: allow -->
**Context.** #642; work items #697–#701. The library spelled its own names in British English — `colour`, `centre`, `grey`, `normalise`, `serialise`, `honour`, `labelled`, `realise` — in names, keys, docs and spec, while the user's rule (2026-10-14) is American English for everything we name and write. Most occurrences are prose and private names, but some are released: functions (`Board.realise/3`, `Color.Vision.recolour/2`, `Theme.colour_safe/0`, `Theme.colour_slots/1`), the built-in theme name `:colour_safe` a stored design holds, the JSON tag `$colours` a stored document holds, and the schema types a validator fault carries (`{:type, :colour}`). The style keys a stored design holds were already moved by the 2 → 3 step (D-172: a paint's `centre`, a stop's `colour`).

**Decision.**
- **Released public functions are renamed outright, with no `@deprecated` shims.** 0.7.0 is already a breaking release with a migration (schema v3) and the project is pre-1.0; a shim layer would keep both spellings in the surface for a release and the guard would have to allowlist them. The CHANGELOG carries a rename table, old → new.
- **Stored documents migrate on read.** `Migration.canonical/1` reads the theme name `:colour_safe` as `:color_safe` wherever a design, an inline theme (`name`, `dark`, `light`) or a theme document names it; the tag table of D-182 reads `$colours` as `$colors`. Writing always uses the new spelling, and an old document renders byte for byte as the same document written in the new names.
- **Everything else is renamed in place:** schema types and fault terms (`:colour` → `:color`, `:colour_map` → `:color_map`, `:theme_colour` → `:theme_color`), module and file names, builder event names, private names, docs, tests, spec, guides and the examples app.
- **What stays:** Elixir's own `behaviour` (`@behaviour`, a module that is an Elixir behaviour, prose about one), the CHANGELOG's past entries, and quoted third-party text — CSS's `grey` keywords, d3's scheme names, ARIA's `aria-labelledby`, a unit alias a host may type (`metre`) — and the old names a migration reads, marked so.
- **A recurrence guard in the gate**, with that allowlist (spec/12 §3).

**Rejected.**
- **Keeping the British names as `@deprecated` delegates for one minor release.** See above; the consumer adapts once, with the table.
- **Renaming the stored names without a migration.** A stored design naming `:colour_safe` would stop drawing in its theme, and a stored `$colours` range would be refused by D-182's reader.
- **Accepting both spellings everywhere** (a key `colour:` beside `color:`). Two names for one key is the ambiguity the style grammar removed (D-172).

**Consequences.**
- A host that calls a renamed function or matches `{:type, :colour}` changes the name, by the CHANGELOG's table. A host passing `theme: :colour_safe` in code passes `:color_safe`; a stored design that names it keeps working.
- New code and docs are held to American spelling by the gate.
<!-- spelling: end -->

## 184. D-184 — A table that is a function of nothing, or of the theme alone, is built once, and the builder's editing pass is held to a reduction budget

**Context.** #704; work item #706. Main's `examples:test` timed out in the board-mode builder test (`builder_live_test.exs:293`, ExUnit's 60 s) with the stack in `Theme.color_names/1` under `Builder.Chain.resolved/2` under `Builder.Picker.groups/1` under `Builder.Editing.editing/1`. Measured on that test's board, one editing pass cost about 5.6 million reductions and the test 18 s alone. An eprof of one pass found two tables rebuilt per call: `Chain.resolved/2` rebuilt, de-duplicated and sorted the theme's ~190 color names for every name the picker's groups resolved (213 rebuilds a pass), and `Schema.describe/1` rebuilt a node kind's key specs on every call — about 12,000 calls and 150,000 key specs a pass, the larger half of its work — while `Schema.key/2` scanned the rebuilt list. Every builder event derives the pass at least once and every render once more, so the test, which binds every component to every feed, paid it hundreds of times.

**Decision.**
- **A table that depends on nothing is a compile-time constant.** The schema's tables are built when the library compiles (`Visualize.Chart.Schema.Design`, `Schema.Frame`, `Schema.Mark`, sharing `Schema.Spec`), each with its keys by name, and `describe/1` and `key/2` look them up (spec/14 §14.2).
- **A membership test builds no list.** `Visualize.Theme.color_name?/2` answers `name in color_names(theme)` from the theme's slots, its own and the default's `colors` and a compile-time set of the CSS names (spec/08 §7.6). The picker's chain and groups and the legend's swatch use it.
- **A value that is a function of the theme alone is derived once per editing pass** and shared by every field that reads it: the picker's groups (spec/14 §19.10).
- **The editing pass has a reduction budget** (spec/12 §6): `Editing.editing/1` on the builder fixture's design with a color field selected, the second of two counts (#703), within a budget set from the measured cost after the fix with headroom.

**Rejected.**
- **Raising the test's timeout.** The test was near its limit because the code was quadratic; a longer timeout hides the next table rebuilt per call the same way.
- **A lazily filled cache (`:persistent_term`) of the schema's tables.** It would work, but it is shared mutable state whose first filler pays the cost — the kind #703 suspects of moving the gallery's counts — where a compile-time table is a constant of the code.
- **Memoizing the picker's groups across passes in the socket's assigns.** Once per pass is linear in the table and a few thousand reductions after the fix; carrying a derived value in assigns would need invalidating on every theme change.

**Consequences.**
- One editing pass on the board test's board costs a fraction of what it did, and the board-mode test runs well inside its timeout (numbers on #704).
- A node kind's table is changed in `Schema.Design`, `Schema.Frame` or `Schema.Mark`; a table may call any pure function at compile time, and none may read the application's environment, since a compile-time table could not see it.
- A per-name or per-field rebuild that returns fails `builder_cost_test.exs` by name.

## 185. D-185 — A scale's list range is a color position

**Context.** #710; work item #716. Since D-173 every color position of a stored design holds a typed color, and `Migration.canonical/1` reads a bare atom there as `{:color, atom}`. Building the 0.7.0 examples (#705) found one position that was not one: a scale's `range: [color(:red), color(:amber)]` was refused by the validator as `{:type, :extent}`, while `range: [:red, :amber]` passed, so the grammar's own canonical form was rejected where a color scale takes its colors, and the gallery's Colors chart (#707) chose a style per row instead. The cause is the schema: a range given as a list was typed `:extent`, the *domain's* type, whose entries are categories — numbers, strings, atoms — and which has no tuple in it. Neither `canonical/1` nor the validator could see a color there, so a bare atom stayed bare (D-173 wrote it down as "at render a bare atom in a scale's range is still read as a name"), a typed color was no category, and #664's tests, which reached every `:color`-typed key through the schema, never reached a key that was not typed so. The same gap let a `:sequential` scale take `range: [:red, :amber]` and then crash at render, interpolating `:red` as a hex string.

**Decision.**
- **A list range has its own type, `:range`** (spec/14 §1.4): each entry a position — a number, a `DateTime`, a variable or a fact — or a color position, a `:color` of every form that type takes. A scale's `range` is `{:one_of, [:range, :ramp, :color_map, :name]}`; the domain keeps `:extent`.
- **Its atoms are colors.** A range's entries are what the scale maps to, and nothing but a color is ever an atom there, so `canonical/1` types a bare atom `a` as `{:color, a}` as in every color position (spec/14 §9), the validator checks each entry that is no position as a `:color` at its own index — a name the table lacks is `{:undeclared, :color, name}` there — and the JSON codec writes a color entry by its tag.
- **A color scale given a list resolves its stops through the theme** (spec/14 §4.3): a `:sequential` or `:diverging` scale interpolates the literals of its entries, as it does a ramp's anchors.
- **A theme's ramp anchor may be a transform** (spec/08 §7.5), the one color position the guard found refusing one: it is measured as the literal it computes once each name in it is replaced by its literal, so it can no more reach itself than a name can.
- **One list of every color position guards the rule**: a test holds every color position — the style parts, the frame's background, a scale's list range and its map of colors, a theme's colors and ramps — checks each with a typed color and a transform through validation, canonicalization and render, and fails by name when the schema gains a color-typed key the list lacks (spec/12 §6).

**Rejected.**
- **Accepting typed colors in `:extent`.** The domain would accept colors it cannot use, and an atom there must stay a category, so canonicalization could not type a range's atoms without typing a domain's.
- **Typing a range by its scale's kind** (colors on a color scale, positions on a position scale). An ordinal or threshold scale is a color scale by what it is bound to, not by its kind, so the schema cannot know; a range's entries say what they are, a number or a color.
- **Leaving bare atoms bare in a range.** The design would hold two spellings of one color, and the validator would check one of them.

**Consequences.**
- `range: [color(:good), color(:warning), color(:bad)]`, a transform or a shade works in a scale range on every backend; `range: [:good, :warning, :bad]` reads, validates and draws as before, stored as the typed form.
- A range's atom that is no name of the theme's table, which passed before, is now `{:undeclared, :color, name}` at its index, as it is in a style.
- A stored design's JSON writes a range's colors as `{"$color": …}` where it wrote `{"$atom": …}`; the old form still reads.

## 186. D-186 — An in-place gradient is hoisted from the paint its style resolved to

**Context.** #711; work item #717. Building the 0.7.0 examples (#705), a style that `extends` one whose fill is a gradient written in place (`fill: %{type: :radial, …}`, D-172) and sets its own stops failed at render: "paint … is not declared under defs". Each style alone rendered. The frame held the gradients written in place in a table built from every style node **as written** — the named styles, the sites, the defaults — while an element's `fill` was `{:paint, name}` with `name` a hash of the **merged** paint: under a merge that keeps the gradient's `type`, the child's stops over the parent's geometry is a gradient neither node holds, so its name was in no table. The same gap broke a stack (`[:lit, :blue_stops]`), a gradient a defaults node lays under a site's stops, and a generator returning a style with a gradient. D-172's merge tests checked the merged node, never a render of it, and the gallery's paints were each written whole, so nothing drew a merged gradient until #707 tried, and wrote both looks in full instead.

**Decision.**
- **The element carries the resolved paint.** `Visualize.Chart.Style.resolve/3` projects a gradient written in place as `{:paint, node}`, the node the style resolved to without its opacity, and a declared paint as `{:paint, name}` as before (spec/14 §3.2).
- **The frame resolves the node it is given.** `Visualize.Chart.Frame.generate/2` turns `{:paint, node}` into its `Visualize.IR.Paint` under `vis-paint-<h>-p<h'>`, `<h'>` the hash of that node, and its `defs` declares every paint the drawn tree holds — declared ones always, as before (spec/14 §2.7). Hoisting therefore runs on what is drawn, so no merge can reach a paint the defs lack.
- **A color list with no type is stops** (spec/14 §3.1): one color with no type is already a solid, so `fill: %{color: [a, b]}` in a style that extends a gradient is that gradient in these colors — the plain way to derive one look from another, which the validator refused as no color.
- **A compiled chart's static defs cover what a later tick may draw**: every gradient of the named styles with their `extends` chains laid, of the sites and of the defaults, and every one of its first draw of every mark.

**Rejected.**
- **Computing every merge ahead.** A stack can hold field entries, so the merged paints are a product over the data's choices; a table of them is a guess where the drawn tree is the answer.
- **Looking an unknown name up by recomputing it.** The name is a hash; nothing can be recovered from it.
- **Projecting the bare paint map.** A map under `fill` is a part (§3.1); the tagged `{:paint, node}` keeps a projected style distinct from one in parts, and every place that passes a paint through untouched already matches `{:paint, _}`.

**Consequences.**
- A style may extend, stack or default a gradient and change its stops, its geometry or its type, on SVG and both canvases; the gallery's Paints chart derives its second incident look by `extends`.
- A host reading `Style.resolve/3`'s output sees `{:paint, node}` for an in-place gradient where it saw `{:paint, "p…"}`; the SVG and the canvas commands are byte for byte what they were for every gradient that drew before.
- A compiled chart whose later ticks reach a gradient only through a stack's field entry combined with another entry's gradient, which its first draw did not hold, draws that element on SVG with a `url(#id)` its static layer lacks; the canvas draws it, since it reads the paint from the element. *Answered by #721, D-190: the tick declares it.*

## 187. D-187 — Decomposing a design covers every design key, checked against the schema when the library compiles

**Context.** #713; work item #718. `Visualize.Chart.Composite.decompose/1`, which the builder uses to turn a design into one site per root (spec/14 §18.12), named the design's keys by hand: theme, meta, layout, interaction, frames, styles, sources, vars, defs, marks, labels. 0.7.0 added a design-level `defaults` (#651, D-174), which the list never gained, and made a `styles` entry a list of entries (#658), which the list reached but placed wrong — a style site whose body is a list was not recognized as a style, so flatten cascaded it as a design key `style` and the name vanished from `styles`. A design using either did not round-trip through the builder, and the gallery's Composition chart, which shows both, was kept out of the builder's templates (`Templates.not_templates/0`, #705). Every other gallery design round-tripped, which is why nothing failed: no test decomposed a design that used the new keys, and nothing tied the hand list to the schema.

**Decision.**
- **The design's keys are placed by a table checked against the schema.** `decompose/1` reads a table from each design key to its site rule — one positional site, a keyed site per entry, a site per element, or the frames' own rule — and the module raises when it compiles if `Visualize.Chart.Schema.describe(:design)` has a key, `version` aside, the table lacks, or the table a key the schema lacks. The schema's tables are compile-time constants (D-184), so the check costs the build nothing.
- **`defaults` is a positional site**, placed at the design key of its name, as `theme` and `meta` are (spec/14 §19.6).
- **A style site's body may be a list** (§3.6): flatten places it under the site's key as it places a node, and a style site with no key is keyless either way.
- **The round trip is tested over every design** the library's tests and the gallery hold (spec/12 §6), and the gallery has no design the builder cannot hold.

**Rejected.**
- **Deriving the site rules from the schema's types alone.** A `{:map, _}` key is keyed and a `{:list, _}` key per element, but `frames` decomposes its scales and axes into sites of their own, and the order the builder lists the sites in is a choice the types do not make; a table with a compile-time check says both and still cannot fall behind.
- **Keeping the list and adding the two keys.** It is the gap that let `defaults` be missed; the next design key would be missed the same way.

**Consequences.**
- A design with a design-level `defaults` or a list-valued style imports into the builder and flattens back unchanged; the Composition chart is a builder template like every other gallery design.
- A design key added to the schema fails the build until `decompose/1` places it.

## 188. D-188 — A chart's root takes its id from the host, and a board gives each cell one

**Context.** #712; work item #719. A chart's root id is content-derived: `vis-` and a hash of its title and description (D-56, D-142), the stem of its title's and description's ids and, since #668 (D-173), the selector of the automatic dark-mode rule its root carries. Two charts on one page with the same title and description therefore share an id: their accessible names collide, and the dark-mode rule of one restyles the other. Building the 0.7.0 examples (#705) met it in the Colors chart's dark-mode panel, which draws one design twice — with the rule and with `color_scheme: false` — beside the gallery's own drawing of it, and gave each drawing a different title so the three roots differed. A host had no way to say which chart is which. A board met the same thing in every cell: each cell's root was named by its title and summary, so two cells showing one component shared it.

**Decision.**
- **`Visualize.Chart.generate/2` and `render/2` take `id:`** (spec/14 §4.7): the root's id, its title's and description's id stem, and the selector its scoped rules are written against. Without it the id is content-derived as before, so nothing that renders today changes; an id given is the root's even with no title or description.
- **A board names each cell's root** (spec/15 §8.2, §8.3): `<board id>-<cell>-chart`, the board's id the board process's `id:` option, by default `vis-board-` and a hash of the board's `meta` and cell names — a value that does not change while the board runs, and that a snapshot of it derives the same way (§12.2), so a snapshot's cell is still its live drawing byte for byte. The suffix keeps the root apart from the cell's own DOM id, `<board id>-<cell>` (§9.1), when a host gives both the component's id.
- **The builder's board preview names each cell's root** `<id>-<cell>` (spec/14 §22.6).

**Rejected.**
- **Hashing more of the chart into the default** (its data, its design). Two drawings of one design over one dataset, which the dark-mode panel is, would still collide; only the host knows that two roots are two.
- **Making `id:` required.** Nothing about one chart on a page needs it, and every render today would break.
- **A random default.** A render would no longer be a function of its input: goldens, diffs and a snapshot's equality with its live drawing all rest on the same input drawing the same bytes.

**Consequences.**
- The examples' dark-mode panel draws its two roots with ids of their own and the design's own title.
- A board cell's root id changes from the title-and-summary hash to `<board id>-<cell>-chart`; a host that styled cells by the old id styles them by the new.

## 189. D-189 — The builder types its pool and its catalog once per change of the data

**Context.** #714; work item #720. After #706 (D-184), measuring `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Editing.editing/1` on the examples' board-mode test board found `Visualize.Data.Table.column_types/1` called 161 times in one pass: the host pool's 76 sources typed twice — once under the channel columns (`Scales.columns/2`), once for the data page — about 232,000 reductions each, and the 9 catalog feeds' preview rows once more for the binding's row check (§22.8, D-153), about 21,000. The pass cost about 2.61 million reductions, so the typing was about a fifth of it. The types change only when the host's `sources` or `catalog` change, which is never during an edit; every event and every render paid them again.

**Decision.**
- **The builder types the host's data when it arrives.** `update/2` keeps, beside the host's `sources` and `catalog`, the column types of each source and of each catalog feed's preview rows, with the data they were read from. A pass reads them when its `sources` and `catalog` are the very terms they were typed from (`===`, which the VM answers at once for the same term), and types them itself otherwise, so a socket whose data was changed some other way is never read with stale types (spec/14 §19.10).
- **What moves with the tick is typed in the pass**: the signals' rows, a handful of sources.
- **Within a pass the pool is typed once** and every reader — the channel columns, the data page, the faults — takes that one.
- **The board-mode pass has a budget** (spec/12 §6), beside the composite one.

**Rejected.**
- **A cache keyed by the data in shared state** (`:persistent_term`, an ETS table). It is mutable state outside the socket, whose first filler pays and whose entries outlive the data; D-184 rejected it for the schema's tables for the same reason.
- **Typing from the catalog's schemas instead of its rows.** D-153 checks a binding against what the preview's rows show; the schema is checked separately, and the row check exists for the rows that disagree with it.
- **Caching per pass only.** It halves the pool's cost and leaves the rest paid on every event, though the data has not changed.

**Consequences.**
- A board-mode pass on the examples' board costs about 2.13 million reductions where it cost 2.61 million, and on the cost test's board about 405,000 where it cost 1,520,000; every builder event in any mode stops paying for the host pool's types (numbers on #714).
- A host that hands the builder new `sources` or a new `catalog` pays the typing once, in `update/2`.
- The cell previews, which realize and draw each cell, are the largest remaining part of a board-mode pass; they draw, and are left as they are. *Answered by #723, D-191: they are kept by their inputs.*

## 190. D-190 — A compiled chart's tick declares the paints it first draws

**Context.** #721; work item #728. D-186 hoisted an in-place gradient from the paint its style resolved to, so a frame declares every merged paint its drawn tree holds, and a compiled chart's static `defs` cover every gradient of the named styles (with their `extends` chains), the sites, the defaults and the first draw of every mark. It recorded one gap: a stack whose field entry is laid over another entry's gradient (`style: [:a, {:field, :look}]`, `:a` a radial gradient and the style a row names changing its stops) reaches a merged gradient that no style holds whole. When no row of the compilation's data names that style, the static `defs` lack it, and the later tick whose row does draws the element on SVG with a `url(#id)` declared nowhere — no fill at all — while both canvases draw it, since they read the paint from the element. The #717 property test rendered charts whole through `Visualize.Chart.render/2`, whose frame declares what it draws, and never through `Compiled.step/3` with a value arriving after compilation.

**Decision.**
- **A tick declares what it draws that the compiled `defs` lack.** Every SVG output of a compiled chart — `step/3`, `svg/2`, `tick/3`'s `svg`, `render/2`'s `dynamic_svg`, a re-realized frame's static layer — holds a second `defs` element of every paint and effect filter its own elements use whose id the compiled `defs` do not declare (spec/14 §2.7, §12.4), after the compiled `defs` where the output carries them and first where it does not.
- **It is computed per output, from the drawn elements, and kept nowhere.** The paints are already resolved on the elements (D-186), so the definitions are the elements' own; nothing about the compiled chart changes between ticks.
- **An output that draws nothing new is unchanged byte for byte.**
- **The static `defs` declare what the first draw holds, and no longer every in-place paint of the design's styles.** That table (#652, D-186's last bullet) existed to cover later ticks, which now declare for themselves; it also built every named style's in-place paint on its own, so a style that is only part of a paint — `%{fill: %{type: :hatch, pattern: :lines}}`, meant to be stacked over one with a color — raised at compilation (`key :color not found`), though the design validated and its frame rendered.

**Rejected.**
- **Declaring every reachable merge up front.** D-186 rejected it: a stack with field entries reaches a product over the data's values, and the drawn tree is the answer.
- **Growing the compiled `defs` as ticks draw new paints.** The static layer is drawn once (spec/14 §12.4) and a tick's SVG layer replaces the last one, so a definition added to compiled state reaches the page only if some layer carries it each tick — which is this decision — and the state would make a tick's output depend on the ticks before it.
- **Re-rendering the static layer when a new paint appears.** It moves furniture the page holds still, for what is a property of one element.

**Consequences.**
- A compiled chart's SVG never references an undeclared paint: a board cell or a run draws a merged gradient a later row selects, and a design whose styles hold part of a paint compiles.
- A compiled chart's static layer no longer declares in-place paints its first draw does not use; a page that read one of those ids from the static layer before any tick drew it finds it in the tick that draws it.
- A tick that first draws a merged paint carries its definition in that tick's SVG on every tick that draws it — a few hundred bytes per paint, in the tick's payload `bytes`.
- Two SVG layers on one page may define the same id once each; they define the same paint, since the id is a hash of it.

## 191. D-191 — The board mode keeps each cell's preview and the board's faults by their inputs

**Context.** #723; work item #730. After #714 (D-189) a board-mode pass of `Visualize.Chart.Builder.Editing.editing/1` on the examples' board cost about 2.12 million reductions. Measured by part: the cell previews — every cell realized and drawn on every pass, whatever the event changed — about 1.4 million; `Visualize.Board.validate/2` of the whole board about 0.3 million; the cells' slots (`Board.required_sources/2`, each cell alone) about 0.27 million; the variables the components declare, read through the store three times over, about 0.05 million each time. On the cost test's board the pass was about 405,000. Every builder event and every render pays a pass, and none of these changes unless the board, the store's components, the binding, the data, the catalog or the theme does. #704 and #714 were the same defect class: work that is a function of an unchanged input, done per event.

**Decision.**
- **A cell's preview is kept under its inputs** (spec/14 §22.6): the cell, the board's shared state, its component flattened through the store, its pool, the theme, the catalog and its slots' feeds, its type refusal. A pass draws only the cells whose inputs differ from the kept ones, in one `Boards.preview/3` call over those cells.
- **The board's faults and declared variables are kept under the board and its components flattened** (§22.5); **each cell's slots under the cell and its component**. The components are flattened once per pass — what the store says of each, nested references and all — so a store whose entries change behind the same store term (a module store, `:persistent_term`, a database) is seen; this costs one flatten per component the board names, about 6,000 reductions each.
- **The kept map is an assign**, refreshed after every builder event and every `update/2` (`BoardMode.keep/1`), compared with `==`, which the VM answers at once for the same term. A pass whose inputs are not the kept ones computes what they make itself, so nothing is read stale (D-189's rule).
- **The catalog's sample rows are made when the host gives the catalog** (D-189's `update/2`), ending at that instant, rather than on every pass at its own instant: a preview kept by its pool must not be drawn again because the clock moved.
- **Budgets** (spec/12 §6): the board-mode pass at 80,000 (measured about 36,000), and an event touching one cell with its pass at 260,000 (about 175,000; every cell drawn again is about 285,000), asserted to draw that cell's preview alone.

**Rejected.**
- **A process or a table holding previews** (an ETS cache, `:persistent_term`). State outside the socket outlives what it was made from and is filled by whoever asks first; D-184 and D-189 rejected it for the same reasons.
- **Keying on the store term.** A module store holds its entries elsewhere; the term does not change when they do, and a saved component would keep its stale preview.
- **Validating per cell.** `Board.validate/3` is one contract, with board-level faults (overlaps, the sync group, drills) across cells; re-implementing it in the builder to save its cost on a one-cell edit would let the two drift. A one-cell edit changes the board, and the board is validated again.
- **Keeping per event kind** (an event that names the cells it touched). Every event would have to say so correctly forever; keys derived from the inputs cannot be wrong by omission.

**Consequences.**
- A board-mode pass on the examples' board costs about 73,000 reductions where it cost 2,120,000, on the cost test's board about 36,000 where it cost 405,000; a selection is a pass that draws nothing. An event that retitles one cell costs about 615,000 on the examples' board (2,120,000 before), most of it the board's validation, and draws one preview.
- A host that hands the builder a new catalog has its sample rows end at that instant; a preview's time axis no longer advances with every render, only with the data.
- The examples' board has eight cells; the saving grows with the cells, since an edit draws one.
## 192. D-192 — A group's presentation attributes are its children's state on the binary canvas of SVG elements

**Context.** #734, recorded in spec/09 §4.7 by #727. `Visualize.Backend.CanvasBinary.SVG`, the binary encoder for `Visualize.SVG.Element` trees, read presentation attributes on the drawing elements only, so a child that relied on its `:g` for its fill, stroke, width, opacity, dash, cap or join drew on the binary canvas without it — or, with no fill or stroke of its own, not at all — while SVG drew it with the group's. The IR encoder already passes a group's style to its children as canvas state inside `save` … `restore` and decides each element's draws by its effective style (D-37, D-111). #727's parity guard put each paint key on the drawing element only, so the gap passed it.

**Decision.**
- **What a group sets is its children's state** (spec/09 §4.7). A `:g` — and the root `:svg`, which SVG's children inherit from too — that sets `fill`, `fill-opacity`, `stroke`, `stroke-opacity`, `stroke-width`, `stroke-linecap`, `stroke-linejoin` or `stroke-dasharray` writes, inside `save` … `restore` and after its transform, a style record of the fill and the stroke it touches and its width, and a `paint_state` record of its cap, join and dash. The children draw in it, as the IR encoder's children draw in their group's style.
- **The encoder carries the inherited attributes down the tree**, the nearest group's value for each. A drawing element's fill and stroke draws follow its effective attributes — its own over the groups' — and each color a record writes is the effective color at the effective opacity, so a color and an opacity set on different elements combine as SVG combines them.
- **A group's opacity is its children's global alpha**, bit 3 of the group's style record, which the SVG encoder had never set; nested groups multiply, since each record writes the product of its own opacity and the ones above it.
- **A tree with no group that sets one is the stream it was**, byte for byte.

**Rejected.**
- **Merging the inherited attributes into every drawing element and writing nothing on the group.** It is correct for colors, but it repeats the group's style in every child's record, and it has no place for the group's opacity, which is not inherited in SVG and must not become the child's own.
- **Folding a group's opacity into each child's colors.** A child's own `opacity` is folded into its fill alone (§4.7); folding the group's the same way would leave the stroke opaque, and a nested group's opacity would have to be folded through every level of colors.
- **Compositing the group offscreen** to draw a group's opacity exactly where children overlap. The binary format has no record for it, and a new record would be a version 4 stream for every reader; the global alpha is exact where children do not overlap.

**Consequences.**
- Inherited group attributes draw the same on SVG and the binary canvas: `test/visualize/backend/canvas_binary_svg_group_test.exs` holds the records and a translucent group's two bands to resvg's pixels, and `test/visualize/backend/paint_opacity_test.exs`'s parity guard now puts every paint key on a group above a child that sets none, for every canvas encoder.
- A group whose overlapping children it makes translucent draws the overlap more opaque on the binary canvas than SVG does, the lower child showing through the upper.
- A drawing element's own `opacity` still reaches only its fill's alpha, as before; this decision does not change it.

## 193. D-193 — A drawing element's records are its own on the binary canvas of SVG elements, wherever it stands

**Context.** #744, reported by #743 (#742) from reading the encoder; work item #745. Reproduced at d6a60d1: two root-level `<line>`s, the first `stroke-width: 6`, the second stroked with none, encoded by `Visualize.Backend.CanvasBinary.SVG` as two style records, the second without the width flag; the binary hook sets `lineWidth` only when the flag is present, so the second line drew 6 wide where SVG draws it 1 wide. D-179 scoped a leaf's state on the IR encoders' streams (spec/09 §3.3.1, §4.4) and its guard, `canvas_scope_test.exs`, drew only those; the SVG-element encoder (§4.7) is a module of its own and was never under it. Its leaves were scoped piecemeal since: a leaf with a dash, a cap or a join (#693), and a leaf whose style changes a group's fill, stroke or width state (#742, under D-192's promise that a tree with no such group is the stream it was). A leaf that set only colors or a width at the root, under a group that set nothing, or under a group that set only a dash, kept no pair. Of the state a leaf sets, the width leaked to any later sibling that strokes; the colors reach a sibling only where it draws a paint it does not set, which outside a group's state is a `<line>` (its stroke is unconditional, spec/09 §4.7); the alpha never, since a leaf folds its opacities into its colors; the dash, the cap and the join were already scoped.

**Decision.**
- **A drawing element that writes a style record or a `paint_state` record writes all its records inside `save` … `restore`, wherever it stands** (spec/09 §4.7): D-179's rule, on this encoder too. #742's group-conditional wrap is the case of it under a group's state, and #693's the case of a paint state; the one rule replaces both.
- **A leaf that writes neither has no pair**, as on the IR encoders: a child that draws in its group's state alone costs nothing.
- **The guard is one list of encoders and one set of state keys.** The encoder list #727 introduced moves to `test/support/canvas_encoders.ex`, each encoder with the hook that replays it, and `canvas_scope_test.exs` runs, for every encoder and every state key, two siblings — the first setting the key, the second not — at the root, under a bare group and under a group with state, holding the second's draws on the stub to its draws alone. The stub records the width, cap, join and dash with each draw.

**Rejected.**
- **Writing every state field on every leaf's record** (the width at its default when a leaf sets none). It costs bytes on every leaf, and it would have to restate the group's state to keep inheritance, which is what `save` … `restore` keeps for free.
- **Keeping the byte-for-byte promise for trees with no group state.** The promise preserved the defect; a stream that draws differently from SVG is not worth keeping identical.
- **Making the hook reset the width when a style record lacks the flag.** The record's missing field means "unchanged" on every stream, the IR encoder's included, where a group's width is its children's.

**Consequences.**
- A styled SVG-element leaf costs two bytes more where it had no pair; nothing else in the stream changes. No golden changes: the goldens hold SVG.
- Every canvas encoder is held to the same sibling rule for every state key, so a new encoder or a new piece of leaf state cannot leak unnoticed.
- A `<line>` without a stroke still strokes in the context's current color on this encoder, where SVG draws nothing; after this decision that color is the groups' or the default black, never a sibling's.

**Amended 2026-10-19 by #756 (proposal #746): a leaf's own transform is one of its records.**
- **Context.** Reproduced at 761eb30: a root `<rect>` with `transform: "translate(50,50)"` encoded as `save, style, rects, fill, restore`, with no transform record, so it drew at the origin. The encoder read `transform` only on a `:g`, as the first SVG-element encoder did (February 2026), and spec/09 §4.7 was written from that code (#10). Its parser read a string only when it began with `translate(` or `scale(`, and only that first operation, so the `rotate(30)` that `Visualize.SVG.Element.from_ir/1` writes for a turned leaf, and a group's `translate(…) rotate(…)`, drew without the rotation. The guard this decision made held a sibling's draws to its draws alone; for the transform key the encoder dropped the transform in both runs, so the two agreed and the case passed while testing nothing.
- **Decision.** A drawing element's `transform` attribute is read, and its transform record is the first of its records inside its `save` … `restore`, as on the IR encoder (spec/09 §4.4); a leaf that sets only a transform is scoped too. The attribute is read as SVG reads it, on a group and a leaf alike: the whole list of the six SVG operations, into a `Visualize.IR.Transform`, written with the IR encoder's choice of record kind, so one encoding serves both encoders; a string in error writes no record, as SVG ignores it. A lone `scale(…)` on a group, which was kind `0x03` with no translation, is now `0xFF` with the same matrix: the same drawing.
- **The guard tests the transform itself.** The sibling guard's transform case also asserts, on every encoder, that the first leaf drew turned (the stub's recorded matrix), so an encoder that drops a transform fails it; a `:js_resvg` case holds a transformed leaf's position on the binary hook to resvg's raster of the SVG.
- **Rejected.** Extending the old prefix parser with `rotate(` alone: it would still drop every operation after the first, and keep a second, narrower reading of the one attribute the IR's `to_string/1` writes in full.

## 194. D-194 — A virtual source's function is checked Elixir, compiled once into a closure and called in a bounded process

**Context.** #643, the milestone "Virtual sources"; work item #748, the first of six. A virtual source is a source computed from other sources by a function, rows in and rows out; what #641 had called a role is one kind of its output. Its function is Elixir (#641 point 4; the project's rule that functions as data are Elixir, never a language of the library's own): a `def` in a host module, or source text stored in a design. Stored text is untrusted — a builder, a store or a shared host can put any text there — so it needs a sandbox. #643's prior-art review read Dune (an Elixir sandbox: an allowlist of `{module, function, arity}`, an atom pool, and a process per run with heap, reduction and time limits), agentronic and ryutronic (which evaluate stored code with `Code.eval_string` and keep no sandbox) and Ash Reactor (a one-shot saga, not a graph kept alive). Dune does not fit compiling once and calling many times: it interprets its rewritten AST on every call, and a function it returns runs outside its sandbox. Two decision notes on #643 (2026-10-15) fixed the allowlist: start small with control structures, math and comparison, data and `Enum`; then add `Map`, `Keyword`, `String`, `Integer`, `Float` and `:math`, random functions out, each module an explicit list held to its exports by a test. The shaped proposal (2026-10-16) settled an interpreted closure cached by source hash (question 8), a heap limit and a wall-clock kill with no reduction monitoring (question 9), and parsing after a design's schemas are decoded, with every field a schema's (question 10).

**Decision.**
- **The checker** (spec/08 §10.5.1) parses with `existing_atoms_only: true` under a maximum source length, requires one `fn` of the expected arity, and walks the AST before macros expand in three contexts — expression, pattern, guard — refusing whatever the allowlist does not name, with every fault `{line, reason}`. `row.field` with no parentheses and no arguments is a map read; a call on any receiver other than an allowed module is a dynamic call and refused. A map read names a field of the given schemas. `for … into:` and `Enum.into` take a literal empty target. Struct literals, aliases as values, atoms naming Elixir modules and `:__struct__` are refused, since a map naming a module under `:__struct__` dispatches a protocol to it. Undefined variables, unsafe guards, non-patterns and mixed `fn` arities are found by the checker so their line is named.
- **The allowlist** (spec/08 §10.5.2) is the decided one, as explicit `{function, arity}` lists per module, with the decided exclusions — `Enum.random/1`, `shuffle/1`, `take_random/2`; `String.to_atom/1`, `to_existing_atom/1` — and **every deprecated function excluded**, as the decision's table excluded `String`'s and `Integer`'s and `Float`'s `to_char_list` and the `strip`/`just` family "deprecated; their replacements are allowed". The 1.20.4 exports also deprecate `Enum.chunk/2,3,4`, `filter_map/3`, `partition/2` and `uniq/2`, `String.valid_character?/1`, `Float.to_string/2`, `Map.map/2` and `size/1`, and `Keyword.delete/3`, `map/2` and `size/1`; the table, read from the documentation, omitted them because the documentation hides them, and the rule its reason column states excludes them. `Enum.__in__/2`, the compiler's internal, is excluded as internal. The test holds each module's exports to its allowed list plus its excluded list, exactly.
- **Compile once** (spec/08 §10.5.3): after the check, every map read is rewritten to `:erlang.map_get/2` — in 1.20 `x.name` on an atom still calls `name/0` of the module the atom names — and the `fn` is evaluated once with `Code.eval_quoted/3`, under `require Integer`, into an ordinary closure, in the bounded runner so a compiler raise is a fault read from the exit reason. The closure is cached in a value keyed by the source's SHA-256, its arity and its fields; refusals are cached too.
- **The bounded runner** (spec/08 §10.5.4) calls every function — stored, and the host's own — in a process spawned with `:monitor` and `max_heap_size` (`kill: true`) set at the spawn, kills it when `timeout` passes, and reads the outcome from the result message or the `:DOWN` reason: `:memory` for the heap kill, `:timeout` for the timer's, `:raised` with the exception's module for any other exit. Nothing is rescued. The five fault kinds of a virtual source are `:restricted`, `:timeout`, `:memory`, `:raised` and `:nonconforming`.
- **Defaults:** a source of at most 10,000 bytes, a call of at most 1,000 ms and 10,000,000 words (80 MB).

**Rejected.**
- **Adopting Dune.** It interprets per call, returns functions that run outside it, has no structs and allows far more than calculation and mapping; its README disclaims strong guarantees. Its design — triples, failure kinds, a process per run, its attacks as tests — is borrowed.
- **A module per function** (`Code.compile_quoted` into a module named by the hash). Faster calls, but each function costs atoms and a module to purge, and the rates virtual sources run at (minutes, hours) do not need the speed.
- **An atom pool.** `existing_atoms_only` refuses unknown names at parse, and a design's schemas make its fields' and values' atoms before its functions are read, so no pool is needed. The cost: a variable name the node has never seen must be renamed.
- **Rescuing a raise into a fault.** The project rejects try/rescue; the runner's process boundary turns every raise into an exit reason read by monitoring, which is the BEAM's own way to contain a failure.
- **Reduction monitoring.** The wall-clock kill bounds a loop as surely, and a reduction budget would need a tracer or a polling process (#643, question 9).
- **Refusing costly calls** (`String.duplicate/2` with a huge count, a huge `**`). Cost is the developer's to weigh (#643's second decision); the runner is the backstop.
- **Allowing string interpolation.** It is a call of `Kernel.to_string/1`, which is not on the decided list; `<>` with `Integer.to_string/1` and `Float.to_string/1` builds a label. It is a candidate for the list, one addition at a time.

**Consequences.**
- A stored function is refused with every fault at its line before anything runs, which is what the builder shows as text is typed (#643's last work item).
- A function cannot make an atom, call outside the list, send, receive, spawn, read the clock or the disk, or name a module; a runaway ends as `:memory` or `:timeout` and the caller goes on.
- **The residue.** A heap limit is checked when the process collects, so one allocation larger than the limit is made before the kill: the host needs room for one such allocation, and a single allocation larger than the node's memory is not contained. A new Elixir release fails the exports test until its additions are reviewed.
- A function's raise is logged by the VM as any crashed process's is; the caller sees only the exception's module, never its message, which may quote the rows.

**Amended by #749 (the source value, item 2 of #643).**
- **A virtual source is a struct of data** (spec/08 §10.5.5): `id`, `from`, `schema`, `elixir` or `function`, `per`. It is checked alone by `new/1` and in context by `validate/2`, where its inputs' schemas and the host's functions are known; the fields a stored function may read are its inputs' and its own (#643, settled question 10).
- **An input is `{name, window}`** (spec/08 §10.5.6). A name is an atom (a virtual source, inside out, else a feed), a string (a feed, never split at its dots) or a list of atoms (a full path). A window is `nil`, `%{last: n}` — each identity's newest `n` rows when the input declares identity, which is what "the history of each entity" means, else the newest `n` — or a D-168 box. `{:window, opts}`, the proposal's spelling, reads as the map. The function gets what is there (settled question 3) and aligns inputs itself (settled question 13).
- **Declared values are a schema fact**: `values`, the fourteenth description key (spec/08 §10.1), on a `:category` field, a closed set; `order` and `status` name only values it lists. It does not change the version of a schema that does not use it.
- **Per identity** (settled question 5): one identity input, named when there are several; the output declares exactly its identity fields; each call gets the entity's rows (the rows alone with one input, else the inputs' map), answers a row or rows, and is given the entity's identity; the calls for every entity are one bounded batch. **Conformance** names the row's index and the field, never a value.
- **`defsource`** (spec/08 §10.5.8) mirrors `defgenerator` (D-177): a `def` of one argument whose source is captured with `Macro.to_string/1` when the module compiles, registered by module under `config :visualize, virtual_sources:` and an option, trusted but bounded.
- **Paths are lists of atoms, shown dotted, resolved by one function from the inside out** (spec/08 §10.5.9, settled questions 1 and 2). On a board the containers are the board and its cells: a board's `virtual_sources` key holds sources at `[id]`, a cell's at `[cell, id]` (spec/15 §1.2, §1.3). The key is `virtual_sources`, not `sources`, since `[:cells, cell, :sources, slot]` is already a slot's path in a binding's faults.
- **No new JSON tag**: inputs, schemas and boxes are written as pair lists of terms, so a name's spelling survives (spec/08 §10.5.10).
- **Rejected:** `values` as a reading of `order` (an order says how states sort, not that they are all there are); a dotted string as a path (a feed's name is often dotted); a `Visualize.Stats` module on the allowlist (the proposal's example named one, but the decided allowlist does not, and the shaped terms hold to it).

**Amended by #750 (the engine, item 3 of #643).**
- **The engine is a value the board process holds** (spec/15 §14), beside its feed monitor and for the same reasons (D-140): one owner of the window, of "now" and of the rows, and no process boundary that would copy every delivery once more.
- **Defined in three steps when a board starts** (spec/15 §14.1): references resolve from the source's own scope (inside out, then a feed); cycles are named with `:digraph_utils.cyclic_strong_components/1`, at the first member, every member listed; ranks are one more than the greatest input's. A board whose sources fail is not started, `{:error, {:virtual_sources, errors}}`, at the sources' paths in the board.
- **A pulse** (spec/15 §14.2, settled questions 4 and 11): a feed whose delivery is not the one the engine last saw marks its readers; the first mark makes the next pulse due `debounce:` later — the board's option, which also debounces a brush, so one knob says how often a board may recompute. A pulse runs the marked sources in rank order, each at most once; an answer that differs marks its readers, an unchanged one does not. A source runs only when every input has a delivery.
- **Validation in context is lazy.** A board does not know its feeds' schemas when it starts — the monitor describes them at its first tick — so a source is validated given its inputs' schemas before it first runs and again when an input's schema version changes; a failure is the fault `:restricted`, its detail the faults. That widens `:restricted` from "the checker refused the function" to "the source is refused where it runs".
- **A fault keeps the last good output and stops the pulse there** (spec/15 §14.3). Its state is `{:down, {:virtual_source, id, kind}}`; its detail is in `info/1`.
- **A binding names a virtual source as it names a feed**, resolved from the cell's scope first (spec/15 §14.5); the cell is handed the source's output as a delivery with its schema and its state, so every consumer of a source reads it unchanged.
- **Deterministic to test**: the engine takes the owner's monotonic now; a test sets `debounce: 0` and turns the board with `tick/1`, so no test waits on a clock (spec/12 §6).
- **Rejected:** a process per source (a second owner of rows the board owns, and a message per input per update); propagating a failed source's previous output as if new (its readers would recompute from data that did not change); running a source with an input still loading (it would answer for half the world).

**Amended by #751 (the consumers, item 4 of #643).**
- **A joined field is named `:"<id>.<field>"`** (spec/15 §14.7, spec/14 §3.6): the proposal's `health.state`, an atom because a design's field bindings are atoms; it is made when the board starts from the source's id and its output's fields, a bounded set the host defined. The board writes the joined fields on each row of the identity input from the source's last good output and describes them in the slot's schema, so every consumer — a style by field, a `:choose`, a legend, a tooltip — reads a joined field as any column.
- **`:choose` is a mark type** (spec/14 §5.2): `on`, `cases` (a map from a value's name to a `:choice` node — a `:circle`, `:symbol`, `:text` or `:rect` with its own channels, options, style and label) and `else`; each case's rows are drawn by its child over the choose mark's channels. A value names a case as it names a style (an atom, or a string matched by spelling, never made an atom); a value no case names draws `else`, or nothing.
- **A legend of declared values** (spec/14 §4.5): `values` — usually `fact(slot, field, :values)` — makes the legend list the values, each swatched by the style it names, and frees it of `scale`.
- **Coverage** (spec/14 §7.4): at application, a style entry `{:field, f}` and a `:choose` over a field with declared `values` must cover every value (a named style; a case or an `else`), else `{:uncovered, f, missing}`. It runs once the frames are realized, where the theme's palette is known.
- **Rejected:** string field names in a design's bindings (every binding site, validator and codec reads a field as an atom; one atom per joined field is the smaller change); a legend that infers its values from a style site (a legend explains one thing, and the fact says which); coverage at render (a missing style would then draw silently as the mark's own look, which is the fault the check exists to name).

**Amended by #752 (the examples, item 5 of #643).** The examples' `/board` holds a *Hosts* cell over a synthetic hosts feed with declared identity and two per-identity virtual sources, one of each kind: `health`, a stored function in the board value, and `trend`, a `defsource` in the host's module registered with the board's `virtual_sources:` (spec/15 §10). Its states are named by the palette's role styles (`:good`, `:warning`, `:bad`), so the style by field, the `:choose` icons and the legend need no styles of the design's own and the coverage check passes on the theme alone.

**Amended by #753 (the builder, item 6 of #643).** The board mode runs a board's virtual sources over the preview's sample rows with the same engine and one immediate pulse, no process (spec/14 §22.9): cells preview over the answers and the joined fields, and the binding check reads each feed's schema with its joined fields, which closes the gap #752 left (a fact of a joined field was a builder fault). A function is edited as text: a text that holds is written and run at once; one the checker refuses is kept as a draft beside its faults at their lines, the board keeping the last function that held. A host's `defsource` is shown as its captured source, read-only — the host's code is edited where it lives.

**Amended 2026-10-19 by #769 (work item #770): interpolation and `Visualize.Data` join the list.** The user decided on 2026-10-19 to add both gaps #643 shipped with, one entry at a time as the decision notes asked.
- **`Kernel.to_string/1` is allowed, and with it string interpolation** (spec/08 §10.5.2). `"#{x}"` parses as a binary whose segment is `Kernel.to_string(x)::binary`; the receiver is the atom `Kernel`, already a module the checker knows, and a `binary` segment was already allowed, so the one list entry is the whole change: no special case for the interpolation form, and `to_string(x)` written out is the same call. `String.Chars` on what a function can reach is pure. Atom interpolation (`:"a#{x}"`) is a call of `:erlang.binary_to_existing_atom/2` (as the checker parses it) and stays refused, so a function still makes no atom and probes none. The "Rejected: allowing string interpolation" entry above is withdrawn.
- **`Visualize.Data` is the eighth module** (spec/08 §5, §10.5.2): every export allowed as an explicit `{function, arity}` list with an empty excluded list, held to the module's exports by the same test, so a function the library adds to `Visualize.Data` fails until it is reviewed and listed. Its accessors and reducers are `fn`s the stored function made. It stands in for the `Visualize.Stats` #643's example named and #749 rejected: the library keeps one home for statistics.
- **`slope/1,2` and `delta/1,2` join `Visualize.Data`** (spec/08 §5.1), the two statistics #643 asked for that it lacked (`count` and `max_by` are `Enum`'s). `slope` is the least-squares slope, mean-centered, of `{x, y}` points, a number read as `{index, value}`; `nil` for fewer than two points or no `x` variance, as `variance` is `nil` for one value. `delta` is last minus first.
- **Rejected:** a `Visualize.Stats` module (a second home for what `Visualize.Data` holds); allowing only the interpolation form and not `to_string/1` itself (the same call twice spelled, refused once).

**Amended 2026-10-20 by D-195 (#780): a stored function's names are read without an atom made.** The checker parses with a static atoms encoder instead of `existing_atoms_only` alone (spec/08 §10.5.1): a call's name is the checker's own atom or a sentinel it refuses by the name as written; a variable is one of a fixed pool of the library's atoms (256, and 256 for `_`-prefixed names), its written name kept for faults; an atom literal, a map key and a keyword key is a word an allowed function takes, else its string; a map read keeps its field as written and reads the row by spelling at run time (`Sandbox.Runtime.get/2`, a guard `:erlang.map_get/2` of the field as the inputs spell it). The engine hands a stored function its inputs, its rows' fields and their category values by the same rule, so a row's `:ok` is the function's `:ok` and a field named `day` is the key `%{day: d}` matches; a host's function gets its inputs keyed by their strings and its rows as the feed gave them; every source's output rows are keyed by their fields' strings. The joined field of #751 is the string `"<id>.<field>"`. A virtual source's id, its inputs, its paths and its fields are strings (spec/08 §10.5.5–§10.5.10); a feed-only input is `{:feed, name}`, and the JSON form is unchanged. **Reverses** this decision's rejection of an atom pool: with names strings, nothing makes a variable's atom before the function is read, and a pool is the bounded set that lets any variable name compile (class (d) of D-195).

## 195. D-195 — No atom is made from a string a document or a client controls: a closed vocabulary is looked up, a key is decoded against the schema, and a name a document declares is a string

**Context.** #778, the milestone "No atoms from untrusted input"; work item #779, the first of five. Atoms are never collected and the table is capped (1,048,576 by default); exhausting it stops the whole node, not a process. The deep dive on #778 (2026-10-19, at 2397295) measured the leak: a design with seven names made seven atoms on every read; a design with 1,000 unknown top-level keys made 1,000 atoms **although it was refused**, because the codec turned every key into an atom before the validator ran; one 5.4 MB design holding three maps of 99,999 keys under an unknown key made 299,997 atoms, and eleven such maps (about 19 MB) pass every limit of D-137 and stop the node by themselves. `items` bounds one map, not a document, and nothing bounds atoms across documents. The builder made an atom per event from params any client of the LiveView sends (`remove_source` called `String.to_atom` before any lookup), and its literal reader parsed typed text without `existing_atoms_only`. About 60 sites were classified: (a) a closed vocabulary the library defines (schema keys, enums, slots, anchors, units, kinds) — (a-host) when host code registers it (generator names, `defsource` functions, built-in themes); (b) a name the document declares (fields, vars, frames, styles, sources, cells, slots, Use keys); (c) derived from document names (`:"#{key}_#{name}"`, `:"#{id}.#{field}"`); (d) bounded by the library.

**Root cause: why D-172's "designs are untrusted" (#660) did not cover atoms.**
- D-57 made atom creation an explicit, accepted behavior on the premise that "a design is authored, not data", and handed the bound to "a consumer that loads designs from an untrusted source bounds them itself" (spec/14 §11.1). No API let a consumer do that. D-86 repeated the premise for the builder, whose events come from any client of the LiveView, not only from the author.
- D-137 (#541) named the trust boundary — documents travel between hosts — and fixed the order (version and limits before decoding), but its `items` limit bounds one map, not a document or the node, and it did not revisit D-57's premise.
- #660 (D-172, amended) declared strings from a design untrusted, but scoped the threat to what a string does when rendered (SVG, CSS and JS injection, URLs), not to what decoding it costs the node. Nothing tied the new premise back to D-57, so this file held two contradictory premises.
- The rule then lived only as local habit: "never made into an atom" was added tag by tag (#554 `$scale`, #645 `$generate`, #664 `$color`, #651 `$unit`, `$id` kinds), while the general paths kept D-57's behavior, with no mechanical gate and no behavioral test to catch them. D-194's sandbox (#748, question 10) then built on the leak: its `existing_atoms_only` parse accepts `row.cpu` because the codec had made `:cpu`.
- **Lesson:** a trust premise is a decision. When a later decision contradicts one, the earlier one is amended in the same change, and the rule gets a gate, not a habit.

**Decision** (the user, 2026-10-19: Option A of the deep dive, with category values matched by spelling).
- **A closed vocabulary is looked up, never created.** Each library vocabulary a document's string can name — the schema's keys, an enumeration's members, the theme's fixed slots, a text anchor, a frame point, a fact's attribute, a unit, a ramp, an id's kind, a color transform, a builder form's side — is a table from string to atom, built from the vocabulary's own list (at compile time where the list is known then; `Visualize.Vocabulary`). A string the table lacks stays a string, and the reader or the validator refuses it at its path. No reader calls `String.to_existing_atom/1` on a string a document holds: that would let a document probe which atoms the node has, and would make a read depend on what modules happen to be loaded. A series slot `series_k` is read only for `1 ≤ k ≤` the default `items` limit, the same bounded set of atoms the theme's slots are (class (d)).
- **A key is decoded against the schema before anything is made of it** (spec/14 §9.4). A node key the schema knows — the keys of every node kind, and the keys only an earlier version had, which the migration reads — is its atom; any other key **stays a string, is `:unknown_key` at its own spelling, and its value is kept as parsed, undecoded**, so a refused document makes no atom from its keys or from anything under them. The board's, snapshot's and virtual source's readers did this already for their own keys (D-152, #542) and read them through the same compile-time tables now.
- **The builder's literal reader** (`Visualize.Chart.Builder.Editor`) parses typed text without making an atom: with `existing_atoms_only: true` in #779, and with a static atoms encoder since #780, so a literal is a word of the library or its string.
- **A name a document declares is a string in the model** — fields, vars, frames, styles, sources, cells, slots, Use keys and identities (D-167). Elixir input with atom names is still accepted and normalized to strings by `Visualize.Chart.Migration.canonical/1` on every read, so `{:field, :cpu}` reads as `{:field, "cpu"}`. Derived names are strings (or tuples where the consumer allows). JSON on disk does not change — its names are already strings — so stored documents need no migration. **Category values** (`$atom`, a fact's `values:`, a `:choose` mark's cases, style-by-field values) are strings matched by spelling, so `:ok` and `"ok"` are one value. The sandbox (D-194) parses with a static atoms encoder: a stored function's `row.field` reads a string-keyed row by spelling, and the checker still refuses `:__struct__` and module atoms. **Built by #780** for the design core, boards, snapshots, bundles, virtual sources and the sandbox, the builder (an event's string maps onto an existing name or stays a string) and the atom-count guard (the amendments below).
- **A gate holds the rule** (spec/12 §3). `mix vis.atoms` fails on `String.to_atom`, `:erlang.binary_to_atom`, `List.to_atom`, `list_to_atom` or an interpolated atom (`:"…#{…}…"`) anywhere in `lib/` outside `audit/atoms_allowlist.exs`, which gives each allowed site its file, the text it matches, its class and its reason; an entry that no longer matches fails too, so the list cannot go stale. A remaining site is class (a), (a-host) or (d) with its bound stated; an entry of class (b) or (c) fails the task (#780).
- **Amends** D-57 ("every name is an atom" holds for the library's vocabularies, not for a document's names), D-86 (a builder exposed to a client bounds what the client can make), D-137 (the codec decodes no key it does not know) and D-194 (question 10's parse after decoding no longer relies on the codec's atoms).

**Rejected.**
- **A per-document vocabulary with a cap** (Option B): bounds the atoms of one document, not of the node; a node-wide budget turns the crash into refusing legitimate names until a restart.
- **Existing atoms only** (Option C): a design the builder saved with its own default names (`source_1`, `frame_2`, `cell_1`) would fail to reopen after a restart, a bundle from another host would fail, and whether a read succeeds would depend on module load order. Kept only for (a-host) names, which host code made.
- **An atom when one exists, else a string** (Option D): a name's type would depend on the node's history, and a stored `"main"` and a host's `:main` would be two frames.
- **`String.to_existing_atom/1` as the lookup for closed vocabularies**: it lets a document probe the atom table and raises on a miss.

**Consequences.**
- After #779: a document with N unknown keys, refused, makes no atom for any N; the 5.4 MB design makes none.
