After #780: reading, applying and drawing N documents with fresh names — designs, fragments, boards, snapshots, virtual sources and their stored functions — and N builder events naming fresh names make no atom (`test/visualize/chart/no_atoms_test.exs`).
- An unknown key read from JSON is reported at its string spelling (`frames.main.wdth: is not a key of this node` with `"wdth"` in the path), as a board's, snapshot's and virtual source's already were.
- An unknown enumeration member, slot, anchor or frame point read from JSON is refused by type at its path, as before, but holds the string it was rather than an atom made of it.
- 0.7.0 is held for the whole of this decision: the map form's names become strings, a breaking change the CHANGELOG lists with what a host changes.

**Amended 2026-10-19 by #780 (work item 2 of #778): the design core holds names as strings.**
- **Names** are strings in the map form: a source, field, variable, frame, scale, style, paint and Use key, and every reference to one (`{:field, "f"}`, `%Var{name: "v"}`, `%Fact{source: "s", field: "f", key: :unit}`, `{:frame, "f", "s"}`, `{:axis, "s"}`, `{:paint, "p"}`, `{:keys, [...]}`, a mark's `data`, `frame` and `style`, a transform's fields and `as`, a source's `fields`, `types` and `requires` keys and `default`).
- **`Migration.canonical/1` and `Migration.parts/2` normalize** an atom in a name position to its string (`Atom.to_string/1`, which makes no atom), so Elixir input written with atoms reads as the same design. `Visualize.Chart.var/1` and `fact/3` take either spelling and hold the string.
- **Library names in the design's namespaces meet it as strings**: the built-in styles (`"axis"`, `"grid"`, `"label"`, `"title"`), the theme palette's names (`"style_1"`, `"info"`, …), the default frame `"main"` and a channel's default scale name (`"x"`, `"y"`, `"color"`, …).
- **Category values are matched by spelling.** `{"$atom": s}` reads as the library's own atom of that spelling when `s` names one of its vocabularies (an enumeration's member, a mark type, a slot, a unit) and otherwise as the string `s`; a `:choose` mark's cases, a color map's categories, an extent's categories and a fact's `values` match a row's value whether either spells it as an atom or a string.
- **A field reaches its column by spelling** (`Visualize.Data.Table.get/2`, D-52): a string name on an atom-keyed row finds the key with that spelling without making an atom, and the key it found is remembered for the calling process, so a frame reads every row at the cost it had.
- **Rejected:** converting a host's rows to string keys at bind (a copy of every row per tick, against the frame budget); `String.to_existing_atom/1` on a field name (probes the atom table, and raises on a miss).

**Amended 2026-10-20 by #780 (work items 3–5 of #778, built with item 2): boards, virtual sources, the sandbox and the builder hold names as strings, and nothing allowed makes a document's name an atom.**
- **One representation, one work item.** The deep dive planned the design core, the boards and the builder as three work items. They read one another's names — a board's cells hold designs, a board's binding names a design's slots, the builder edits all of them — so no one of them is green without the others, and they were built and tested as one change under #780.
- **Boards** (spec/15 §1): a cell's, a variable's, a link's and a virtual source's names are strings, and `Visualize.Board.from_map/2`, the board's process and every function that takes a name read a host's atom as its string (`Visualize.Board.named/1` for a struct built by hand). A binding's key is `{cell, slot}` of strings; the **feed stays the host's term**, never rewritten, since the host's own maps are keyed by it. A view's and an event's `cell`, a selection's and an identity's fields, `realize/4`'s, `components/3`'s and `required_sources/3`'s keys and a snapshot's cells and slots are strings.
- **Virtual sources** (spec/08 §10.5): the id, the inputs, the paths and the fields are strings. A plain string that named only a feed would read the same as a short id in the map form, so a feed-only input is `{:feed, name}`; the JSON form keeps its spellings — a short id `{"$atom": …}`, a feed's name a plain string, a field `{"$atom": …}` — so a stored board reads unchanged. A stored function reads names as its literals do (D-194, amended), a host's function gets its inputs keyed by their strings, and every output row is keyed by its fields' strings.
- **Themes** (spec/08 §7): `Visualize.Theme.new/1` and `merge/2` name a host's theme by its string and a built-in by its atom, and key a color the theme declares by its string, the library's own colors staying atoms, so a host's theme and its document are one value. A message names a host's theme as `theme "house"`.
- **The builder** (spec/14 §18): every name an event carries is kept as the string the client sent — a source, a column, a scale, a style, a variable, a frame, a cell; a name the builder makes is a string (`source_<n>`, `frame_<n>`, `cell_<n>`, `<key>_<n>`); a seeded or opened layer's names are read as strings when it enters the stack, so an edit merges into them; a host's pool and bindings are read by spelling. The literal reader parses with a static atoms encoder (§9.4), and a word typed in a vocabulary position — a theme, a scheme, a projection — is the library's atom of that spelling.
- **The library's words** (`Visualize.Chart.Words`) grow by the vocabularies a term may spell: the palette's names and its style series `style_k` (bounded as `series_k` is), a term's tuple tags, the color transforms and a data schema's types, polarities and metric kinds.
- **Ids.** A gradient written in place is held under `{:inline, name}` beside the declared ones, which are strings too; the id prefix hashes an inline paint by its own name as before, so a chart of inline paints keeps its ids, and a chart with a declared paint draws under a new prefix.
- **The gate** refuses an allowlist entry of class (b) or (c); the allowlist holds (a), (a-host) and (d) only, and the sandbox's variable pool is a class (d) entry of 512 atoms made when the library compiles.
- **Rejected:** keeping the builder's names atoms while documents' are strings (two spellings of one name inside one stack, which the cascade would hold as two keys); rewriting a host's feeds and pool keys to strings (a binding would no longer name the host's feed); keeping a plain string as feed-only in the map form (the map form of a short id and a feed's name would be the same string, so a round trip could not keep the difference).

## 196. D-196 — A field binding in a style is read where a datum is: per row at a mark's, a choice's and a mark label's site, refused as a value at a frame label's, an axis's and a legend's

**Context.** #766, found by the minesweeper demo (#758, #762). `label: %{style: %{fill: {:field, :v}}}` on a mark validated and applied, then `Chart.render` raised `Protocol.UndefinedError` in the SVG renderer. A field binding is a legal style value (spec/14 §3.2, D-171), and the schema's type for every style site is the one style stack (`Schema.style_stack/0`), so the validator admits it at all six sites. A probe of each site, with the binding as an inline node's value and as a stack entry, found three behaviors: a mark's `style` and a `:choose` case's read both per row; a mark label's read the stack entry but not the inline value, which raised; a frame `:label`'s, an axis's and a legend's raised on the inline value, and laid nothing for the entry, as spec/14 §3.6 says an entry does where there is no datum.

**Root cause.** `Visualize.Chart.Style.resolve/3` keeps a `{:field, f}` value for `bind/4`, and only the mark's own element path calls `bind/4`, with the mark style's bindings. The label's style was resolved by `Mark.Label.style/2` and never bound, so the tuple reached the renderer. At a datum-less site nothing binds a value either. The tests missed both because no label-style case carried a bound value, and no test walked the style sites: each site was tested where it was written, with the values its author thought of. The randomized design generator (`test/support/chart_gen.ex`) did produce frame labels naming a bound style, but the property tests only validate and round-trip, they do not render.

**Decision.**
- **A mark label binds per row.** `Mark.Label.style/2` resolves the label's node and binds it against the datum the label belongs to, with the frame's `color` scale, exactly as the mark's elements bind theirs. Fixing the label path, rather than refusing the binding there, keeps one rule for a style value wherever a row is in hand.
- **A site with no datum refuses a bound value.** A frame `:label`, an axis and a legend draw from no row, so a `{:field, f}` value has nothing to read. The validator reports `{:invalid_for, kind}` at the path that holds it: in an inline node of the stack, or in a design style the stack names, directly or through `extends` or a list. This is the frame label's text rule (spec/14 §6.2) applied to its style. The theme's palette is not walked: a palette entry is a node of the theme's, and no built-in theme binds a field.
- **An entry and a generator argument keep their rule.** A stack's `{:field, f}` entry names a style per row, and with no row it lays nothing (§3.6, #644); a generator's field argument reads `nil` (§3.13, D-177). Each has a meaning at a datum-less site, so a stack written once can serve a mark and an axis alike. A bound value has none: reading it as `nil` would draw the key as `:none`, an invisible axis or title.
- **The schema lists its style sites.** `Visualize.Chart.Schema.style_sites/0` is every `{kind, key}` whose type is the style stack, derived from the tables. The recurrence guard walks it: each site with a datum must render a bound style per row on SVG, the JSON canvas and the binary canvas; each without must refuse a bound value and draw an entry as nothing, on all three. A seventh site fails the guard until it is classified.

**Consequences.**
- A design whose axis, legend or frame label named a style that binds a value, which raised at render, is now refused when it is read. The design generator now keeps the `:axis` style it writes free of bindings and names only an unbound style from a frame label.
- **Rejected:** binding a datum-less site against no row, which renders but hides the author's mistake as an invisible key; and refusing the stack entry there as well, which would contradict §3.6 and break stacks shared between marks and furniture.

## 197. D-197 — A click on a canvas resolves through a hit index sent beside the stream, not by replaying the stream with `isPointInPath`

**Context.** #764, found by the minesweeper demo (#758, #762). `Visualize.Components.Chart` delivered a click as a `Visualize.Event` (D-170) by reading the key — frame, mark position, row ordinal — from the SVG element the click landed on (spec/10 §12.3). A canvas draws no element per datum, so a clickable chart (the minesweeper grid, a status grid, a drill-down) worked on SVG only, and the component drew SVG only. The evaluation of #764 leaned toward a per-frame index of the drawn items, and asked the work item to measure it against replaying the stream with `isPointInPath`.

**The two candidates.**
- **A hit index.** The server writes, beside the stream, every keyed element of a mark with an event as its shape in the chart's units — a box, a disc, or a path with its matrix — under its key. The hook hit-tests that list on a click, topmost first.
- **Replay.** The stream gains a record that marks where each keyed element begins, and the hook keeps the stream and, on a click, replays it into a path per element, asking the context `isPointInPath` of each.

**Measured** (2026-10-20, at the work item's branch; examples' charts at their page sizes; server times the median of 51 to 201 runs on this host; click latency in node 22 over 2,000 random points, the replay's path test emulated geometrically because node has no canvas, so its figure is a **lower bound** of a browser's, which also builds a `Path2D` per element):

| Chart | Keyed items | Index bytes | Index build per frame | Generate per frame (for scale) | Replay markers, JSON stream | Replay markers, binary stream | Click, index | Click, replay |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Minesweeper grid, 368 × 368 | 81 rects | 1,839 | 0.51 ms | 3.9 ms | +2,906 on 48,834 (+6.0%) | +324 on 19,258 (+1.7%) | 5.9 µs (parse 92 µs once per patch) | ≥ 135 µs (parse 995 µs, kept) |
| Interaction tooltip chart, 600 × 360 | 72 circles | 1,908 | 0.33 ms | 2.5 ms | +2,582 on 33,931 (+7.6%) | +288 on 6,194 (+4.6%) | 5.5 µs (parse 72 µs) | ≥ 210 µs (parse 760 µs) |
| Motion demo bars, 600 × 360 | 8 rects | 342 | 0.06 ms | 0.7 ms | +280 on 14,712 (+1.9%) | +32 on 5,796 (+0.6%) | 2.1 µs (parse 8 µs) | ≥ 71 µs (parse 291 µs) |

**Decision: the hit index.**
- **It is cheaper where it matters.** A click is a scan of the keyed items, 20 to 40 times faster than even the emulated replay, and its parse is paid once per patch rather than kept as the whole parsed stream. Its build is 10 to 15% of generating the tree the frame is drawn from. Its bytes are more than the binary stream's markers would be, but they are only sent with a drawing that has events, and they are within the JSON stream's own growth.
- **It changes no stream.** Replay would need a new record in the binary format (spec/09 §4), its encoder, both decoders and the JSON command list, and the hook would have to keep and replay a stream it today draws and drops. The index is a data attribute beside an unchanged stream, the same shape for both canvas formats.
- **It is the SVG's key by construction.** The index is built from the generated tree the SVG and the canvases are drawn from, out of the very attributes the SVG keys its elements by (`data-tooltip-event`, `data-frame`, `data-mark`, `data-row`). A click at one point pushes the same payload on every backend, and `Event.resolve/3` is unchanged.
- **Precision where it is cheap.** Rectangles and discs are tested exactly, grown by half their stroke as SVG's hit area is. A path is tested by its box first and then, where the browser has `Path2D`, by `isPointInPath` or `isPointInStroke` under its own matrix: replay's precision, for the one item under the pointer rather than for every item.
- **It serves the keyboard too.** The index is a list of every keyed item with a position, which is what a grid's focus order needs (#765).

**Consequences.**
- `Visualize.Hooks.Tooltip.items/1` writes the index; `VisualizeItems` reads it; `TooltipHook` resolves a click with no datum element through it (spec/10 §12.5).
- `Visualize.Components.Chart` draws on a canvas with `backend: :canvas` and `canvas_format: :binary | :json`, and on the binary stream lays the chart's text over the canvas as an `aria-hidden` SVG, since that stream has no text record by design (spec/10 §19.3).
- A non-event element drawn over a keyed one stops an SVG click (it is the target) but not a canvas click, which sees only keyed items. A page that wants the SVG's behavior gives such marks `pointer-events: none`, as the minesweeper page already does.
- **Rejected:** `isPointInPath` replay, for the reasons above; and an index of boxes only, which would make a line's whole extent clickable.

## 198. D-198 — The keyboard reaches every mark with an identity, a band grid in two dimensions; Enter on a keyed point is its click, and a click on a keyed item is one event

**Context.** #765, found by the minesweeper demo (#758, #762). `KeyboardHook` (D-142) built its focus list from the summary's points, which are the rows of marks that **draw a number** (spec/14 §21.5). A band × band grid — minesweeper's cells, a dashboard's status grid — draws none, so it had zero points: no arrows, no Enter, and a chart that was "accessible by construction" could not be played or read from the keyboard. Separately, a board's cell wraps its chart in `KeyboardHook` with a pick event and in `TooltipHook`, and a click went to both: the pick of the nearest point and the mark's declared event, two `Visualize.Event`s for one click.

**Decision.**
- **Identity is enough to be reached.** A data mark whose rows yield no number contributes its rows that carry an identity (declared, D-167, else inferred as a pick infers it), each at the center of what it draws and read as its identity and its tooltip's text. Identity is the right criterion because it is what an event, a selection and a transition already key a row by; a row with none could not be acted on anyway. A mark that draws numbers keeps its readout by value, so no existing chart's points change.
- **A band grid is two-dimensional.** A mark with both axes on band scales is a grid: each point carries its cell, and the arrows move by row and by column, skipping empty cells and stopping at the edges. Along one series, as before, the arrows would walk a 9 × 9 board cell by cell in reading order, and Up and Down, which move between series, would leave it.
- **Enter on a keyed point is a click.** A point of a mark with a declared event carries the key a click on it sends (frame, mark position, row ordinal, D-170). Enter pushes `visualize_event` with that key, the point's position as `at` and the drawing's token from the tooltip's element, through the same `VisualizeItems.send` the tooltip uses, so the host gets the same `Visualize.Event` and writes one clause for both.
- **One click, one event.** `KeyboardHook` asks `VisualizeItems.key` — the tooltip's own resolution, an SVG element or a canvas hit (D-197) — before it picks. A click on a keyed item is the tooltip's to send, and the keyboard only moves its reached point there; a click elsewhere still picks the nearest point, which is how a board selects by pointer where no mark declares an event.
- **The component is reached by keyboard.** `Visualize.Components.Chart` wraps its drawing in `KeyboardHook` on every backend, so a clickable chart is one tab stop whose items are reached and clicked from the keyboard.

**Consequences.**
- `Summary.points/1` gains identity series, grid cells and keys; `Keyboard.attrs/1` writes points as `[x, y, readout, key, cell]` when they have either, and `"grid": true` on a grid's series. A chart whose marks all draw numbers writes what it wrote.
- On a board, a click on a status cell whose mark declares an event no longer also selects by pick; a board whose cells declare no event picks as before.
- A grid's series is not sampled: a grid is reached cell by cell, and a grid of more than 1,000 cells is reached whole.
- **Rejected:** a separate focus list sent beside the hit index (two lists of one set of items, with their own order); making Enter push the pick event and letting the board translate it (a page's chart would still get nothing); and dropping the pick on every click when the chart has any event (a board would lose its pointer selection over marks without one).

## 199. D-199 — A secondary action is a second name a mark declares, `context_event`, raised by right-click, long-press and the Menu key, and the browser's menu yields only to it

**Context.** #767, found by the minesweeper demo (#758, #762). Only the builder's hook listened for `contextmenu`, so a chart host could not offer a secondary action on an item: right-click to flag a cell, a context menu on a dashboard series. The proposal left open whether the action is a new event name (`"item_context"`) or a `button:` field on the click event, and proposed a chart-wide opt-in. The evaluation decided it: **the opt-in is a declared name on the mark beside its `event`**, no new `Visualize.Event` field, and the browser's menu suppressed only on items whose mark declares one.

**Decision.**
- **A name, beside `event`.** `tooltip.context_event` is a name like `event`, declared on the mark and delivered as the event's string. The host receives an ordinary `Visualize.Event` with that name and the item, row, path, `at` and modifiers a click on the item gives, and writes one more `handle_info` clause; nothing in the struct changes, and nothing distinguishes the gesture beyond its name. A `button:` field would have made every host match on two fields, and a runtime atom (`:item_context`) would have been one name for every mark's secondary action, which a host with two clickable marks could not tell apart without the item.
- **Per mark, so the menu yields only where it is meant to.** The browser's context menu is the page's own and a reader expects it. A chart-wide flag would suppress it over every pixel of the chart; the per-mark name suppresses it only over an item whose mark declares one — a minesweeper cell, not the page's background or an axis.
- **The two names differ.** A mark may declare either or both, and each keys its elements. Declaring one name for both would give the host one event for two gestures, so the validator refuses it (`{:same_name, :event}`).
- **Three gestures, one event.** A right-click (`contextmenu`), a long-press on touch (500 ms, 10-pixel slop, the click and menu a browser fires after it swallowed for 800 ms), and the Menu key or Shift+F10 on the item focused from the keyboard (D-198) each push `visualize_event` with the context name through `VisualizeItems.send`, so they resolve on the server through the one `Event.resolve/3` path (D-170) and on every backend (D-197). The resolver accepts a click named by either of the mark's names.

**Consequences.**
- The hit index's entries and the keyboard's keys carry `context`; the SVG's mark group carries `data-tooltip-context-event`.
- `Visualize.Event.declared/1` lists context names too, so a host that checks a chart's names sees them.
- A long-press that would have opened a touch browser's own menu over such an item opens nothing now: the item's secondary action is the host's.
- **Rejected:** a `button:` field on the click event, a single runtime `:item_context` event, and a chart-wide opt-in, for the reasons above.

## 200. D-200 — A mark's events need no hover text: `tooltip.show: false` keys the elements and shows nothing under the pointer

**Context.** #803, found on the Minesweeper page (user, 2026-10-20). A mark declares the events a click and a secondary gesture deliver only through its `tooltip` node (spec/14 §5.7, D-170, D-199), and `TooltipHook` shows hover text for every element that node writes: the template, else `field: value` per line, and the palette's `:hover` outline around it. A design that wanted clicks therefore also had a label following the pointer, and Minesweeper showed "hidden" and "revealed 1" over every cell. The proposal left open the key's name (`show: false` or `hover: false`), whether the outline stays, and whether a template beside it is refused as contradictory.

**Decision.**
- **`show`, a boolean, default `true`.** `tooltip: %{show: false, event: :pick}` reads as what it is: the tooltip node, not shown. `hover: false` was rejected because *hover* already names a view state the palette draws (spec/10 §4.4) and the sync group's shared pointer (§4.3), and a reader could take it to turn those off chart-wide rather than this mark's text.
- **The key stays, the hover goes.** Under `show: false` the mark writes everything it writes now for a click — `data-frame`, `data-mark`, `data-row`, `data-tooltip-event`, `data-tooltip-context-event`, and its entry in the hit index (D-197) — so clicks, right-clicks, long-presses, the Menu key and the keyboard (D-198) are untouched. The group adds `data-tooltip-show="false"`, which the hook reads as the template is read, nearest wins.
- **No outline either.** The outline is drawn around *the element the tooltip shows* (spec/10 §12.3, #638); with nothing shown there is nothing it outlines, and an outline that tracks the pointer is itself a thing following the pointer. A sync group's hover skips such elements for the same reason. The keyboard's focus marker is the keyboard's (§17.3) and is not the hover's, so a focused item is still shown.
- **Only the named fields are written.** With nothing shown the fields have no reader on the page, and `fields: []`'s "every column" would put a grid's whole rows into the DOM for nothing; so under `show: false` the fields `fields` names are written and `[]` writes none, the element keeping `data-datum=""`, which is how a click finds its element (`closest('[data-datum]')`). A host that reads fields from the DOM itself names them.
- **A template is allowed.** The proposal suggested refusing `show: false` with a `template`. It is not contradictory: the template is also the item's text in the keyboard readout (spec/14 §21.5, D-198), and that is how a grid that shows nothing under the pointer — Minesweeper's — still tells a screen reader each cell's state. Refusing it would force a choice between the readout and a clean pointer.
- **Byte for byte without it.** `show: true`, or no `show`, writes no new attribute.

**Consequences.**
- The schema table of spec/14 §5.7 gains the key; JSON carries it as a boolean, and it round-trips.
- `TooltipHook` hides over an element under `data-tooltip-show="false"`, and its sync lookup skips one; on a canvas, which draws no datum element, the hook already showed nothing, and still does.
- **Rejected:** `hover: false`; refusing a template beside `show: false`; keeping the outline for hidden marks.

## 201. D-201 — A chart's name is the root's `aria-label`, never a `<title>` the browser shows under the pointer

**Context.** #803, found on the Minesweeper page (user, 2026-10-20): "Minesweeper" followed the pointer over the grid. Since D-56 a root names itself with a `<title id>` child that `aria-labelledby` points at, and since D-142 every root `Visualize.Chart.render/2` writes is named — the design's `meta.name`, else its kind. Every browser shows an SVG `<title>` as a native tooltip after the pointer rests on the element, and nothing can turn that off but leaving the `<title>` out, so **every** chart the library draws as SVG showed its name under the cursor, and a host could not keep the accessible name without the tooltip. The proposal weighed (a) `aria-label` in place of the `<title>` for every chart, (b) keeping `<title>` by default with a render option to switch to (a), and (c) anything a screen-reader check found better.

**What screen readers do.** The accessible-name computation (W3C Accessible Name and Description 1.2, with the SVG accessibility mapping) takes, in order, `aria-labelledby`, then `aria-label`, then the host language's own label — for SVG, the first `<title>` child. So `aria-label` on a `role="img"` `<svg>` is a step *before* the `<title>` in every engine that implements the computation, which Chrome, Firefox and Safari all do; JAWS, NVDA, Narrator, VoiceOver and TalkBack read the name the browser computes rather than the markup. The field reports agree: the `<title>`-alone pattern has a history of being ignored (VoiceOver with Safari and Firefox in particular), which is why D-56 added `aria-labelledby`; `aria-labelledby` itself has had VoiceOver failures in Firefox and Safari where `aria-label` read in every combination tested; and the W3C SVG accessibility task force's own advice is `aria-label` for "an accessible name without generating a tooltip in visual browsers". Automated checks (axe's `svg-img-alt`) accept either. The library has relied on `aria-label` for a chart's name since D-142: a canvas, and a hybrid chart's wrapper, are `role="img"` with `aria-label` (spec/14 §21.4). The one caution in the reports is that some automatic page translators skip attribute text; a host serving several languages already passes its own `title:`, translated.

**Decision.** **(a), for every chart.**
- `Visualize.IR.Element.root/3`'s `:title` is the root's `aria-label` (spec/02 §2.1), and no `<title>` child is written. The `<desc id>` stays, as does `aria-describedby` naming it: a `<desc>` shows nothing on hover. A root with a description and no title is still named by `aria-labelledby` pointing at the `<desc>`, its only text. The ids are derived as before, so the root's `id` and the selector of its scoped rules (D-188) do not move.
- Every component of spec/10 §1.2 names its `<svg>` the same way.
- So every backend names a chart one way: the SVG root, the canvas, a hybrid chart's wrapper and `Summary.attrs/2` all by `aria-label`, each described by an element `aria-describedby` names.
- **(b) is rejected** because the tooltip is the defect, not a preference: a chart's name under every reader's pointer helps nobody who can see the chart, and under (b) every host would have to learn an option to stop it. A host that wants a hover name on a hand-built root still prepends `Visualize.IR.Element.title/1` itself; `title/1` and the IR's `:title` type are unchanged, and a legend's cut entry keeps its `<title>` (D-169), which is a deliberate hover showing the text the cut hid.
- **(c)**: no pattern reads better. `aria-labelledby` pointing at a visible label outside the SVG needs a label the host may not have; a `<title>` hidden by CSS still tooltips.

**Consequences.**
- **Breaking** for a host that reads the `<title>` out of a chart's markup, or styles or tests it: the name is the root's `aria-label` now. Every rooted chart's first lines change, so the charts golden changes on every line in the root and its first child, and the component fixtures with a title change in the same two places; the canvas and hybrid outputs, which already used `aria-label`, are unchanged.
- No chart drawn by the library shows a native tooltip; with `tooltip.show: false` (D-200) a chart can take clicks with nothing following the pointer at all, as Minesweeper does.

## 202. D-202 — A 2,500-item interactive grid redraws one move in 100 ms and 200 KB, on every backend

**Context.** #808 measured a 50 × 50 Minesweeper board, 2,500 cells on two band scales, drawn by `Visualize.Components.Chart` (spec/10 §19). A move took 0.46 s on SVG, 0.75 s on the JSON canvas and 0.55 s on the binary canvas on the server, and its patch carried 1.6 to 3.8 MB of changed dynamics; the Expert board (16 × 30, 480 cells) took 60 to 98 ms. The page's own work, the game step and one `Chart.apply/2`, was under 25 ms: the cost was the library's. #809 found where it went: `Visualize.Scale.Band.apply/2` searched its domain and recomputed its layout on every call, so a band × band chart cost `O(n²)`; `Summary.points/1` placed every item a second time for the keyboard; and the component sent the whole drawing and the whole `data-keyboard-points` attribute on every update, though a move changes three cells. The browser side was measured under the hook harness (spec/12 §6) and is not the cost: `KeyboardHook` takes 4–7 ms over 2,500 points, and a click's hit-index lookup 0.03–0.1 ms.

**Decision.** A budget for a large interactive grid, recorded in spec/12 §6 and held by a cost test:
- **2,500 keyed items on two band scales, one move:** `Component.update/2` with the next `%Applied{}`, plus the component's render and diff, in **≤ 100 ms on the server**, and **≤ 200 KB of changed dynamics on the wire**, on SVG, the JSON canvas and the binary canvas.
- **The Expert board, 480 items: ≤ 25 ms.**
- **The bytes are uncompressed.** They are the component's diff, JSON-encoded, as the socket carries it. The examples endpoint, like Phoenix's default, does not compress the socket, and a host that does is not one the budget may assume. Socket compression is documented as a host's option and counts for nothing here.
- **The milliseconds are a measurement; the test counts work.** By D-114 no default-suite test reads a clock, so the cost test holds the update and render to a `Visualize.Work.reductions/1` budget per backend, set from the measured counts, and the bytes of one move's diff to 200 KB.
- The milestone meets it in order: the band scale in constant time per lookup (#810), one placement pass per update (#811), the avoidable per-item cost of generating and serializing (#812), the transport (#813, its choices D-203 on), then the guard (#814).

**Consequences.**
- 100 ms is the rate at which a click still feels immediate, and 200 KB a move is what a slow connection carries in well under a second; a 2,500-item chart that misses either is not interactive, whatever its first render costs.
- A change that makes a lookup walk a domain, places items twice, or re-sends what a move did not change fails the cost test by name, not as a slow page someone notices.
- The budget is the component's: a page that recomputes its whole design per move pays for that itself, as the Minesweeper page's `Chart.apply/2` (17–24 ms at 2,500 rows) does outside it.

## 203. D-203 — A drawing travels as keyed segments, so a move sends the items it changed, on SVG and on both canvases

**Context.** #809 (D-202): `Visualize.Components.Chart` sent the whole drawing as one dynamic on every update — the SVG, or the canvas stream in `data-binary` or `data-commands` — so a move on a 50 × 50 grid, which changes a few cells, sent the 2,500 others again. The candidates were measured on #808's move (2,500 cells, the LiveView diff of the component JSON-encoded, as the socket carries it): the whole drawing as before, **1,274,212 B** on SVG, **2,833,702 B** on the JSON canvas and **1,379,161 B** on the binary canvas; the SVG keyed by item with the keyboard's attribute whole (candidate A), **583,787 B**; A with the keyboard's positions sent once and its readouts whole (C), **563,209 B**, since the positions were 25,501 B and the readouts 323,584 B; A with the keyboard's points keyed (D-204), **14,853 B** with items keyed by their place, and **1,417 B** (SVG), **2,063 B** (JSON) and **1,255 B** (binary) once an item is keyed by what it draws. The spec/09 incremental path (§5, §7) is the third candidate for a canvas: it is a scroll's protocol, and a delta painted over the last frame is wrong for any item that is not opaque — a grid's translucent cell painted again over itself darkens — while a delta that clears first uncovers what lies beneath. It would need a stateful hook that a reconnect or a missed patch desynchronizes, and its bytes would be those of the changed items, which the keyed segments already are.

**Decision.** A drawing is sent as **keyed segments** (spec/09 §1.4, spec/10 §19.3): `Visualize.Render.Segments` cuts a backend's output into one segment per element drawn alone and one for each start and end of a group, keyed by the element's identity (D-167), else by its group's path and a hash of what it draws; a group by its path. The component renders them as a keyed LiveView comprehension: on SVG each segment raw, so the markup is the same byte for byte; on a canvas each the `data-c` of an `<i>` in a hidden `<div data-vis-stream>` beside the hook's element, which puts the stream together — the JSON commands joined, the binary pieces decoded and concatenated — when its `data-drawn` names a new drawing. LiveView sends a keyed comprehension's changed entries only. A hash only names a key; a text is reused only for an element equal to the last one under that key.

**Consequences.**
- A move on the 2,500-cell grid sends 1.2–2.5 KB where it sent 1.3–2.8 MB, under D-202's 200 KB uncompressed, on every backend; an item keyed by what it draws keeps its key when an item before it comes or goes, so a new count label does not resend the labels after it.
- **Breaking** for a host that reads the component's canvas markup: the stream is no longer in `data-binary` or `data-commands` of the hook's element, which carries `data-stream` and `data-drawn`, and its pieces are children of the `data-vis-stream` element. `CanvasChart` and `CanvasBinaryChart` read both forms, so a hand-written page's `data-binary` or `data-commands` draws as before. The SVG's markup is unchanged.
- The canvas hooks rebuild the stream from its pieces on each new drawing: for 2,500 cells that is 2,560 attribute reads and as many base64 decodes, a few milliseconds the hook harness can execute but no browser on the host could time.

## 204. D-204 — The keyboard's points travel one element each, not positions and readouts split

**Context.** #809: `data-keyboard-points`, 344 KB of JSON for a 2,500-cell grid (750 KB HTML-escaped), was re-sent on every move. #809 proposed splitting it into the points' positions, sent once per drawing, and their readouts, sent when they change. Measured (D-203's move): the positions are 25,501 B and the readouts 323,584 B, and a move changes the readouts of the cells it changes only; split, a move still sent 563,209 B.

**Decision.** `Visualize.Hooks.Keyboard.listed/2` writes the points one element each: the series' names, `grid` and counts in `data-keyboard-series`, each point as `data-keyboard-points` writes it in the `data-p` of one `<i>` in a hidden `<div data-keyboard-list>` inside the hook's element, keyed by its series' place and its own. `KeyboardHook` reads either form. `Visualize.Components.Chart` sends the list as a keyed comprehension, so a move sends the points it changed; `attrs/1,2` stays for a page that writes the attribute itself.

**Consequences.**
- A move sends a few points: the SVG move of D-203 fell from 583,787 B to 14,853 B with this alone.
- **Breaking** for a host that reads `data-keyboard-points` off the component's markup: it carries `data-keyboard-series` and the list instead. A hand-written page's attribute reads as before.
- A point's readout counts its series (`3 of 2,500`), so a move that changes how many points a series has rewrites every readout of it; a grid's count is fixed.

## 205. D-205 — Every part of a drawing is its own assign and its own cache, so what a move did not change is neither sent nor written again

**Context.** #809: the component held the canvas's stream, text layer, hit index and summary in one `@canvas` assign, and LiveView re-sends every dynamic that reads an assign that changed: a move re-sent the 61,665 B hit index though no item had moved. And every update wrote every item again — serialized each element, encoded each point, measured each hit box — though a move changes a few.

**Decision.** The hit index, the summary, the canvas's size, the text layer, the segments and the points are separate assigns; LiveView marks an assign changed only when its value differs, so an unchanged part is not sent. The segments and the points keep what they wrote — the segments' cache (spec/09 §1.4) and the points' (`Keyboard.listed/2`'s `cache:`) — each reusing an item's text only when what it was written from is equal to the last drawing's, compared whole. The hit index is computed whole each drawing: a cache of it saved its 0.6 M reductions and cost as much again in copying the cache in and out of the drawing's process (D-206), and was left out.

**Consequences.**
- At 2,500 cells serializing the SVG falls from 1.4 M reductions to 0.05 M on a move, the canvas streams from 1.3–1.7 M to 0.05–0.11 M, and the points' JSON from 0.48 M to almost nothing.
- The caches hold the last drawing's elements and points: their memory is one drawing's, which the component already held once in the drawing it compared against.
- A cache is an economy only: every part is the same with an empty one, and the tests hold each to its uncached output.

## 206. D-206 — The component makes its drawing in a process of its own, sized by the last drawing

**Context.** #809: with D-203–D-205 a 2,500-cell move did 3.6–4.2 M reductions, about 70–90 ms of work, yet took 258–300 ms in a process holding what a LiveView holds — the component's state and the diff's prints. The rest was garbage collection: an update allocates tens of megabytes it drops at once, and each collection in the LiveView's process copies the live data around it, the state among it. The same update in a process whose heap is sized not to collect took 83–94 ms. The library cannot size the host's LiveView process, which is the host's to configure.

**Decision.** `Visualize.Components.Chart` makes each drawing — the placements, the keyboard's points, the segments, the hit index — in a process spawned for it and linked to the LiveView, with a minimum heap of what the last drawing used and a quarter more, and a binary heap no drawing reaches, so it collects nothing once sized; the first drawing is sized by the BEAM. It returns the parts and their caches, which are copied into the LiveView's process, and its heap is freed whole when it ends. A fault in it exits the LiveView, as it did in place.

**Consequences.**
- With the per-item work #813 also cut — each channel's placement decided once per mark, a label's style made once per mark when it reads no datum, a mark's identity read through one function of the row, the points' JSON cached — a 2,500-cell move takes 77–97 ms where it took 258–300 ms with the drawing in place, and the Expert board 16–22 ms (spec/12 §6, D-202), the median of five moves once the drawing's heap has settled, on a host shared with other work (load 3–20). What remains is the drawing's own work (55–68 ms), copying its inputs and its results, and LiveView's diff.
- The drawing's memory is transient and whole: a 2,500-cell drawing reserves its heap for the length of the update and returns it at once, instead of the LiveView's heap growing and shrinking around it.
- `Visualize.Work.reductions/1` counts what a process executes, so the cost test counts the drawing's function itself (`Visualize.Components.Chart.drawn/4`), not the component's update around it.

## 207. D-207 — A dependency advisory fails the build, read from Hex's own audit, with a reasoned allowlist

**Context.** #782: `examples/mix.lock` pinned bandit 1.12.5, which carries two advisories: EEF-CVE-2026-105659 (HIGH, HTTP/2 stream processes kept alive by a trickle of frames) and EEF-CVE-2026-105660 (MEDIUM, an unvalidated HTTP/1 request target sets `conn.host` and `conn.port`). Both are fixed in 1.13.0. `mix deps.get` printed them as a warning and exited 0, and no job read them, so the lock kept them until someone noticed the warning by hand. Hex publishes advisories with its registry, and `mix hex.audit` (Hex 2.5.3 here) reports them, with retired packages, and exits non-zero on either. A third-party auditor such as `mix_audit` would be a new dependency reading a separate database.

**Decision.** `mix vis.advisories` (spec/12 §3) runs `mix hex.audit` in the root project and in `examples/`, one OS process each, and fails when either exits non-zero. Accepting a finding is an entry in `audit/advisories_allowlist.exs`: its project, one advisory id or one retirement, and its reason. The task hands each project only its own entries, through Hex's `HEX_IGNORE_ADVISORIES` and `HEX_IGNORE_RETIREMENTS`, which it always sets. Each entry is proved live by running the audit without it, which must then fail. The CI job `verify:advisories` and `mix vis.check` run the task. The gallery's lock moves bandit to 1.13.1, with the hpax 1.1 and thousand_island 1.6 it requires, and nothing else.

**Consequences.**
- No new dependency: the advisory data is Hex's own, signed with its registry, and read by the Hex client every job already installs.
- A newly published advisory against either lock turns the next pipeline red, on any branch, even when the branch did not touch the lock. That is the point: the finding is acted on, by a lock update or a reasoned entry, not scrolled past.
- A retired package fails the gate too, since `mix hex.audit` reports both. A retirement is allowed the same way, by `retired:`.
- The allowlist cannot go stale: an entry the audit passes without fails the task. Proving each entry costs one more audit run, about a second, and the list is meant to stay short.
- The job fetches the examples app's dependencies, without compiling them, since `mix hex.audit` checks that a project's dependencies are on disk before it reads the lock.

## 208. D-208 — The binary canvas clips and blends, in a version 4 stream, and a guard holds it to every style key

**Context.** #788: the binary canvas is the gallery's canvas backend and every compiled chart's. It had no record for an element's `clip` and none for a style's `:blend`. Both were stated deviations in spec/09 §4.4. So an `:inside` or `:outside` stroke, which `Visualize.Chart.Mark.Strokes` draws as a doubled width clipped to the shape or away from it, drew at twice its width, centered on the edge, and a multiplied element did not multiply. SVG and the `:json` canvas drew both. The Paints chart's swatches (#786) show the difference side by side. Nothing would have stopped the next style key shipping SVG-only the same way: the paint-key guard of #675 covers only the paint keys.

**Decision.** Version 4 of the stream adds two records, under the rule of D-175 and D-180: a stream that uses neither is byte for byte what it was. `clip` (`0x56`, `keep:u8`) makes the current path the clip. The encoder writes, in the `:json` commands' order: `save`, the shape's geometry record, `clip`, the element's own records, `restore`. Outside adds the ±100000 rectangle and clips even-odd, as the `:json` canvas does. `blend` (`0x57`, `mode:u8`) is the composite operation, written after the style record as `effect` is. Its byte names one of the fifteen CSS `mix-blend-mode` words besides `normal`. Both are context state that `save` and `restore` scope. The shipped decoder, shared by `CanvasBinaryChart` and `CanvasIncrementalChart`, reads both and accepts version 4, as does the reference decoder. A clipped element, a group among them, is one segment of the keyed stream (D-203). The two deviations are removed. spec/09 §4.8 gives every style key of the schema a row, its binary records or **not drawn** and why. A conformance test fails on a key with no row. It also fails on a drawn key whose binary draws are not the `:json` canvas's on the recording stub, and on a not-drawn key the binary stream does carry.

**Consequences.**
- On the binary canvas an inside or outside stroke draws at its width on its side of the edge, and a blend composites as on SVG. The Paints chart looks the same on every backend.
- **Breaking** for a page that runs an older hook against a stream that clips or blends: the old decoder throws at the version record (`0x00 0x04`). It does not draw the stroke doubled. Every other stream is unchanged.
- SVG blends an element as a whole, and the canvas blends its fill and then its stroke. Where the two overlap they differ, as a paint's opacity already does (D-180).
- What the binary stream still does not draw is listed in one table, text and class. A style key added to the schema fails the guard until either the binary canvas draws it or the table says why not.
- The SVG-element encoder (`CanvasBinary.SVG`) reads no `clip-path`, `mask` or `mix-blend-mode`. It resolves no references, and no IR render goes through it. The audit on #788 lists it, and #825 proposes it.
## 209. D-209 — A `:tiles` mark is clipped to its frame's plot area

**Context.** #826, found building #819. A `:tiles` mark covers the plot area with whole slippy-map tiles (spec/07 §8.3), so the tiles at its edges reach up to one tile past it on every side, and nothing clipped them. A chart of one frame hid this: the frame filled the `<svg>`, and the document's edge clipped the tiles. In a design of several frames (spec/14 §2.8, §4.2), one frame's basemap painted over its neighbors. *Grandma's Marathon* showed it at 960 × 620. The course frame's tiles ran 148 px into the right-hand column. The pack inset's reached 314 px below its plot, over the clock and the standings. Both covered the course frame's legend. The same held on a hybrid page, because each plot's backdrop document is the design's full size with the frame translated inside it.

**Decision.** The `:tiles` mark's group carries a clip to the plot's rectangle, `[0, 0, width, height]` in the plot area's coordinates (`Visualize.IR.Element.clip/3`, `:inside`). The clip is on the group rather than on the images alone. A hybrid page splits the group, the images going to the backdrop document and the attribution to the SVG layer (spec/14 §12.4, D-120), and each half keeps the clip. The attribution lies inside the plot already, so the clip changes nothing for it. Only the tiles are clipped. A frame-wide clip of every mark (a frame `clip: true`) is a separate proposal. It is #828, which the binary canvas clip record of D-208 makes possible.

**Consequences.**
- No tile paints outside its own frame's plot, on SVG or in a hybrid page's backdrop. Frames in a box can each carry a basemap.
- A single-frame basemap chart draws exactly what it drew, since the document's edge was already clipping it. Its markup gains a `<clipPath>` definition and a `clip-path` on the tiles group, and the goldens that hold one change accordingly.
- The binary canvas stream has no clip record, but it carries no tiles either: an image has no record there (spec/09 §4.4). So nothing on the binary canvas changes.
